It’s not difficult to develop an incorrect theory. Ice cream and spaghetti have more letters than tofu and celery, so therefore, foods with long names are more fattening than those with short ones. 
If you start moving forward with this theory, you’ll soon find outliers. Sugar only has five letters, and watercress has ten. If we want to stick with our theory, we’ll have to come up with more and more complex reasons why we ignore some of the data and embrace the rest.
Of course, this is absurd.
And yet we do it all the time. Ornate explanations about the stock market, SEO or politics. Charts and graphs where we spend most of our time fitting a plot to a few dots, or amplifying the obscure to justify the theory.
A simple theory that matches the data is much more likely to accurately explain causation. It might not be a comfortable theory, or one that matches our trajectory, but it’s a better theory.
It turns out that the number of letters is completely irrelevant to how fattening a food is. I’m sorry you spent all this time on research, but you’re on the wrong path.
If you want to know why your marketing, your investments or your arguments are floundering, it might pay to reconsider the theory, not look for more data to justify the current one.

1,2026-11-15,"Author of the AI Insider newsletter
There's a date looming at Meta that some employees are calling ""The Rapture."" And it could trigger an exodus of wealthy tech workers.
February 15, 2027, marks the final payout from a particularly lucrative batch of company shares awarded to employees in 2023, when Meta's stock price was in the doldrums.
Meta's stock has soared more than 400% since the beginning of 2023. That stock surge has turned those awards into a gold mine.
Andre Nader, a former Meta employee who writes the FAANG FIRE newsletter, estimates senior engineers could see annual compensation from stock awards plunge more than 40%, after these payouts end.
That's a serious pay cut. But there's a delicious twist. 
Some Meta employees have made so much money from this extraordinary stock rally that they may no longer need to work. Over the past four years, several of these equity grants have jumped in value as Meta stock rebounded. Some employees could be sitting on gains of $2 million or more.
Once you've made money like that, golden handcuffs sometimes don't work as well. For some lucky Meta veterans, The Rapture could mean early retirement rather than another job.

3,2026-11-15,"A pocket watch and a parcel of farmland.
Veteran trader Steve Hanke counts those two items among the best investments and biggest misses of his life.
Hanke, 83, started trading soybeans at age 14, and he's been playing the currency and commodity markets ever since.
The longtime professor of applied economics at Johns Hopkins University served as president of Toronto Trust Argentina when it was the world's best-performing emerging-market mutual fund in 1995.
Hanke spoke to Business Insider for the debut story in our new ""Hits, Misses, and Millions"" series, in which we ask elite investors and business leaders to share their best and worst investments.
Even top investors make painful mistakes.
This story is part of our Hits, Misses, and Millions series, where we ask leading figures in finance and business to share the best and worst investments they've ever made, and what they learned from them.
What's your top investment? Do you have one major regret? Let us know by email at tmohamed@businessinsider.com.
At age 16, Hanke purchased a Patek Philippe pocket watch for $90 at an auction in Omaha.
""I bought that timepiece because it was beautiful and the price was little more than the value of the gold content of the case,"" Hanke told Business Insider in a recent email.
""As it turns out, I had unknowingly purchased a rare collectible, custom-made for the original owner in 1917,"" he continued. ""I've continued to enjoy the ever-appreciating Patek for 67 years.""
Hanke's watch could fetch over $100,000 at auction today, or more than 1,100 times what he paid for it, based on online listings for similar timepieces.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
He told Business Insider that when he bought it, ""no one wanted pocket watches."" They typically sold for around $40 or roughly the value of the gold in their cases, so he ""did pay a bit of a premium for my beauty,"" he said.
Hanke now only wears the watch on special occasions, but ""the rest of the time I admire it,"" he said. The ""pleasure"" he gets from seeing it every day is a form of non-financial payout, he added.
""So, the Patek has generated both a capital gain and significant dividends,"" he said.
Hanke also shared other notable investments from his career. In 1985, he modeled the oil market and anticipated a breakdown in OPEC's production discipline that would send crude prices crashing to under $10 a barrel.
""I was leveraged and short in more ways than you can shake a stick at,"" he said, adding that when his prediction came true, that was a ""pretty big payday.""
Hanke also recalled that at TTA, he and his team ""loaded up"" on Argentine bonds while others were selling them during the so-called ""Tequila Crisis"" in 1994, when the Mexican government devalued the peso, leading to major turmoil in emerging markets. This resulted in the firm outperforming all of its emerging-market peers in 1995, Hanke said.
Hanke said he's endured many losses as a trader, but prudently using stop-loss orders — which instruct a broker to sell a security or cover a short when the price falls to a set level — has ensured that ""none have been catastrophic or even notable.""
""In any case, I am a Stoic and don't cry over spilled milk,"" he added.
Hanke did share one investment that he ""thought about making, but failed to pull the trigger on,"" and it now ranks among his greatest investing regrets.
""As a farm boy from the state of Iowa, I always wanted to follow in one of my grandfathers' footsteps and invest in Iowa farmland,"" he said.
When the average price of Iowa farmland fell below $2,100 per acre in 2002, he was tempted to buy but didn't. The average price has risen more than fivefold since then to over $11,500 an acre, he said.
Hanke said the annual inflation-adjusted rate of return is only 5.2%, but his ""mistake of omission,"" in Warren Buffett's parlance, is ""perhaps the only investment regret that I can report.""
Hanke's pocket watch showcases the value of a storied brand, a good deal, a decadeslong holding period, and a beautiful object that can be worn and admired.
His lack of large investing mishaps speaks to the power of capping losses, while his regret over not buying Iowa farmland shows the importance of acting when opportunity arises.

4,2026-11-15,"This week's ""Saturday Night Live"" cold open was a who's-who of the AI revolution.
Comedian John Mulaney kicked off the episode by playing a CIA officer conducting national security training for tech billionaires. Specifically, how to identify a spy.
Mulaney's character is joined by Palantir cofounder Peter Thiel (James Austin Johnson), OpenAI CEO Sam Altman (Ben Marshall), Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei (Jane Wickline), Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg (Andrew Dismukes), and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk (Jeremy Culhane).
""America needs you to learn the difference between a woman who actually wants to have sex with you, and a woman who is clearly a spy,"" Mulaney said. ""You all saw how former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned this week after he was caught having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.""
Last Sunday, Nvidia's board of directors said it would investigate whether Huang was the ""target of a foreign intelligence effort"" on the same day he admitted to an extramarital affair in a memo to staff. Huang resigned from his position days later.
On ""Saturday Night Live,"" Mulaney's character empathized with the tech billionaires.
""Look, I can't imagine how disorienting this has been for you. To go from these bug-eyed little weirdos to being gods,"" Mulaney said. ""Never in the history of our species have people as pale and as strange as you, A: survived, and B: been given all the world's wealth and power.""
Mulaney asked the tech executives why someone ""very beautiful"" would be interested in them.
""Because I have a good personality and normal, human mannerisms?"" Marshall's Altman said.

Zuckerberg, played by Dismukes, guessed it's because ""she's comforted by my Meta glasses recording her at all times.""
Wickline's Amodei theorized it's because ""she's using me... for my body.""
Mulaney then asked the group to pretend an attractive woman approached them at the bar and asked, ""What are you working on?"" Amodei and Zuckerberg weighed in.
""I would say, Madam, what a pleasure to see you this evening. May I interest you in an industry secret?"" Amodei said. Zuckerberg said he would ""assume that she wanted to sleep with the man who created the Metaverse, as all women do.""
Near the end, former 'SNL' cast member Bowen Yang returned as Huang. ""It's me. Jensen 'hide your wife' Huang,"" Yang says as he joins the other tech billionaires. ""Ladies, when you invite Jensen over, you know what's about to go down. The S&P 500.""
He added, ""That's right. I'm into BDSM: Breakin' da stock market."" Nvidia lost more than $500 billion in market value over the last week.
Yang's Huang cautioned his fellow tech executives against being like him. ""Remember, women don't like guys like us. That's how we became guys like us,"" he said.
In real life, some Nvidia loyalists are hoping the board will reinstate Huang. Nvidia's board of directors said it's considering internal and external candidates for the permanent CEO position.

5,2026-11-15,"
            More than a month after President Donald Trump announced a surprise deal to import Russian diesel, the oil has struggled to reach the US market amid outrage and legal concerns.
    

            Several oil tankers that departed from a key Russian oil-loading port — and are believed to be laden with diesel — are no longer bound for the US, according to ship tracking data from MarineTraffic, and US businesses and ports have steered clear of accepting Russian diesel for fear of legal and reputational risks, experts told CNN.
    

            Bipartisan lawmakers and European allies expressed outrage at the agreement that Trump said he reached with Russian President Vladimir Putin to bring tens of thousands of barrels of Russian diesel into US and global markets. They condemned it as a concession to Russia as it continued its war against Ukraine and warned that those who imported the diesel could run afoul of international sanctions and US law.
    

            One of the tankers, Agioi Fanendes, departed Russia with tens of thousands of barrels of Russian diesel. Its destination was listed as the Port of New York and New Jersey, and it was initially expected to arrive around November 7.
    

            As of Sunday, the vessel was hovering hundreds of miles off the eastern coast of the US. It was officially broadcasting “FOR ORDERS” instead of a destination port. A spokesperson for Eastern Mediterranean Maritime Limited, the company that operates the tanker, told CNN on Thursday that its “discharging port is not confirmed yet.”
    

            Several other oil tankers that departed Primorsk — one of Russia’s largest oil-loading ports — in mid-October have also shifted away from the US, according to data from MarineTraffic reviewed by CNN. One, the Talisman, docked at the Port of Alexandria in Egypt in recent days. Another, the Leruo, is now heading to Brazil. They had earlier declared their destinations as the US Atlantic coast. Both vessels are under European and British sanctions, and the Talisman is also sanctioned in the US.
    

            A White House official told CNN on Wednesday that “consistent with the deal agreed to between President Trump and President Putin, Russian diesel is on the water and heading to the market, which will ease prices for Americans.”
    

            The official did not say the diesel was heading to the US. The person said the deal “was made to add diesel supply to the global market — lowering prices for Americans and the world — which is exactly what will be done.”
    

            Trump announced in a social media post on October 9 that he had reached a deal with Putin for Russia to “immediately supply over 300,000 Tons of Diesel Fuel to the American and Global Marketplace.”
    

            The administration billed the deal, which was announced weeks before the US midterm elections amid steep fuel costs, as a way to lower prices for US consumers. Experts said the diesel would have little to no impact on prices, and amounts to less than a day of US energy consumption.
    

            Last month, a group of bipartisan lawmakers sent letters to the CEOs of JPMorgan Chase, Bank of America, Citigroup, Wells Fargo, Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley, and the heads of Port Authorities of New York/New Jersey and of Houston, Corpus Christi and Beaumont, Texas, warning that they could face legal action if they are involved in importing the Russian diesel into the US.
    

            The Ending Importation of Russian Oil Act of 2022 bans Russian-origin energy products, including diesel fuel, from being imported into the US “unless — following a period for consultation with Congress — the President certifies to Congress that Russia has ceased military hostilities in Ukraine, that Russia poses no immediate military threat to NATO members, and that Russia recognizes the right of Ukrainians to choose their government,” the lawmakers warned.
    

            Although the US Treasury Department issued a sanctions waiver to allow the import of the diesel under the deal between the Trump administration and Moscow, the lawmakers consider that sanctions waiver to defy the law.
    

            “Any importation of Russian diesel into the U.S. raises significant legal questions and may be subject to legal action, as well as congressional investigations,” wrote Democratic Sens. Jeanne Shaheen, Ron Wyden, Sheldon Whitehouse, Chris Coons, Elizabeth Warren, GOP Sen. Thom Tillis, and Democratic Rep. Gregory Meeks.
    

            “Criminal penalties may apply to any company that knowingly buys Russian diesel or facilitates its importation, and not simply the importer of record,” they wrote.
    

            New Jersey Gov. Mikie Sherrill warned her state’s port and terminal operators last week that “any effort to profit from the illegal trade in Russian fuel will be met with harsh penalties.”
    

            Charles Lichfield, the director of economic foresight and analysis and the C. Boyden Gray senior fellow at the Atlantic Council’s GeoEconomics Center, noted that US importers would face “the immediate reputational risk” as well as the legal ramifications.
    

            He noted that a general license from the Treasury Department, which allows businesses to import the diesel without fear of sanctions, would appear to be good protection but the 2022 law is still on the books, which raises questions about legality and the potential for prosecution down the line.
    

            “All of this is very politically charged (and) the volumes are small,” he said. “You could make a few million dollars off this shipment, but is it really worth it?”
    

            Julian Hayda, the associate director for advocacy at the organization Razom for Ukraine, noted that “now that Democrats have control of both chambers of Congress, they are most certainly going to launch investigations into these companies, so it’s not going to be worth it for many of these companies to buy this vehicle in and when it arrives on American shores.”
    

This story has been updated with additional information.

W've been hearing for years that crypto, because of its fixed supply, was going to be an excellent hedge against inflation.And those watching the charts have been noticing 75%'ish corrections (losses) after the big fast upward spikes. Then a long plateau at around 25% before the next enthusiasm fueled run up. Its mostly a ""greater fool"" based high risk investment scheme, faith that someone will eventually show up to buy it at a higher price than you. 

Commercial use is an illusion. Sellers typically do pricing and sales strictly in fiat currency on their end. If someone shows up wanting to pay in crypto they are passed off to a crypto payment processor who handles verifying the crypto transfer. The crypto payment processor then credits the sellers account the exact amount of fiat currency the goods/service was priced at. Sellers never see or touch crypto, never experience any holding risk. 

Amusingly, the person buying with crypto has created a complication for their taxes. Since crypto is considered an asset not a currency in many jurisdictions, they typically have to report a capital gains/loss on that crypto they used to buy that pizza. Needing to report the value of those coins at the time they acquired them and the value at the time of their transfer. 

Crypto does have some utility as an alternative to wire transfers. Typically the sender buys the crypto to transfer, immediate transfers, the receiving party immediately sells for their local currency. In the minutes this takes there is minimal holding risk.

7,2026-11-15,"The housing market hasn't been inspiring for prospective homebuyers lately.
With many potential buyers waiting on the sidelines as interest rates rise, home sales are taking a hit. 
In 2024 and 2025, existing home sales were identical, at 4.06 million. However, that number fell to a seasonally adjusted annual rate of 3.75 million in October 2026, according to the National Association of Realtors. It's the lowest number in over a decade.
""Home buying is difficult when mortgage rates have climbed to their highest levels since 2000, aside from a single month in 2023,"" NAR Chief Economist Lawrence Yun said. ""As a result, home sales fell to their lowest level since August 2010.""
So who's still buying homes?
NAR released its annual Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers. This year's report covers home sales between July 2025 and June 2026, offering a snapshot of the economic climate and housing market over the past year.
Since the previous report, the median homebuyer age has dropped slightly, while their income has grown considerably. Buyers also resoundingly favor the suburbs.
Here's what the typical homebuyer looked like in the past year, according to NAR.
The steady increase in the median age of homebuyers has been a talking point for years.
When NAR published its first report in 1981, the median age of all homebuyers was 31 — now it's up to 58.
For first-time homebuyers, that number stayed put at 40, while the age of repeat homebuyers also stayed flat at 62. While the age of first-time buyers is usually the report's main talking point, the median age of repeat buyers has increased at a faster rate since 1981.
Even though the median homebuyer age was 58, the most common homebuyer was in the range of 65 to 74 years old — that age group made up 26% of all homebuyers.
Homebuyers figured it was easier to go in on a house with another person than alone.
Married couples accounted for the largest share of homebuyers by a wide margin. Sixty-three percent of all homebuyers were married.
The amount of single women homebuyers slightly decreased to 20% — after doubling since 1985 — but they are still more common than than single men, who accounted for 8% of all homebuyers.
The gap between single women and single men homebuyers was only a 2% difference in 1985 — 11% and 9%, respectively.
According to the US Census, the median household income reached its peak in 2025 at $87,460. Homebuyers this year make well above that, with a median household income of $120,000. For first-time buyers specifically, that number is $100,000.
The typical buyer bought a house with three bedrooms, two bathrooms, and 1,900 square feet of space; enough room for children or extra guests. Most buyers, however, were without kids.
Homebuyers with no children under 18 living in the home accounted for 74% of buyers.
Buyers with one or two children made up 10% of buyers, and those with three or more children made up 6%.
Today's homebuyers are highly educated. NAR's survey found that 30% of buyers have a bachelor's degree, followed by 27% with a master's degree, MBA, or law degree. Just 16% are high-school graduates.
College graduates tend to have higher incomes than people without degrees, which may help explain their higher homeownership rates.
The suburbs are the ""It"" place for the typical buyer.
This year, 48% of all buyers purchased homes in suburban neighborhoods, compared with 21% in small towns and 14% in rural areas.
It's a reversal from recent years. In 2022, the share of buyers purchasing homes in the suburbs fell below 40%. Small towns and rural areas attracted an influx of buyers that year, narrowing the gap between suburban and small-town buyers.
As more Americans gravitate toward suburban areas, the shift suggests the pandemic-era rush for more land and space in smaller towns and rural communities is easing.
The typical homebuyer would rather not share walls with neighbors.
This year, 80% of homebuyers chose detached homes. By comparison, townhouses accounted for 7% of purchases, while duplexes and condos made up 4%.
With buyers continuing to favor the suburbs, it's not surprising that condos, often concentrated in urban areas, are getting less love.
The real estate market is increasingly a tale of the haves and have-nots. NAR's survey shows that first-time homebuyers are struggling to keep up.
In 2026, the typical buyer had already purchased one or more properties. Repeat buyers, whether purchasing a new primary residence or a vacation home, accounted for 78% of all home purchases.
Repeat buyers are also much older than they were in the past. In 2026, their median age is 62, compared with 36 in 1981.
""Repeat buyers are driving the housing market,"" said Jessica Lautz, NAR's deputy chief economist and vice president of research. ""Unlike first-time home buyers, repeat buyers have seen their housing equity grow rapidly over the past decade, enabling them to trade up for larger homes or move into more desirable locations. These are often moves by choice rather than necessity.""

8,2026-11-15,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Dave Posey, who recently turned 55 and is a general manager and vice president at Sanborn. He's located in Parker, Colorado. The essay has been edited for length and clarity.
I spent three decades in manufacturing, distribution, and channel sales, and I quit my previous role in May. Nobody forced me out — I could see where the company was headed, and it wasn't where I wanted to go.
I left on principle, with no job and no prospect of one. I'm in my mid-50s, and this was the first time in my career that had ever been true.
A month later, in June, I'd accepted an offer as general manager and vice president of the spatial intelligence division at Sanborn.
In Still in the Game, Business Insider is chronicling the realities of job hunting in your 50s — from overcoming age bias and navigating career transitions to rebuilding confidence, updating skills, and finding new opportunities in a competitive job market. Read more stories here, and share your own experience here.
I hired a career coach two months before I quit, while I was still employed and not actively looking for work. My coach showed me what I should do to interest employers, and as a result, a recruiter came to me instead of the other way around.
This market is brutal — nobody cared about my 30 years in the industry. I couldn't lean on any of it.
What companies want to know is how candidates will fix their problem now, not what they fixed in 2014. You have to prove yourself from scratch, in the first hour of your interviews, every single time.
These are the strategies I learned from my career coach that I used to get three offers and land my current job in one month in my mid-50s:
Before coaching, I could tell you everything I'd ever done in my career, but I couldn't tell you in one line what I bring to the table.
My coach made me answer that in the employer's terms instead of mine. She moved me from talking about ""who I was"" to ""who I am and what I can do for your company.""
In practice, the biggest change was that before, I'd been posting almost entirely in one lane on LinkedIn, focusing on credibility proof: ""Here's what I built. Here's the number I hit. Here's my track record."" It's the safest lane and the one nobody responds to, because it's your résumé in a different costume.
My coach moved me into two other lanes: pattern recognition and decision-making insight. These lanes weren't about what I did, but how I think: ""Here's a problem you probably have. Here's my read on it. Here's the evidence. Here's what it means for you.""
I stopped showing people my 30 years of experience and started showing them how I'd think about their problem if they hired me. I worked with my coach three times a week on figuring out how to do this.
I think it's a similar pattern where everybody my age who's had a lengthy career gets comfortable — we haven't actively positioned ourselves. In the past, I'd never had to interview or fill out a résumé — the next opportunity always just opened up for me.
But when you get too comfortable, you lose your network and, all of a sudden, you just fade. You don't post like you're supposed to, you don't put yourself out there, you're not visible to everybody. That was the case with me.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Many people at this stage of life do the same thing: put up a banner on LinkedIn, talk to a few people, and go to some recruiters. You're not really seen when you do that, because you haven't worked on a larger network.
I think the big difference in what I did with my coach was that I put myself back in a place where I was being seen again.
I looked at everything I've done through all my jobs, identified what I'd successfully done over and over again, and found a pattern. I asked myself: What's the big dataset I have from the past that really describes who I am and what I do, and how is that going to help the employer I want to work for?
My pattern was that I could diagnose a sales system, identify where leaks were in it, and fix them to get into positive sales territory. So, I was able to take that pattern and repackage it into a sellable tool. I never again said, ""I did this."" Instead, I said, ""This is what I do.""
Specifically, instead of saying, ""I worked at my last company for three years and increased sales by 25%,"" I said: ""I'm able to diagnose CRM systems and stop margin leaks."" Then, for credibility, I pointed to my résumé so employers could see I'd done those things, and my ability to do them became my brand.
Once I figured out the pattern that created my unique brand, I repeated it to myself until I knew it inside and out.
I had my headphones in all the time, listening to myself repeating statements about repairing $10 million in leaks, margin compaction, all the stuff that was my brand. I listened to it while I was in the shower, when I was driving somewhere at night, before going to bed.
I was able to repeat that pattern statement whenever I needed it — whatever network I was talking to, whatever person I was connecting with over coffee, whatever interview I had. This moved me very fast from first contact to the next stage because I was very clear on what and who I was.
I also made sure that all of my job search materials reflected the same language about my pattern — every move that I took, every place I advertised myself, it was all the same brand. If you looked at my LinkedIn, my résumé, or my cover letter, it was everywhere. I became a brand that showed I fixed unique sets of problems, as opposed to just a generic sales leader.
My brand was still based on my 30 years of proof, but instead of using the proof, I used the brand substantiated by the proof.
We think we should be confident individuals based on the length and depth of our career, and this market just whacks the confidence out of you.
I think that people struggle with that, and they don't have anybody to give that confidence to them again and tell them why they're important and what's unique about them. Learning these strategies from my coach has taught me that I can help people with this. I'd like to help others feel that confidence again too, that I feel now.
Do you have a story to share about navigating your career in your 50s? If so, please reach out to the editor, Agnes Applegate, at aapplegate@businessinsider.com.

9,2026-11-15,"We're about to learn just how much one person truly matters to the AI economy.
Jensen Huang was larger than life at Nvidia. He was the AI boom's most recognizable evangelist, the chip maker's chief visionary and dealmaker, and the force behind Nvidia's ascent to the most valuable company on the planet.
As Zak Jason writes, the cult of the tech founder has very much become America's religion. And Huang was ""its most convincing saint.""
A week ago, Huang admitted to an affair and apologized to his family. Nvidia disclosed it was investigating whether he was the target of a foreign intelligence effort. And then The New York Times reported a second affair, this one with an Nvidia executive who was one of Huang's direct reports. He resigned on Thursday.
Huang's abrupt downfall is a stress test of founder worship. The market put a price on it: Nvidia's market cap tumbled $573 billion last week, more than all but 18 companies in the entire S&P 500.
""It's like the Bulls without Jordan,"" author Stephen Witt, who wrote a biography on Huang, said on CNBC. ""I don't know how they are going to replace this guy.""
That is the essence of key-man risk, as my colleagues Sarah E. Needleman and Tim Paradis reported. When one leader is so closely identified with the strategy, the products, and the culture, their departure can create a vacuum that no org chart (let alone one with 60 direct reports to the CEO) can immediately fill.
Much of Silicon Valley came to Huang's defense in recent days. Sarah Guo, a venture capitalist, called him ""one of the most extraordinary entrepreneurs of all time."" Sequoia's Shaun Maguire said Huang's ""contributions to American innovation and AI leadership cannot be overstated.""
A petition to reinstate Huang as Nvidia CEO circulated over the weekend, collecting more than 100,000 signatures, including those of Elon Musk and other prominent figures. Our own Alistair Barr also points to new reports that are casting doubt on the intelligence behind Jensen Huang's downfall.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
For sure, there are several examples of seemingly irreplaceable CEOs — Steve Jobs at Apple and Travis Kalanick at Uber — whose companies still thrived under new leadership.
And even some of Huang's biggest proponents aren't standing by him. ""No one was a bigger defender of Jensen than I was but there are rules about sex with subordinates,"" CNBC's Jim Cramer said on X. ""Just because someone is brilliant doesn't mean he can do what he wants.""
The question is whether Nvidia, which lacked a defined succession plan, can prove to be more durable than its founder's legend.
If it can't? Dick Costolo, the former Twitter CEO, floated an idea. ""Prediction: 3-4 years from now, [Nvidia] will miss a quarter or two, and they will bring Jensen back to run the company,"" he said.
""Like fame, everybody is outraged for 15 minutes, and there is nothing Americans love more than the comeback story.""

10,2026-11-15,"The real estate market has felt anything but normal in 2026. High interest rates and home prices have turned off many would-be buyers, even in some of the country's previously hottest markets.
New taxes affecting high-end properties, coupled with broader economic uncertainty, have also prompted wealthy buyers to question whether it still makes sense to buy or invest in real estate in their cities — or look elsewhere, even overseas.
These challenges are likely to carry into next year. But Business Insider's 2026 Rising Stars of Real Estate say it's not all doom and gloom.
Some rising stars see a bigger role for agents as buyers and sellers try to make sense of a complicated market. Others are experimenting with AI to manage and grow their businesses. And in some markets, like Miami and New York, they say demand is picking up again.
Here are what 10 of this year's rising stars say are the biggest issues facing real estate and how they think the industry will adapt.
Joey Ben-Zvi, founder of the real-estate firm Ben-Zvi Group, says technology has made data and information more accessible, leading some buyers and sellers to believe they can navigate a transaction on their own, without an agent.
""Consumers have more access to information than ever before, which is ultimately a good thing, but access to information isn't the same as being in the trenches on a daily basis and staying deeply connected to the product and consumers, along with having the experience, judgment, and relationships necessary to navigate a complicated transaction,"" Ben-Zvi told Business Insider.
While Ben-Zvi believes agents remain fundamental to the homebuying and selling experience, he says knowing the basics is no longer enough.
""I think the industry is going through a necessary evolution,"" Ben-Zvi said. ""Agents can no longer rely solely on access to listings or information. They have to demonstrate real value through their expertise, creativity, negotiation skills, and ability to solve problems.""
Saharsh Chordia, the 33-year-old vice president of strategy and analytics at Cortland, said that data will become much more important. The challenge, he said, is turning the vast amount of data generated by physical assets into actionable insight.
""Real estate is fundamentally a business built around physical assets, yet much of the industry's data remains fragmented and underutilized,"" Chordia said. ""AI will play an important role, but the organizations that create a meaningful advantage will be the ones that can effectively collect, structure, and learn from information generated across their portfolios.""
Michael Dillon, a 31-year-old vice president at Prime Finance, said data centers and power-adjacent properties could drive changes in what traditional property types benefit and suffer. He noted that it's unclear how an AI-driven economy could position certain asset types over the long term.
""For a lender, that means underwriting not just the building today, but folding those questions into our view of durable demand for that specific property over a ten-year horizon,"" Dillon said.
Alex Dobosh, an associate director at HKS Real Estate Advisors, sees artificial intelligence as real estate's biggest challenge, and potentially one of its greatest opportunities.
It's not hard to see why. Americans are turning to platforms like ChatGPT for real-estate advice, home-construction guidance, and help with the buying and selling process.
Business Insider has even written about homebuyers who have successfully used AI as their real-estate agent.
Dobosh doesn't see AI as a reason for agents to panic. He says it can be a powerful tool for professionals willing to embrace it. His firm is already doing so.
""Our biggest challenge is also our newest advantage, which is AI,"" Dobosh said. ""It can be overwhelming to see how it's changing our world. We are leveraging it every single day to source business and help our clients, but also because we refuse to fall behind.""
Landy Liu, the 33-year-old founder of Foyer, a ""401(k) for homeownership,"" expects 2027 to be another year of affordability concerns.
""We are facing a structural squeeze on consumers that is leading to a dramatic evolution of the homebuying experience,"" Liu said.
Liu, who founded Foyer to give first-time homebuyers more support throughout the process, said a lack of affordable homes has widened the wealth gap.
""The industry needs to fundamentally change to better support younger Americans and other consumers at a lower cost,"" he said.
Katherine McGrath, whose work at lobbying firm Kasirer deals with land use projects, believes the lack of affordable homes — and of homes in general — is a major challenge, especially in her city of New York.
""Demand continues to rise across all five boroughs, but land, construction, labor, and financing costs make it extremely difficult to build projects with a meaningful share of affordable units,"" McGrath told Business Insider.
McGrath said that mixed-income development is one of the most effective ways to combat both supply and affordability in one blow. But developers need assistance from their local governments.
""Developers can't close that gap alone,"" she said. ""They need incentive programs that reflect real-world costs, a faster approvals process, and continued zoning reform. If we want more affordable homes, we have to make it feasible to build them.""
Cordelia Meserow, a vice president of asset management at real estate investment and management firm Harbor Group International, said that while interest rate volatility has had an effect on pricing commercial buildings in Manhattan, the city's leasing market has never been stronger. The problem is that there's not enough good office space to go around.
""There seems to be no ceiling for what tenants will spend on desirable space,"" Meserow told Business Insider. ""The biggest challenge in New York is that the market is actually running out of Class A space.""
Because of the shortage of Class A buildings, transaction velocity will be constrained.
""Velocity will certainly slow down because of the lack of availability,"" she said.
After the structural changes to commissions, real estate agent Steven Rivera thinks agents need to win back their clients' trust to succeed.
""Since the commission settlement, buyers sign an agreement with their agent before touring a home and rightly ask what they are paying for,"" Rivera told Business Insider. ""That is healthy for the industry, but it raises the bar: agents now have to demonstrate their value, not simply claim it.""
Rivera said he gives his clients a high level of service to instill trust in the final result.
""If you cannot explain what you do for the fee, the market will ask,"" he said.
""Real Estate is built for a world that no longer exists,"" cofounder of Snag, Selin Sonmez, told Business Insider. ""Its biggest challenge is adapting to the new economic reality of the rising generation.""
Sonmez's startup Snag is geared toward younger renters looking to sublet their spots using a social-media-like approach to listing. She's recognized that consumers are getting younger, and thinks the rest of the industry should try to cater to the next generation.
""Young people are expected to rent for 20 years, have multiple jobs, and not be permanent,"" she said. ""How is real estate adapting to this new reality? Most industries shifted to bite-size, approachable, scalable units, from media to investments,"" she said.
James Williams Jr., a shareholder in the real estate group at the law firm Gunster, believes real estate professionals need to adapt their plans to meet the changing needs for housing, employment, and services.
""That means looking at whether a property's existing use still serves the community's needs — and, if not, what it could become,"" Williams Jr. told Business Insider.
But he said the work doesn't stop once you identify a more optimal use for a building; there are a lot of hurdles — like zoning or funding — that need to be overcome to make it happen.
""Existing zoning may not permit the proposed development, infrastructure may need significant upgrades, and the cost of those improvements may make an otherwise promising project financially unworkable,"" he said. ""The challenge is addressing those obstacles while ensuring that the development fits its surroundings and delivers meaningful benefits to the community.""

11,2026-11-15,"Apple’s long awaited smart glasses, akin to Meta Ray-Bans but tailored to Siri AI and the Apple ecosystem, are almost here. According to Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman, the product should be introduced at the next WWDC, and they should be on the market by the end of the year. Here’s what to expect.
First and foremost, Apple wants to rely on its deep app ecosystem to give its glasses a boost. While companies like Meta are undeniably leading in the agentic AI assistant game, the third party app integration just isn’t quite there.
Starting with iOS 27.3 beta, Apple is finally laying the groundwork for proper third party app integration within Siri, rather than just Siri Shortcuts. By the time the glasses release at the end of next year, there should be a plethora of third-party integrations to utilize.
That said, the glasses themselves won’t run apps directly. Everything will rely on a nearby iPhone, per Gurman. The glasses will still have Wi-Fi for apps to function if an iPhone isn’t nearby, but these will largely serve as an iPhone accessory.
According to the report, Apple’s glasses will likely be slightly thinner than a pair of Meta Ray-Bans:
In terms of industrial design, internal prototypes are slightly thinner than Meta’s second-generation Ray-Ban glasses. At least on some test units, the cameras are housed in oval-shaped openings that sit flush with the frame — rather than protruding from it. There’s also a light on the front that shows when the glasses are in use or charging. Apple has been experimenting with other features on the front of the glasses related to privacy.
Lastly, a big focus of these glasses is the audio quality. According to Bloomberg, engineers are describing the audio quality as “industry-leading.”

Forget about the money and just think about dogs instead.  If you own one dog or even 10 dogs you can give them a good life.  If you own even a thousand dogs, I guarantee you some of them will be mistreated.  It is just too much for a single mind to track, you have to assign other people to deal with it and each of them can not track more than 50 or so without hiring minions.  So with a thousand dogs you probably have 20 or more people.  5 percent of people are criminals, so one of these is likely to be an a-hole mistreating 50 dogs.
This does not give the rich an excuse.  Just because something is hard does not give you a pass for not being ethical.   You got the money, either give it all away or take responsibility for what you do with it.
Part of being rich is making sure that the people you hire do what you want them to do, not just what they think you want them to do.
Surprised more landfill operators don't have waste to energy operations (downstream of recycling).  It isn't perfect, but it does a lot to reduce the operating costs of the landfill.
Even if he was entirely above board in trying to tackle environmental issues, would his actions be any different here?  I am not trying to give him more credit than he is due, but getting regulations passed at the federal level to fix this isn't going to happen.  Owning a controlling stake is about the only thing that reasonably could.  The question is whether he has the will or the ability to lead that change.
What, pray tell, should we do with our trash?  Genuine question.
And ""make less of it"" is a religious statement, not an answer.
Thanks.

14,2026-11-15,"Jensen Huang is considering a return as Nvidia's chief executive, days after he resigned over reports that he had affairs with a suspected Chinese spy and a subordinate, the Financial Times reported Sunday.
Huang has discussed the possibility with confidants, according to the FT, which cited people familiar with the matter. One ally told the paper they were putting together a ""war room"" of Silicon Valley executives to lobby Nvidia's board for his return and would try to win support from the White House. The ally said Huang backed the effort.
Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney have backed a public letter calling on Nvidia's board to ""Bring Jensen Back."" On Friday, Nvidia shareholders Brad Gerstner of Altimeter Capital and Gavin Baker of Atreides Management urged the board in an open letter to keep ""all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang.""
A spokesperson for Nvidia and a spokesperson for Huang declined to comment to the FT. Huang doesn't hold a controlling stake in Nvidia and can't overrule its board.
The push runs into opposition from Washington. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent on Sunday called for a ""dramatic shakeup"" at Nvidia and urged its board to look outside the company for a new CEO.
""I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?"" Bessent said on Fox News, CNBC reported. He said he wants ""answers in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside counsel,"" according to Axios.
""If this were a bank and they hadn't complied like this, the OCC would be in there right now, and a lot of the executives would be removed,"" Bessent said. Nvidia has said it complies with U.S. export laws.
Bessent has also blamed the Commerce Department, led by Secretary Howard Lutnick, for not restricting Megaspeed, the Singapore-based firm at the center of the scandal. ""Treasury, War, USTR and State, they were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the Entity List for months, but Commerce has the pen,"" Bessent told CNBC on Friday. Lutnick has agreed to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 10, CBS News reported.
Nvidia reports quarterly earnings Tuesday, its first since Huang's resignation. Interim CEO Colette Kress has scheduled a company-wide meeting for Wednesday, CNBC reported. The board said Friday it has not yet hired a search firm or ""formally engaged any potential candidates"" to replace Huang, according to CNBC. The FT reported Friday that the board had approached Arm CEO Rene Haas.
Huang resigned Thursday after revelations that he had extramarital affairs with multiple women, one of whom was an alleged Chinese spy. The company said in a statement that Kress, Nvidia's executive vice president and chief financial officer, will serve as interim CEO. Harvey C. Jones was appointed chair of the board.
Nvidia's stock plunged 13% at the market open Thursday. The company has lost more than $1.2 trillion in market value since the scandal broke in late October, Axios reported.
In its statement, the board thanked Huang for his ""immeasurable contributions to the company."" The chipmaker is at the center of the global AI economy and has multiple agreements with the U.S. government. Huang, who founded Nvidia in 1993, led the company through the AI boom that made it one of the world's most valuable publicly traded companies.
Last Sunday, Nvidia's board of directors said it was launching an investigation into Huang being the target of an alleged Chinese spy, with whom he had an affair.
Huang admitted to the affair in an email to Nvidia employees and said he is cooperating with the investigation. Reports have identified Alice Huang (no relation), a former executive at tech firm Megaspeed, as the woman he had the relationship with.
""I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry,"" Jensen Huang said in a statement.
The New York Times reported Wednesday that Huang also had an affair with Kimberly Powell, a married Nvidia executive who reported directly to him. Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, joined the company in 2008.
On Thursday, Reuters reported that Huang also had an ""intimate relationship"" with another Nvidia employee. Reuters did not identify the woman, and Nvidia declined to comment.
Rep. Brian Mast, the Republican chairman of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told reporters Thursday that Huang had agreed to testify before Congress after being threatened with a subpoena. House Republicans have asked Huang to sit for a closed-door interview on Dec. 1, CNBC reported.
Here's what we know so far, and why the scandal matters.
Jensen Huang founded Nvidia, one of the biggest makers of specialized computer chips in the world. Its chips were first designed for computer graphics, but demand for Nvidia's hardware has skyrocketed with the AI boom of the past few years. China and the U.S. are in a heated competition for AI technology, and that includes access to chips like the ones Nvidia makes.
Huang is an American citizen who was born in Taiwan. He founded Nvidia in 1993 and served as its president and CEO until Thursday.
According to the New York Times, it's unclear when Jensen Huang met Alice Huang, but the two were photographed together in 2024 at a bar in Taipei, Taiwan. That was roughly when Megaspeed, the AI company she worked for, began its push into the AI sector. News reports have not confirmed whether Alice Huang still works at Megaspeed. Megaspeed has touted its membership in Nvidia's partnership program, which would give it access to Nvidia's latest technology.
Huang apologized for the affair in a message to Nvidia employees and asked for privacy for his family. ""I take full responsibility for what I did and the hurt I caused,"" Huang wrote, ""and I accept the criticism that comes with it.""
He pledged to earn back his employees' confidence and said he was cooperating fully with the board's investigation. ""I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security,"" he wrote.
The New York Times reported that U.S. intelligence officials believe Alice Huang was gathering information for the Chinese government. China denied that it was spying, calling the allegation unsubstantiated. ""It is nothing but smears against China,"" a Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesperson said.
The New York Times also reported that Nvidia executives contacted the U.S. Commerce Department last year seeking information about the department's investigation into Megaspeed. The investigation was later transferred to the Justice Department, which is reportedly looking into whether Megaspeed illegally routed Nvidia products into China.
AI is the biggest thing in tech right now, from chatbots like ChatGPT and Claude to software development and bioengineering. Governments are trying to work out how to use AI for security and across the economy, and China and the U.S. are racing to lead its development. The technology has moved so fast that some CEOs of AI companies have suggested a pause before it develops beyond what humans can control.
Nvidia has contracts with the U.S. government to use its technology in several areas, and Huang has a close relationship with President Trump. U.S. officials are trying to determine whether the affair allowed the Chinese government to obtain information about Nvidia's technology and its agreements with the U.S. government. Nvidia's board said in its statement that it does not believe national security or company secrets were compromised.
Trump said last Sunday that he considers Huang ""a fine person"" who should stay on as CEO of Nvidia.
""I think he probably has an excuse for it. We'll have to hear it,"" Trump told reporters. ""He's done an amazing job for our country.""
On Tuesday, the New York Times reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who is also national security adviser, told Trump in May that the U.S. believed Huang was involved with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy. Trump received the information aboard Air Force One en route to a summit in China, the Times reported. Huang went with the president on that trip.
The Times said it was not the first time Trump had been told about U.S. intelligence on the affair. But the president said this week he was not aware of Huang's affair when Huang joined him for the China summit.

15,2026-11-15,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Robot vacuums are perhaps the most sci-fi modern invention that has proved realistically practical. The idea of an autonomous robot that goes around cleaning your home is nothing short of insane, yet unlike most sci-fi gadgets we wish really existed, it is a technology that is actually seen in action in many houses today. Of course, it wasn't always smooth sailing. The early days of robot vacuums were fraught with challenges, as the emerging technology grappled with the real-life conditions of operating in a messy home environment.
There are many barriers to simply getting around the house, navigating through the obstacles big and small, and dealing with carpets and thresholds between rooms. Then there are factors like handling especially dirty areas or avoiding tangles in clumps of pet hair, which proved tricky even for the most expensive vacuums. And that's before we get into the new factors introduced by integrating mopping into robot vacuums: dealing with dirty water, the reach of the mops, and not running out of charge between vacuuming and mopping.
But robot vacuums see innovation year after year, and the issues we outlined above have already been solved by the latest models. The robot vacuums of today are smarter, faster, and more efficient than ever before, packing a lot of convenient features that make them completely hands-free. So let's look at some robot vacuum upgrades that just work.
What is the most fundamental feature of a good robot vacuum? It is not the suction power or mopping technology; it is the mapping. Mapping out the house accurately is the first step toward navigation, and without it, the robot vacuum cannot efficiently traverse the area, let alone understand which areas to clean. Of course, older vacuums skipped this step entirely, relying on random bump navigation, but we have long realized the issues with that approach. Now, every modern vacuum has mapping built-in.
On the surface, it may appear that the technology used for this has not changed at all, as LiDAR is still present in every robot vacuum. But the actual implementation of this sensor has changed. Older robot vacuums used LiDAR LDS (Laser Distance Sensor), with a spinning laser turret on top of the robot to determine the positions of objects. New robot vacuums like the MOVA V70 Ultra Complete have moved to a different variant of the technology: dToF (direct Time-of-Flight) LiDAR.
Instead of roughly computing the distance, the dToF LiDAR sensor measures the time taken by the reflection of every pulse of light, calculating the precise distance involved in real time. This means that modern robot vacuums can now rapidly construct extremely accurate maps of the environment, and then retract the sensor when needed. This also translates to more precise customization of the maps, letting you designate virtual no-go zones or extra cleaning zones to control your robot vacuum's behavior.
When we talk of robot vacuum technologies, it is the robot vacuums themselves that get all the attention, but their humble charging docks have also come a long way. They aren't just basic charging stations anymore, but fully featured cleaning stations in their own right. The changes started simple – docks featuring self-emptying bins, pulling the robot's dirt into a larger bag in the dock. But the introduction of a mopping feature in robot vacuums means that they need a lot more maintenance now. Water has to be filled, detergent loaded, and the mop pads themselves cleaned after every cycle.
Doing all this manually is a tedious chore, defeating the purpose of an automated cleaning machine. So robot vacuum docks have also evolved to make the robot vacuum hands-free again. New-age robot vacuum docks like the Saros 20 Flow will not only empty the robot's dustbin, but will also clean the mop pads. Not just by dousing them in water, but with hot water jets, which kills germs and eliminates sticky residue, ensuring zero smell and complete sterilization.
Of course, this is followed up with the regular mopping maintenance. The water chamber of the robot is refilled, new detergent is loaded up, and a hot blower dries the mop pads, leaving the robot ready for the next cycle of cleaning. This, along with the other advanced features, makes the Saros 20 one of the best robot vacuums you can buy in 2026, according to experts.
All leading vacuums feature high suction power already, but what about mopping? It is still a relatively new feature, with a lot of nuance in functioning, and quite a few moving parts. The design of the mop itself, the cleaning process used, or the methodology for handling different types of surfaces. Many factors governing a robot mop's cleaning performance, and therefore plenty of innovation in this space.
The simplest robot vacuums with mops simply add a spinning mop pad at the bottom (or even several) to mop the floor as it moves. But there are many problems with this approach. For one, this leaves edges and corners untouched. It also struggles with carpets, as, unlike vacuums that can universally run on both floors and carpets, a mop must only be used on floors. Even leaving aside carpets, taking the same mop through different types of flooring — say kitchen tiles with a spill and your bedroom floor — just drags the dirty mop through the house.
The latest robot vacuum mops fix these issues with more dynamic mopping systems. The mops are attached to little arms, which means they can be lifted over carpets and extended outward to reach corners. The best vacuums even come with the ability to change their own mop pads now. The Dreame Matrix X Pro, for example, will swap out its mop pads after every type of area, with separate cleaning solutions for each as well.
How a robot vacuum navigates the world is one of its most key features. We touched upon this briefly when we discussed mapping, but detecting walls and furniture to construct a map of the surroundings is just one half of the puzzle. The robot vacuum also needs to navigate past the smaller obstacles along the way, from things like toys to random socks on the floor. These smaller objects are more of an issue than walls because the robot won't just bump off them, but get stuck.
The standard LiDAR module that serves as a robot vacuum's primary sensor is useless in this regard. The LiDAR is mounted on top of the machine and cannot fire lasers at tiny objects in the way. To cover this blind spot, robot vacuums started integrating other sensors like 3D structured light, or even an RGB camera. But while these did improve the object avoidance of robot vacuums, each method had its own shortcomings, as the robot lacked the intelligence to combine them all.
With improved AI models, however, that's possible now. We are getting robot vacuums that can put together a comprehensive picture of their surroundings through multiple sensing methods. The Narwal Freo Z10 Ultra, for example, features two high-definition cameras to accurately recognize even the smallest objects. This might seem concerning, as robot vacuums have a big problem with privacy, but the Freo has dual AI chips onboard to process the video feed locally, not uploading to any server.
Robot vacuums are great for apartments with smooth tiled floors, as the little robot can zoom through the whole place without an issue. But what about houses with elevation differences between various rooms? Sometimes it could be a floor that is a couple of inches higher, or an elevated threshold in the doorway. Both of these stump most robot vacuums running on wheels, preventing them from accessing most of your house and cleaning it.
Failing to prepare the house for a robot vacuum is one of those common mistakes that robot vacuum owners should try to avoid, but it is tricky. Even with ramps, things like jutting legs of furniture can still get the robot stuck. So robot vacuums started adding threshold climbing to their models. Most are capable of pushing their chassis up for a few millimeters of clearance, which can only cross the shallowest thresholds. Modern robot vacuums improve upon this by also featuring proper folding legs to lift the robot even higher, letting it more easily cross higher thresholds.
The Dreame X50 Ultra, for example, can go over chair legs and room thresholds without getting stuck, thanks to its retractable legs. It (and any other flagship robot of today) is also capable of the opposite, retracting the LiDAR sensor and using its slim profile to slide underneath furniture to clean even hard-to-reach areas. We are even starting to see robot vacuums that can climb staircases, though only in tech expos — for now.
Every modern robot vacuum features powerful suction, which is good for cleaning. But this also has a drawback in that the robot uses more power, and its battery drains most quickly as a result. For the most part, robot vacuums are designed to be able to complete a cleaning cycle on that one full charge, heading back to the dock to spend hours getting back that juice, but for larger houses, this does not work out. The average 60 minutes of runtime cannot cover the whole area, especially for models that mop and vacuum both.
Even with a smaller home, if you have a lot of clutter, the robot will backtrack a lot and will end up being less efficient in cleaning the area, wasting time. This can also lead to a scenario where the robot runs out of charge mid-cleaning and has to return to charge before resuming the cleaning cycle. And since charging these powerful vacuums with big battery capacities takes a while, that slows down the cleaning process significantly.
To solve this issue, modern robot vacuums like the Ecovacs Deebot X11 have started incorporating fast charging. This is the same technology you see in smartphones, but on a larger scale, capable of quickly topping up the robot vacuum batteries. This GaN-based fast charging activates even as the robot returns to the base station to refill its water tank or empty the dustbin, giving it a little more charge to complete the cycle.
Dealing with pet hair scattered through the whole house is one of the main reasons people invest in robot vacuums. Only a tireless robot can keep up with the steady stream of hair shed by your pets — assuming the hair doesn't clog up their vacuums. Pet hair is coarser and hardier than human hair, with longer strands. This means that the tiny suction port of a robot vacuum easily gets choked up on pet hair, requiring manual cleaning to de-tangle the hair and let it work again. Even vacuums with brushes end up getting the hair tangled in the brushes, rendering them useless.
The solution undertaken by new robot vacuums like the Narwal Freo Pro is two-fold. Firstly, specialized brushes. Instead of a standard roller brush, detangling brushes feature a spiral grooved design that ensures the hair is not caught up in the brush but is separated and funneled into the suction port. They usually feature rubber bristles instead of plastic to be able to detach from the hair strands more easily.
These new Freo vacuums also come with a second anti-tangle brush to force hair coming in from any directions into the detangling brush without getting stuck on the surface. This is paired with a better dustbin management system. Since a house with pets will fill up the robot's tiny bin in a single run, modern robot vacuums will use self-emptying functions to frequently clean out the vacuum's own bin, and even compress the dirt collected.
The mapping capability and the object avoidance take the focus when talking about sensing capabilities, as that is central to the navigation ability of a robot vacuum. But for a device meant for cleaning, what about a method for detecting dirt itself? Traditionally, robot vacuums aren't equipped to assess the cleanliness of the house. They simply go over the whole house, vacuuming and mopping the floor they come across, irrespective of how clean or dirty any particular part is.
This means that while robot vacuums are good at evenly cleaning out the daily dirt buildup, they can struggle with spills or filthy patches that need more dedicated cleaning. Of course, you can try to manually fix that by forcing it to do another cycle, or limiting it to a particular area to clean it again, but these are convoluted workarounds. The actual solution would be for the robot vacuum to be able to somehow see stains.
And that's what the latest robot vacuums like the Shark PowerDetect are now doing. Just as black light flashlights can be used for spotting stains, robot vacuums can detect spills or stains with UV light, even when invisible to the naked eye. Then the onboard AI categorizes the type of mess to respond with the right cleaning method, vacuuming debris, mopping up light spills, and on some models, even scrubbing it clean with hot water. The robot will take multiple passes if required to clean up any mess completely.
The whole premise that makes robot vacuums appealing is a machine that will clean your house automatically without you having to do a thing. But the logistics of this also need to be considered. If your robot zips around trying to clean the kitchen while you are cooking in it, or has to dodge people going about the living room, it becomes more of a nuisance. This is not an aspect that can be handled with better sensors or smarter AI. It requires smart scheduling from your own end.
Which may sound simple, but is anything but. Even with older robot vacuums, you could always set up cleaning times, which in theory ensures that your house is cleaned without conflicting with your own day. In practice, however, the cleaning process itself takes a while, and not every room is occupied at the same time. The schedule for cleaning your bedroom should be different from the one for your hallways. With new-age robot vacuums, this kind of discrete control is actually possible.
Thanks to better navigation systems and more detailed maps that robot vacuums can create these days, it is easy to designate various rooms and schedule them individually. You can even split vacuuming and mopping schedules, or vary the cleaning intensity. The Dreame X60, for example, has a comprehensive scheduling feature that gives complete, granular control. It is one of the reasons owners say it is one of the most reliable robot vacuums."

News > Social Media
There'll be an Emergency Alert System test on that date, but predictions that a catastrophe is coming are likely false, experts said.

				Edited by Laura Michelle Davis 
November 15, 2026, 8:35 am ET
As global conspiracy theories go, this is a juicy one. 
On Tuesday, Nov. 17, at around 2:20 p.m. ET, the Federal Emergency Management Agency and the Federal Communications Commission are planning a test of the Emergency Alert System. That’s when all hell is supposed to break loose. At least, that’s what you might believe if you only rely on social media.
Coming just days after an abrupt and disruptive government-ordered shutdown of air travel in the US and technical issues that caused an unwelcome outage of widely used Microsoft workplace software, the test will be happening in an atmosphere of heightened anxiety.
More from CNET
Videos across Facebook, Instagram and TikTok have floated the theory that this emergency test signals a catastrophe is coming, potentially stemming from a cyberattack or a digital disruption that could knock out electricity, internet and other services. Many have started scrambling to buy emergency supplies, pointing to the need for a 90-day stockpile of food, water, cash and essential medicines in case hackers wipe out bank accounts and shut down the web. 
Similar rumors have spread globally, as the governments of France, the UK and Canada have put renewed attention on the importance of households being able to survive a potential weather-related crisis or technological disaster.
How extreme have the conspiracy theories gotten? Some have cited a 2023 Simpsons episode as proof, which referenced a massive Nov. 17 blackout in Lisa’s future, even though it didn’t mention a year.
For Gen Xers and Boomers, this panic could feel familiar. Leading up to the year 2000, there were fears that a potential computer-related disaster, known as Y2K, would cause widespread chaos and disruption, based on the belief that older digital systems couldn’t handle the calendar change. The new year ended with only minor glitches, thanks to a significant global effort to update computer systems to avoid the bug.
Before we delve into the suspicions, let’s start with a bit of debunking: CNET spoke with multiple experts in disaster preparedness and cyberattacks, who all cautioned against letting our guard down. But remember that no federal agency or institution has mentioned a blackout. Unless hackers decide to take advantage of the hysteria already generated, there’s no reason to think Nov. 17 is any riskier than any other date.
Richard Mariano, a protective intelligence expert at Kronus Intelligence, who spent nearly three decades with the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives, said that people share all sorts of things that get picked up and go viral. “Then it creates panic where panic is not necessary,” he said.
Mariano said too many people are spreading fear rather than seeking the truth. “On every one of these social media posts that is suspect or has some form of conspiratorial edge to them: source it, source it, source it.”
Next week’s nationwide Emergency Alert System, or EAS, is similar to the public tests Americans have experienced since 1963, when the Emergency Broadcast System replaced an older radio-based warning system called CONELRAD. In 1997, the Emergency Broadcast System was replaced by the EAS, part of FEMA’s Integrated Public Alert and Warning System, which broadcasts emergency alerts to any wireless device in the country, including phones, radios and televisions. The system was designed for the president to communicate with US households within 10 minutes of a national emergency. 
The Nov. 17 test will consist of a nationwide EAS message issued by FEMA, noting that no public action is required. The test will include standard alert tones to support accessibility for those who are deaf or hard of hearing and should last about a minute. This time around, wireless emergency alerts will not be triggered on mobile phones, so don’t expect a notification on your cellphone. After the alert, programming should resume as normal on TV, radio and other broadcast channels. 
Many equate the emergency system with threats of nuclear war, but it could also be used to alert people to a major weather event, a national cyberattack or even AMBER alerts at the local level (without a presidential message). So it’s natural there might be some anxiety. 
“With this event simulating an activation of an emergency notification system, it would be a logical next step to worry about what happens if something bad happens during that test,” said Robby Winchester, chief global professional services officer at SpecterOps, a Virginia-based security company,
But, all in all, these system tests are routine. Local stations conduct a weekly test, and federal law requires a nationwide EAS test every three years. In addition to helping people get used to responding to alerts, the tests also allow the government to identify potential technical or operational issues it needs to fix before an actual emergency alert.FEMA has a backup date of Dec. 3 in case it can’t perform it on Nov. 17 — which could happen if the agency is dealing with a real emergency or if there are technical issues. Representatives from the FCC and FEMA did not respond to a request for comment.
Another factor that helped spark conspiracy theories was a Pentagon order requiring military bases to stock enough supplies for 90 days without outside support. Dubbed “Fortress America,” the initiative memo (PDF) was released in late September but got more attention as the November date approached.
The timing of that memo coincided with the UK launching a campaign on Oct. 22 called Be Ready, urging citizens to keep enough supplies (PDF) such as water, food, medical kits and pet products to last at least three days. Other countries, including Japan, Sweden and about a dozen more, are also urging citizens to prepare for emergencies.
Still, the blackout rumor isn’t solely driven by government communications. Heightened global instability over the past few months has also played a major role. Just in the last month, the Federal Aviation Administration called a nationwide ground stop on all departing flights; a major Microsoft Outlook outage kept millions of people from working; PHP vulnerabilities exposed websites to hacking; and cyberattacks have targeted supermarket fridges and freezers across Europe.
On the financial side, Nvidia’s CEO scandal led to a stock plunge and fears of an AI bust, while Paraguay just experienced a major digital bank collapse that froze millions of accounts. The ongoing wars in Iran and Ukraine have sparked national security concerns, and floods in Nepal and fires in Indonesia demonstrate the increased dangers of climate change.
Though there’s never been a nationwide power outage in the US (several regional outages have been triggered by overloaded lines and software bugs), The New York Times reports that there’s still no comprehensive plan for what to do if the country goes completely dark.
All the online chatter surrounding Nov. 17 has raised concerns that warnings of a potential cyberattack might inadvertently trigger one. 
Winchester said that even though there’s no specific information indicating an imminent crisis, “it is common for attackers to use current events of all types to help with achieving successful attacks.” The upside? If people are being spurred by government notices and their anxiety over global instability to prepare emergency supplies, it could actually help in the event of a real threat or disaster.
Regardless of whether you buy into the Nov. 17 conspiracy theories, it doesn’t hurt to have some supplies at the ready, which could be useful in the event of a digital disruption or weather event, even if it doesn’t involve nuclear weapons or hackers.
“We live in a world where there are many threats to our communities, our families and ourselves,” said Philip Farina, chief advisor for Risky Business and a federal law enforcement veteran. “Understanding the basics of self-reliance is important. Even preparing for a residential fire, which is very common, can help to save lives and protect us better.”
You don’t need to buy enough for 90 days off the grid (and most of us can’t afford that), but it’s recommended that families have at least three days’ worth of food and supplies. Some of the basics listed on Ready.gov’s Build a Kit page include:
While warnings from governments can spark panic and distrust, they can be beneficial at a time when we’re so reliant on the internet and cell service, which can be easily disrupted. Mariano said it’s worth being prepared in the “old-fashioned ways.”

Humans evolved to survive in a violent world.  Those instincts haven't simply evaporated after a few decades of cultural enlightenment.
It is far preferable to allow those instincts to find expression in a completely harmless video game than out in the real world.  Channeling violence in this way eliminates stress and brings players back to a position of emotional balance from which they can cope with the burdens of everyday life.  It also has side-effects of improving cognitive health and helping to build and solidify communities of friendship.
The overt themes are distasteful because they depict aspects of the real world that we don't like.  But that's also what makes the game effective in providing a healthy outlet for the very instincts that can create those tragedies.  The many studies that have been done on the topic have always given the same results: playing violent video games does not make people violent.  The harm that the haters complain about simply isn't there, according to consciously-obtained data.
You are free to find your own balance.  But before sitting in judgment of those who find playing games like this to be anodyne for the soul, maybe take the time to reflect on what life might be like for people in other circumstances than your own, and how something like this might be a net social benefit.

Published Nov 15, 2026
