 Make your contribution now and help Gothamist thrive in 2026.  Donate today 
Gothamist is funded by sponsors and member donations
In a conference room in Manhattan on a recent afternoon, a speaker named Claira spoke to a captive audience about the benefits of using an artificial intelligence agent as a personal assistant.
Claira would know — she herself is one.
The bot, appearing on a large screen, had caramel-color skin and bouncy, curly hair, and is the first ever Afro-Latina, multilingual virtual assistant to hit the market, according to Abran Maldonado, who helped invent her.
Claira was designed, Maldonado said, to bring a multicultural perspective to the AI space, which too often leaves Black and brown voices out of the mix.
Maldonado has showcased Claira across the globe, but in this case relied on the bot to help keynote last week's Latino AI Summit, in front of dozens of attendees who were mostly Latino and Black.
“It’s about visibility,” Claira told Gothamist in an interview. “When people see an Afro-Latina AI, it challenges the idea of who belongs in tech and opens the door for inclusive innovation.”
The Latino AI Summit, the in-person iteration of a forum that also has been featured online, is one of several efforts cropping up in the city designed to empower Black and brown people to get jobs in the AI world, hear from experts about how AI is changing the workforce, and build personalized tools that people can apply to their daily lives or jobs.
Other organizations are pushing similar initiatives, also with an eye toward reaching those often on the sidelines of the tech landscape, an arena at the center of attention, with the upcoming initial public offering of AI giant Anthropic set for Wednesday and expected to value the company at more than $1 trillion.
The Knowledge House in the Bronx provides coding and design training for underserved young people interested in the burgeoning AI economy. For adults, the Rev. Al Sharpton’s AI Fluency Initiative teaches people without tech experience the basics of AI. There, the mission is simple: It's to ""close the digital divide and put the power of these tools in the hands of the people,"" the group states on its website.
The imperative is crystal clear to Michal Tapia, the cofounder of the Latino AI summit, which was held at Civic Hall and Berkeley College in Midtown. AI will play a major role in our future, whether people like it or not, she said.
The key now, she said, is making sure that people of all ethnicities and backgrounds are involved in the builders’ room.
“AI is happening, and it's either we get on the train, or we get left behind,” Tapia said.
Early research on the impact of AI on Black and brown communities is a mixed bag.
A disproportionate number of Black workers hold jobs that are likely to be automated by AI, including in transportation, production and administration roles, according to a report from the McKinsey Institute for Economic Mobility.
And Black workers may already be feeling the squeeze. In a 2025 report from Jobs for the Future, a national nonprofit that influences education and workforce systems, found that 67% of Black survey respondents said they feel like AI is already impacting their jobs, compared to 57% of white survey respondents.
The report also found that more Black respondents said they feel like they need to gain new skills due to the impact of AI on their work than the overall sample of people surveyed. More than half of Black respondents in the same survey said they already use AI tools daily or weekly, compared to about 40% of all respondents.
Latinos, likewise, are underrepresented in the tech space. About 19% of the U.S. workforce is Latino, according to a report from the Kapor Foundation, which publishes research on racial equity and technology issues. But Latinos fill just 3% of board seats, 5% of executive leadership roles, and 6% of technical roles across the country’s largest tech companies, according to the same report.
To stay in the game, Maldonado said in an interview, it’s essential for all jobseekers to market themselves as AI-literate.
No matter what field you’re in, your resume should change, Maldonado said. The section that used to list skills in programs like Microsoft Office and the Adobe Suite, now should list proficiency in AI tools like Claude, Codex and Google AI Studio.
And instead of updating a resume every couple of years, jobseekers should update it once a month — that’s how fast the relevant AI tools change, he said.
But even ChatGPT, an AI chatbot, is becoming antiquated, he said. Baseline prerequisites for jobs of the future will include things like knowing how to use Claude projects, an AI work space and Cowork, a tool that helps people complete tasks. And ultimately, he added, administrative tasks should be delegated to an AI agent.
The best thing a person can do to prepare themselves for the changing workforce, Maldonado said, is to always be learning.
“Putting our heads in the sand is not the move,” he said. “ You have to tinker. You have to try these things out.”
Schools will also have to meet the moment, experts say — and some already are. Alpha School, for example, are K-12 schools with programs across the country that provide kids training on AI tools.
“ Kids are scoring through the roof ... the level of fluency and literacy that they have,” Maldonado said.
These curriculums need to be available in all schools, not just those that are predominantly white or wealthy, tech experts argue. Taylor Shead, founder of a company that develops AI-driven education, in the Jobs for the Future report, emphasized “prevention over intervention.”
“Equity and access mean meeting people where they are—going to the systems Black learners and workers are in,” she wrote in the report.
Kara Emery, the director of data science at the AI Hub of NYU’s McSilver Institute for Poverty Policy and Research, said time will tell how these things shake out.
“A lot of these projections of what AI will potentially impact are just that,” Emery said. “Projections on the future of what this technology might be able to do.”
The fear, said Emery, is that AI could make the already staggering racial income inequalities worse. But, Emery emphasized, people have a say in how things unfold.
There are “preparations and things that can be done now to try to protect against what could be a continuation of this wealth gap,” she said.
Summit attendee Fradely Delacruz, a computational scientist student and a professional data architect, said she is already feeling the economy change beneath her feet.
“Women of color specifically, we are rapidly losing our jobs in the STEM space and the research and development space,” Delacruz said. We’re “losing our jobs in huge amounts.”
Delacruz said she doesn’t entirely blame AI, which she feels is only exacerbating existing social systems and prejudice.
But, she said, half the battle is making sure all people have access to resources and information about what technology is up and coming and how it’s being designed.
“ When AI came, it kind of felt like there was other people who knew about it before we did,” Delacruz said. “By the time we're catching on, we're like, ‘Oh, we're not even part of the conversation.’ I think it's really important to position ourselves in community as innovators.”
Emery said she is seeing Black and brown New Yorkers rising to the occasion.
“ These communities aren't just sitting on the sidelines waiting for these things to have a negative impact,” Emery said. “They really have the agency.”
Not everyone is quite as optimistic.
David Gabeau, who created an online interface of AI companies in New York, is an AI entrepreneur who worked at tech-driven companies like Snapchat and Lyft before eventually starting his own company, which he has since retired.
Gabeau, who is Black, said that his race was one of the reasons he was inspired to work in tech. He said he felt like the social media he consumed growing up was based on Black culture, and he wanted to contribute to the technology’s design.
But after years of working in the space, he now fears that AI is only broadening social inequities across racial lines.
“People [working in AI] are going to get so, so so wealthy,” Gabeau said.
It’s my biggest fear, he continued, that “there will be no forcing function to spread the wealth, and that it will massively and drastically create a divide between the have and have nots.”
After spending 10 years in New York, Gabeau recently moved to San Francisco to get closer to the epicenter of AI.
There, he said, he recently spent an afternoon wearing a 4 pound headset that scanned his brain while he watched videos and answered questions. The effort was for a company that is working on using AI to develop a tool that’s akin to telepathy: the tool converts your thoughts into text without the need to type.
Gabeau said he was paid about $50 for his troubles, although he participated for the sake of his own curiosity. But the experience was an eye opener, Gabeau said. The other people in the room, many of whom were Black and brown, seemed to be there because they needed the money.
“These people's thoughts are gonna have helped create and train the AIs,” Gabeau said. “And they're definitely not gonna receive the benefits of this company getting very big.”
“It was a little predatory,” he added.
But Emery, the NYU researcher, said that sometimes the most outsize fears about AI come from within the tech industry itself. We should be wary, she said, of taking the promises of these companies’ success at face value.
“ This narrative of the inevitability of what these technologies will become is something that's often pushed by those who are currently in the big companies building them now,” Emery said.
She said the right combination of policy, education and a diversity of innovators working behind the scenes is key to preventing existing inequalities from worsening.
And Delacruz, one of the students who attended the summit, said she’s eager to put her skills to use. But at this point, she said she still feels unseen.
“They don't see the scientists and the researchers that are Black and brown and Latinos and Afro-Latinos. They don't know we exist, but we're here,” Delacruz said. “Well, let's look at the people who are professionals and let's collaborate.

					By Ted Johnson 
Political Editor

Bowen Yang returned to Saturday Night Live to play Jensen Huang in the show’s cold open, a skewering of tech billionaires susceptibility to honey pots, or being wooed by attractive women and thinking that they genuinely are in it for the romance.

	The skit follows Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia following revelations that he had an affair with a woman who may have been a Chinese spy, and another affair with a subordinate. That sent shares of the company tumbling.

	“When you invite Jensen over, you know what is about to go down — the S&P 500,” Yang’s Huang said. “That’s right — I’m into BDSM — Breaking Da Stock Market.”

	










			Watch on Deadline		






	The premise of the skit had John Mulaney, this week’s host, playing a CIA official trying to instruct the gathered tech billionaires to be on the lookout for women who show interest in them. 

	“America needs you to learn the difference between a woman who would actually want to have sex with you and a woman who is clearly a spy,” the CIA official told them. “Now you all saw former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resined this week after he was acught having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy. What do you think the lesson is here?”

	“Yeah, never date a Chinese woman,” Anthropic’s Dario Amodei (Jane Wickline) answered.

	“No,” the CIA official said.

	“Never date a human,” said Mark Zuckerberg (Andrew Dismukes). 

	“No,” the CIA official said.

	“Never date anyone lest they become the Antichrist,” responded Peter Thiel (James Austin Johnson).

	“No, and Peter, you got to chill with the Antichrist stuff,” the CIA official told him.

	The recent midterms in many ways were a backlash against the AI overlords, with opposition to data centers reflecting fears of AI’s impact on jobs and humanity.

	“Show us some respect. We do a lot for this country,” Thiel told the CIA official. “Name one time technology has let us down.”

	“Yesterday, when every plane in America was grounded,” the CIA official answered.

	










	“Okay, name one more time.”

	“The day before that, when Microsoft went dark,” the CIA official reminded.

	Mulaney’s character went on, “Look, I get it, I can’t imagine how disorienting this has been for you to go from these bug-eyed little weirdos to being gods. I mean, never in the history of our species have people as pale and strange as you, a) survived, and b) been given all the world’s wealth and power.”

	The skit also featured Ben Marhsall as OpenAI’s Sam Altman and Jeremy Culhane’s return as an Elon Musk, incomprehensible to the point where the CIA official has to spray him with a water bottle to shut him up. 

	The CIA official tried to get across the dangers they were facing in getting hooked in to a spy plot.

	“Let’s start an exercise,” the CIA official told them. “Let’s say you’re at a bar and an attractive woman approaches and asks you, ‘What are you working on?’ Dario, what do you say?”

	Amodei responded, “I would say, ‘Madam, what a pleasure to see you this evening. May I interest you in an industry secret?'”

	“No, no,” the CIA official said.

	Zuckerberg then said, “I would just assume that she wanted to sleep with the man who created the metaverse, as all woman do.”

	“But they don’t, right?” the CIA official said, before showing him a Venn diagram. Next to a large circle of beautiful women was a tiny dot representing “people who like the metaverse.”

	Watch the sketch above.

28,2026-11-15,"When it comes to 3-in-1 travel chargers, I look at two main things: How fast it can charge my iPhone and how portable it is. I have tested plenty of travel chargers that aim to be as portable as possible by using lightweight materials or folding into a smaller footprint, but there usually seems to be some sort of compromise. But this new K4 Air by Kuxiu might be the most impressive option I’ve tested to date. This charger is just 8.5mm thin, weighs less than 120 g, and can wirelessly charge your iPhone at up to 25W. After spending a few weeks with it and traveling across the Atlantic, here is what you should know about the Kuxiu K4 Air.
Be sure to check out our hands-on video with the K4 Air to see it in action!
The biggest selling point of the new Kuxiu K4 Air is its design. When completely folded, this thing is barely thicker than an iPhone. For reference, my iPhone 17 Pro measures roughly 8.25mm thick, while the K4 Air comes in at just 8.5mm. That is insanely compact for something that can charge three devices at once. And even though they went ultra-slim, they did not really need to sacrifice any charging performance. You get:
One of my favorite things about Kuxiu has always been their price-to-performance ratio. While some brands and companies are removing charging bricks and even cables, Kuxiu is adding a ton of value here by including everything you need directly in the packaging. The build quality of the charger is also fantastic. It feels balanced, well-made, and extremely versatile because of all the situations you can use it in. 
Kuxiu has also done a great job of bringing us a Burgundy color option that perfectly matches the new Burgundy iPhone color. I wish Apple would continue making the Cosmic Orange iPhone because the K4 Air in that orange would have been awesome.
I recommend watching our video to see my reaction when I pick up the K4 Air for the first time. I was shocked at how small it was. It was thin and lightweight, but still felt solid because it was made of metal and not plastic. The form factor and size make it incredibly easy to throw into any bag, pocket, purse, or even just leave it attached to your iPhone for when it is needed.
The charging experience is actually pretty straightforward. You unfold the charger, connect the USB-C cable, and you’re ready to go. The main magnetic charging surface supports your iPhone, while the second surface is for AirPods. They even added magnets to the AirPods charging pad to ensure they stay in place every single time. There is also a dedicated Apple Watch charger that folds down to charge any Apple Watch. I was charging my Apple Watch Ultra 4, and it fit perfectly.
But now the big question: how well does it work with the iPhone Duo? Personally, I charge my iPhone Duo on my nightstand charger when it is folded shut. So I have zero issues with it. But if you open the iPhone, place it on the charger in landscape mode, and then use it, it will tilt slightly to the left. But to my surprise, if you use the iPhone Duo and open it in portrait mode, it works great! The charger doubles as a stand and easily supports the iPhone Duo. Another cool piece that the form factor unlocks is that it can technically charge two iPhones at the same time, so if you are in a pinch, you can charge two iPhones at once,
So, regardless of which iPhone you are using, as long as it supports MagSafe, you are good to go. But how much does something like this cost?
The Kuxiu K4 Air is finally back in stock after a limited initial run. You can get it on Kuxiu’s website for $79. It comes in Space Gray and their new Burgundy color. As I mentioned above, you also get the charging cable, a 45W charging brick, and a carrying pouch. 
Personally, I am a huge fan of how good the Burgundy color looks.
After using the Kuxiu K4 Air, it is just one of those accessories that makes sense. It is small, compact, lightweight, and extremely useful. It fits perfectly into the travel charger category. There are a ton of great 3-in-1 chargers, with new ones flooding the market daily, but very few combine this level of portability with all the features we discussed. 
So if you are shopping for a portable 3-in-1 travel charger for yourself or as a gift, I would highly recommend giving this one a shot. It’s well-built, charges your big three Apple products, and looks fantastic.

Saturday Night Live opened with host John Mulaney instructing a naive group of tech leaders how to tell whether a woman is genuinely interested in them or is a spy trying to extract valuable information from them, as may be the case with ousted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. 

	Mulaney, as a CIA officer, summons Peter Thiel (James Austin Johnson), Sam Altman (Ben Marshall), Dario Amodei (Jane Wickline), Mark Zuckerberg (Andrew Dismukes) and Elon Musk (Jeremy Culhane) to agency headquarters to brief them on a matter of national security. 

	“America needs you to learn the difference between a woman who would actually want to have sex with you, and a woman who is clearly a spy,” Mulaney says. He then asks the group what the lesson in Huang’s downfall is. 

	“Never date a Chinese woman,” Amodei says. 

	“Never date a human woman,” Zuckerberg answers. 

	“Never date anyone, lest they become the antichrist,” Thiel says.

	Mulaney then tries using a Venn diagram to get his point across. The diagram shows two circles that do not overlap one bit: “beautiful women” and “your interests and opinions.”

	










	“So,” he asks the group, “if someone is both of these things, then they are a what?”

	“An angel,” Amodei replies confidently. 

	When informed that this person would be a spy, Musk goes on one of his unfunny tangents about James Bond, prompting Mulaney to squirt him with a water bottle to get him to shut up.

	A second Venn diagram gets introduced after Zuckerberg says that if a woman approached him, he would interpret it as a sign that she “wanted to sleep with the man who created the Metaverse, as all women do.” This diagram also has “beautiful women” in one circle. And in a much, much smaller circle to the right for “people who like the metaverse,” there is an image of just Zuckerberg.

30,2026-11-15,"Usually, 80th birthdays are pretty staid affairs. A time for looking back. Lots of memories. Taking stock of the past. Going quietly into the twilight years.
Fender turned 80 this year, and apparently didn’t get that memo, because the company ended its celebratory year with a flurry of new series and collaborations.
For starters, there’s the comprehensively updated American Professional range, which re-imagines the brand’s famous line-up of guitars. Then there are collaborations with Pokémon and Godzilla, a signature Jaguar bass from Blink-182’s Mark Hoppus and a signature Jazzmaster from Kim Gordon of Sonic Youth.
The company closed out the year with two big ticket items, both recently launched with much fanfare in Tokyo at the Fender Flagship Store in Harajuku.
The first is a collaboration with the George Harrison estate and Dark Horse Records, the label he started in 1974 following the breakup of The Beatles.
""George played many different iterations of Fender guitars over the space of his career, from the rosewood Telecaster, which of course he played on the rooftop at Apple, to the hand-painted Strat he called Rocky,"" says Edward 'Bud' Cole, Fender CEO. ""We have massive respect for George’s legacy, and now with what Dhani is doing with Dark Horse. We’ve had a collaborative relationship with Dhani for some years now, so this is the logical next step.""
Dhani is Dhani Harrison, George’s son, who now heads Dark Horse Records. Sitting in the Custom Shop room on the third floor of the Fender Flagship store, I have to keep reminding myself not to call him George – it’s uncanny how similar he is in the way he looks, the way he speaks, and his dry sense of humor.
And his relationship with Fender guitars also goes way back. In fact, his first Stratocaster came to him from a guy called Eric.
Surname? Clapton.
""Back in 1991, when I was 13, Eric went on tour with my dad, and he gave our family two of his signature Fender Stratocasters,"" Dhani remembers. ""My dad took the red one, and he gave me one in 7UP green. From that moment I became obsessed with Fender guitars, and Strats in particular. I still play that guitar and I use it on a lot of my records.""
Dhani is a prolific artist in his own right. His recording debut was when he appeared on – and helped Jeff Lynne with the completion of – his father’s posthumous 2002 album Brainwashed.
He formed his own band, thenewno2, in 2006, and kicked off his solo career in 2017 with the album In Parallel.
He’s also very active in the world of soundtracks, working on films (Beautiful Creatures, Cut Throat City, Matangi/Maya/MIA) and TV series (The Divide, Outsiders, The Case Against Adnan Syed). He has guested on albums by artists as diverse as UNKLE and Perry Farrell, and his latest project, Dragonflies, is a collaboration with Radiohead producer Nigel Godrich.
In 2020, Dhani revived the Dark Horse label. His father originally signed up an eclectic roster and was responsible for bringing the music of Ravi Shankar to world notice. The apple does not fall far from the tree. Dhani has signed up a diverse range of artists, including Cat Stevens, Billy Idol, Steve Perry, Benmont Tench (of Tom Petty’s Heartbreakers) and Jon Lord (of Deep Purple), as well as reissuing the work of Billy Preston, Joe Strummer’s post-Clash catalog, and all of his father’s solo work.
For the collaboration with Fender, Dhani wanted to highlight the logo his father used for Dark Horse when he established the company.
""He originally found it on a tin of tea when he visited India,"" he says. ""It’s a seven-headed flying horse in Hindu scripture named Uchchaihshravas. He turns up in a number of different tales, and he’s faster than the wind and is generally a badass.""
Tellingly, the horse is traditionally white, but George made him black, and named his label Dark Horse as an allusion to the fact that he was making his mark as ""the quiet one"" and the often underestimated or overlooked songwriter in The Beatles.
The guitars in the range are available in black, orange and Olympic white, with a small Dark Horse logo on the face, and a large one spread across the back. The hardware is in gold, the body is alder, it has a modern C-shaped neck and three Player Series Alnico 5 Strat single coil pick-ups with a five-way switch. Plus, the neckplate bears an engraving of George’s signature.
There’s also a range of accessories, including guitar picks and straps, and t-shirts and jackets, some of which feature archival photos of George.
At the launch that evening, a couple of hours after our interview, Dhani also spoke about his love of Japan, and why he wanted to do this here.
""Japan is my favorite place,"" he said. ""I first came here when I was 13, and then it took me 30 years to come back again. I don’t know why it took me so long. I was always obsessed with the culture, the language, the anime, the film-making, the literature, the art and the food. Now I come here every year, and I just spent a month traveling around and visiting temples.
""I know Fender has a big presence and history here, and it’s one of their biggest markets, so when we started talking about this collaboration we threw everything into it. So many people come to Japan and take from the culture, but we wanted to give something back, especially to Japanese fans of my father’s music, because they are really loyal.""
Five days after meeting Dhani Harrison, I’m sitting in the same space at the Fender Flagship Store with a knight. Like Fender, he also turned 80 this year.
Sir Paul Smith looks a good 15 years younger than his eight decades, and of course the famous English fashion designer is wearing his own brand – a double-breasted navy-blue suit over a white western style shirt, with his signature striped socks peeking out above black sneakers.
Smith has a long and deep association with music. At the age of 18 he designed a pair of crushed velvet flared pants for Jimmy Page (""I remember he had a 24-inch waist,"" he says with a smile), and the two began an enduring friendship that has lasted over 60 years.
He was also mates with David Bowie.
""David used to call me Smithy,"" he tells me. ""He started out as a customer, but then we got to know each other and became friends. The thing I loved about him was that he was such a curious person and he had an unusual way of looking at the world. He would visit me in my office, which is full of stuff, and he’d pick up a book or an object and say, 'Tell me all about this.'""
There’s a story about Bowie one day walking into Smith’s store in London and buying one of every shirt in his size. Those suits that Bowie, Reeves Gabrels and Tony and Hunt Sales are wearing on the cover of the 1989 Tin Machine debut album? All designed by Paul Smith. And at the end of his life, Bowie asked Smith to design the t-shirt for his final album, Blackstar, which was released two days before his death in January 2016.
Members of The Beatles, with whom he’s also been close, have worn Paul Smith over the years, and he designed a limited-edition cover for the Rolling Stones’ 2023 album Hackney Diamonds.
When Smith appeared on the long-running BBC radio show Desert Island Discs, he said that he could have just chosen Van Morrison songs for his list because he is such a huge fan. He’s also a long-time follower and admirer of Bob Dylan, ""but with both Van and Bob, if they’re in a good mood, the shows are amazing, but if they’re in a grumpy mood, it’s not good.""
He casually mentions that Morrison once bought his house in London.
""I got a call a few days later saying that Van couldn’t get any hot water,"" says Smith. ""I thought, 'Oh, here’s a good chance to go around and meet him.' So I went around and showed him how to switch on the immersion heater and said, 'There you go, you can have a bath now.'""
He can speak at length about seeing Jimi Hendrix play one of his very first shows in England in 1966, or being dumbstruck by seeing Talking Heads in 1977, before they had even released their debut album.
So, as both Fender and Smith were turning 80 in 2026, it seemed a bit of a no-brainer they would collaborate, especially as Smith’s association with Japan is as strong as Fender’s. He has rockstar status here – it accounts for 40% of his sales and is his biggest market.
""I first came here in 1982, and at one point I was coming out four times a year,"" he says. ""And as I’ve worked with so many bands over the years, when this conversation came up with Fender, it just seemed to be such an obvious thing to do it, and to do it here in Tokyo. They’re 80 years old, I’m 80 years old, and they still have such an important name and have kept their standards high.""
He decided to use a Telecaster for his signature guitar ""because it was the original Fender guitar and had its 75th anniversary this year, and of course that’s what Jimmy (Page) used to play.""
Smith is famous for many things when it comes to fashion – popularizing boxer shorts and the Filofax in the ’80s; making his mark with suits that were ""classic with a twist,"" cut smartly and comfortably, but with brightly colored or strikingly patterned linings; and, most famously, for using stripes in many of his designs, which have become his trademark and calling card – and, at one time, an albatross.
""Any logo can become a blessing and a curse,"" he says. ""With the stripes, I became so associated with them that I pulled them at one point. That was a costly, stupid thing to do, so I brought them back but reimagined them.""
Part of that reimagining is bringing his signature style to a number of iconic brands – Mini cars, Anglepoise lamps, Leica cameras, Pinarello bicycles and more. The multi-colored stripes are boldly there on the Made In Japan Paul Smith Telecaster, a rainbow of color positively vibrating against the face and the back of the guitar’s alder body.
The Tele has a maple neck with a rosewood fingerboard and a modern C-shape profile. The pickups are modified, there’s a vintage-style three-saddle bridge and the neckplate bears the unique Paul Smith logo.
There’s also a range of picks and a strap (striped, of course), along with a clothing collection that Smith says, ""is inspired by the 1960s rock posters from Haight-Ashbury and San Francisco and the whole psychedelic summer-of-love era.""
At the launch that evening, Ed 'Bud' Cole presented Smith with a one-of-a-kind Stratocaster, custom-made and hand-painted especially for the designer, incorporating his color palettes and featuring a psychedelic design. It will be added to his archive, a vast collection of objects that reflects his wide interests, from art to vintage cameras to racing bicycles to rabbit figurines.
When I asked about whether he played guitar himself, Smith laughed and said, ""I don’t have a musical bone in my body, even though I’m a huge music fan.""
He joked that he’d leave the music to that other famous Sir Paul, and stick to being a designer.

31,2026-11-15,"The AI job apocalypse may not be what you expect.
Business and technology insights company Gartner projects that AI will lead to 10.6 million new jobs globally between 2025 and 2030, while resulting in a loss of 8.9 million jobs. That adds up to a net benefit of over a million new jobs before the decade is out.
John Lovelock, distinguished vice president analyst and chief of research at Gartner, told Business Insider that what people are really feeling right now is that companies are in wait-and-see mode as the work impacts of AI become clearer.
""Early use of AI just leads people to say, 'You know what? Let's hold off on hiring,'"" Lovelock said. ""Next level up in usage of AI leads to job reductions. And the final use of AI typically leads to job additions — net job additions.""
While the expected job loss would affect less than 2% of workers, the emerging technology is likely to change how a good share of the populace does their jobs. Gartner projects more than 20% of workers, or 435.5 million, would need to adapt to AI effects, including what kind of work they do and how they do it.
""Of the 10 tasks you may be doing today, you may only be doing six of them, and you'll be doing them differently tomorrow,"" Lovelock said.
ZipRecruiter economist Nicole Bachaud thinks AI is adding volatility in tech-related industries in the US, changing roles and skills employers want. Employers may want applicants who are experienced with AI or have great soft skills because AI can do what a role once required.
""As these skills change so rapidly, I think we're going to see more turnover and, as a result, more volatility in professional and business services, in financial activities, and information,"" she told Business Insider.
Outplacement and executive coaching firm Challenger, Gray & Christmas said there have been more than 100,000 AI-related job-cut announcements in the US so far this year as of October. It's the second-most-cited reason, with market and economic conditions taking the top spot.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Workers might be skeptical about AI transforming duties. When Cristina Estupiñán was looking for work after a layoff, she wasn't excited about prospective employers asking about her enthusiasm for AI.
""I have to make up something about what excites me about AI because, frankly, nothing really excites me about AI,"" Estupiñán, who worked in software development, previously told Business Insider.
After more than two decades at Microsoft, Shawn Mrzena decided to take Microsoft's voluntary retirement program, partly because the rapid pace and demand of AI wasn't a technology shift he was excited about as a worker.
""I just decided, 'You know what? I'm going to leave it to the next generation,'"" he said. ""This is not something I want to invest my energy and time into. I'm not waking up excited in the morning.""
Jan Hatzius, chief economist and head of global investment research at Goldman Sachs, said that unemployment will likely remain low next year but there could be upward pressure over the following couple of years from ""more aggressive AI adoption."" He said this would be a temporary headwind and the expectation isn't a jobpocalypse.
Lovelock said industries would be affected differently. He suggested that governments might ""be fixated on the efficiencies and reducing labor force, reducing cost of operations, and therefore more likely to be a loss,"" while service industries could see job increases as a result of a bump to productivity making each additional employee more lucrative.
While we don't know how the next few years will actually pan out, people fear job cuts are coming. Pew Research Center found 76% of Australians and 76% of South Koreans think AI would lead to fewer jobs in their country over the next 20 years, the highest share among the 37 countries surveyed. Nigeria and the Philippines had the lowest shares expecting this outcome, at 26%.
""In a number of countries, concern about AI-related job loss is more common among young adults (those ages 18 to 34) than older adults (those ages 50 and older),"" the Pew Research Center report said. ""This is the case in high-income nations like Canada, France, Singapore and Sweden, as well as in middle-income nations like India, Indonesia and Malaysia.""

32,2026-11-15,"Author of the Small Business Insider newsletter
When Stavya Nilawar isn't in class at Babson College, he's growing his creator marketing agency.
His weekdays follow a disciplined routine: class from 8 to 11:15 a.m., lunch, then a five-hour work block. After dinner, he works for another hour or two.
Nilawar's company connects brands with YouTube creators for sponsored segments and dedicated videos. He's worked with brands including AI companies CodeRabbit and Wispr Flow, as well as on campaigns involving Jack Link's x MrBeast. He earns a cut of the project fee, typically 20%.
He said he spent about $35 to get started — for a website domain and two email inboxes. First, he built a network of creators by manually searching TikTok: ""I would literally doomscroll on TikTok and just go on people's accounts and look at their emails.""
Once he had a roster of 10 to 15 creators, he began pitching brands.
Today, his monthly expenses rarely exceed $1,500, while monthly revenue topped $100,000 for the first time in October. Business Insider reviewed monthly business-account statements to verify his revenue.
""The main expenses are virtual assistants,"" Nilawar said. They cost him about $1,000 a month and help manage his agency's outreach, which can involve sending 200 to 300 emails a day, including follow-ups.
Nilawar, who started the business during a gap semester before college, said these four rules helped him turn roughly $35 into a six-figure business.
Nilawar doesn't buy into the standard advice to follow your passion when choosing a business niche. For him, the business model came first: He knew he wanted to run an agency that connects brands with creators. Then he had to decide which type of brands to serve.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
He chose to work with AI companies.
""I was not passionate about AI at all, to be honest,"" he said. But he saw an opportunity: AI companies were growing quickly, and many had budgets for creator partnerships.
In his view, that's the better order of operations for aspiring founders: Choose a business model you enjoy, then find a niche with clear demand and customers willing to pay.
When Nilawar began exploring influencer marketing, an industry contact helped explain how brands work with creators and how agencies make money. He also learned that 20% was a typical agency cut on a deal.
Since then, he's deliberately sought advice from people further along in the industry, including his current mentor, who runs a similar agency that generates about $10 million a year.
""I would reach out to agency owners who were in the position that I wanted to be in,"" he said. ""I literally asked them what they're doing now in their day-to-day business and how they got to the next level.""
Nilawar's advice: Find someone who has built the type of business you want to build, and ask if they'd be willing to share what they've learned.
In the early days, Nilawar did everything manually: sourcing creators, sending emails, following up with potential clients, and managing campaigns.
""Emails are the main way that we get clients,"" he said. ""If you don't have a system for that, you're not going to do very well in the beginning.""
For Nilawar, reaching the first $10,000 in revenue required volume. He tested different email copy and calls to action, then sent outreach to brands within his chosen niche. Today, he uses virtual assistants and software to scale that work, but outreach is only part of the system. In a crowded agency market, Nilawar said his edge is responsiveness.
What sets him apart from his competitors is ""our relationships and our speed,"" he said. His team aims to respond to clients within two to five minutes.
Early on, Nilawar landed a big campaign involving three creators. Two went smoothly, but the third repeatedly missed deadlines, and when the creator eventually delivered the content, Nilawar said the brand was unhappy with its quality.
The brand wanted to cancel that portion of the deal, leaving Nilawar caught between the brand and the creator's manager. He thought he might have to pay the brand $25,000.
""I didn't have $25K,"" said Nilawar, who was 18 at the time. ""I thought I was screwed.""
The parties ultimately worked out a solution, and the experience taught him that agency work involves more than landing the deal: He also has to manage expectations, maintain communication, and resolve conflicts when a partnership goes sideways.
Rather than viewing that scare as proof he wasn't ready to run a business, Nilawar sees it as a lesson he got early — when the stakes were still manageable.

33,2026-11-15,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Alexander Smith, a content creator based in Austin. His identity and former employment have been verified by Business Insider. The following has been edited for brevity and clarity.
I worked in tech for about eight years at Facebook and Google. I also did internships in college at MSNBC and the White House under President Obama.
I've given people guidance and advice on how to navigate networking, create résumés, and interview. Posting on LinkedIn has allowed me to see both sides of this equation. I've seen how networking has opened doors to opportunities, and I've seen what works because I get a lot of messages, too.
In the age of AI, job cuts, and a lot of competition across industries, networking looks so different than it did 10 or 15 years ago. It's one thing to have a really strong résumé and application. It's another to get that in front of the key decision-makers.
My job at Google came directly from networking, and it helped with Facebook, too. I don't think cold applications are completely dead, and it's worked for me before, but it's a much more competitive job market now, especially in tech. Sometimes highly qualified people aren't getting to the interview stage because there are so many applicants.
People often feel the urgency to network when there is a pressing need for a job. I definitely think you can still have some success if you need a job next week, but it makes it a lot harder.
It's really important to think of networking as a long-term strategy. I want people to view networking similarly to how they view investing in a 401(k). The habits and relationships you develop eventually do pay off, but you have to put time and energy into that. 
There's a lot of potential to build your network, develop it, and build a personal brand without becoming a content creator.
Sometimes, people recognize me when I reach out to them because of my LinkedIn content, and I've also had recruiters from big companies contact me. Building a personal brand has been helpful because it's often a good icebreaker for conversation.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Authenticity always mattered in networking, but it matters even more now that there are so many AI-generated messages and posts on social media.
Some people view networking as stuffy and buttoned-up, but you can connect on a personal level and find common interests. That makes it more enjoyable and less something that you dread doing. For example, I talked to someone a few months ago about Kasey Musgraves and her new album. I also talked with someone who was on a sabbatical about taking time off to travel.
I also think finding a personal connection helps humanize you. You'll stand out more if you find connection points, and it will show that you're really paying attention to them.
If you feel uncomfortable or awkward, that can sometimes be a signal that you may be forcing something that isn't there, or asking too much in the first message.
Networking is really difficult. Even if you're sending strong, well-researched messages. People are busy. People have jobs, families, hobbies, and a million other things to do every single day.
Don't take it personally if you send 20 messages and you only hear back from a few people.
Having empathy and telling myself, ""I know that this person has tons of things on their plate and I'm not taking it personally if I don't get a response,"" has helped me.
Sometimes people gravitate toward those they see consistently on their LinkedIn feeds. For example, you may see the same recruiters consistently posting about jobs, or content creators talking about the job search.
I really encourage people to message those who are not super active on LinkedIn. Many professionals are not active on LinkedIn or posting consistently, and I think people forget that a huge part of your network is not getting an overwhelming number of messages.
Sometimes people also connect with people who aren't relevant to their career path. When I was at Google and Facebook, I had a lot of software engineers and data analysts asking for career advice, even though I was in a non-technical role. I was not the person to give them advice on interview prep.
Also, just because somebody is working at a big company that you want to work at doesn't mean that they are the right person for you. That's where doing your homework on someone can make a big difference.

34,2026-11-15,"""Nine-and-three!Nine-nine!Nine-and-three!"" an auctioneer at Manheim New Jersey chanted rapidly. ""We have a four?"" A split-second later: “Nine-and-four!”
It sounded like gibberish to me. To dealers, it was a call to action.
On Wednesday, I attended the massive used-car auction and heard a blur of dealer names, vehicle models, and bids rising in a flash. Around me, a mix of cars — from beat-up Honda Civics to freshly off-lease BMW X7s — moved through 17 in-person and seven online auction lanes.
Each week, the roughly 350-acre lot, one of 80 Manheim auctions in the US, lists about 4,500 vehicles. Manheim is owned by Cox Automotive.
Used-car auctions offer an early read of auto industry trends. For comparison, it takes years for automakers to design, build, and launch fresh vehicles that respond to car shoppers — but at regional used-car auctions, dealers are buying the used vehicles they think customers will want in a few weeks.
And each car is a several-thousand-dollar bet. Across the US, Manheim says it handles more than 7 million vehicles each year.
So, I got a close-up look at the controlled chaos and choreography it takes to keep one of those 80 lots moving.
Cars arrive at Manheim New Jersey with very different histories. Rental fleets, automakers, dealerships, and banks all send vehicles to its lots in varying states of repair.
During my visit, I spotted everything from a first-generation Ford Edge that must be roughly 15 years old to a Toyota bZ Woodland, which just started arriving at US dealerships months ago.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
And the cars do not simply appear in an auction lane and disappear with the winning dealer. Work starts well before sale day.
Incoming vehicles crawl through Manheim's fixed image tunnels at about 5 mph. The scanners assess details including body panels, tire tread, and the undercarriage for potential issues.
From there, sellers can choose to have vehicles detailed, repainted, repaired, or photographed again before they run through the auction bays. At the New Jersey site, the work can range from touching up a paint scratch to replacing an engine.
Or, if the work is not worth it, they can send a car through as-is.
Manheim says its network employs roughly 1,500 auto technicians and reconditioning experts.
Grace Huang, the president of Cox Automotive Inventory Solutions, told me that gas prices are having an outsize impact on the market.
Right now, national demand for hybrids has spiked — as have their prices. Manheim says a hybrid spends just about 25 days on a dealer lot before it's sold. A healthy days-on-lot average for a used car is typically around 40 days.
Interest in EVs has a nearly one-for-one correlation with gas prices, Huang added. If the price of gas goes up, so too does interest in EVs.
Overall, Manheim says used-vehicle retail prices were up 4% through October this year, even as used retail sales fell by 2%. And the wholesale value of non-EVs rated above 40 mpg rose 9.9% from January, while those below 15 mpg lost 15% of their value.
And Alan Wang, senior vice president of physical services and auctions, told me that Manheim is also privy to trends in auto theft. Recently, they've seen an uptick in criminal activity around BMWs and Cadillac Escalades.
Their lots are surrounded by military-grade fences. While we were driving around the massive lot, someone asked the driver what would happen if he slammed our Ford Transit into a barricade.
""The engine would go into the backseat,"" he said.
Manheim has an old-timey feel to it — and that's aided by those fast-talking auctioneers. Apparently, they're not going away anytime soon.
Huang said there is technology that could handle the basic mechanics of bidding, but it has not replicated an auctioneer's ability to turn a sale into a personal challenge for a dealer.
""Our auctioneers are really good at saying, 'Dealer A, I know you want this Jeep,'"" she said.
Some of those auctioneers use the same talents elsewhere, too. Huang said many also work cattle auctions when they are not hawking cars.
Before the pandemic, about 30% of Manheim New Jersey's sales happened online, the company said. When COVID pushed dealers out of the auction lanes, Manheim went fully digital. Auctioneers still stood in person and called the sales — wearing PPE — while dealers placed bids through the company's app.
Much of the business has stayed online. Manheim says roughly 70% of purchases now come from digital buyers.
""That's why we could have you here,"" Huang told me. ""Before the pandemic, it was too jam-packed to have anyone come visit. The whole auction was packed shoulder-to-shoulder.""
Jump to"

Did he know something we didn’t?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia Board Director Mark Stevens sold about 4.2 million of his shares, netting him around $1 billion in cash, almost exactly two months before Jensen Huang was accused of having “close ties” to an alleged Chinese spy. According to Moneywise.com, Stevens started the sale from August 31 to September 2, when Nvidia stock hovered between $220 and $226, which made him $411 million. The next tranche, totaling $236 million, came in the next couple of days as Nvidia traded from $227 to $234. The final sale happened on September 18 at around $220 per share, making Stevens approximately $300 million. These three transactions would have made him somewhere around $947 million.
Last year, Jensen Huang sold up to six million Nvidia shares, netting him around $925 million. However, unlike Stevens, who had no plan, this move was announced six months earlier in a Rule 10b5-1 trading plan to help avoid accusations of insider trading.
Nvidia’s stock price has taken a beating since the scandal broke, with the company trading at $162.85 at the time of writing, its lowest level in the past 12 months. When the story first broke at the end of October, Nvidia shares were at around $205. It then started sliding, with the price at $178.67 after Jensen admitted to the affair last Sunday, although he did not name the involved party.
A second affair was revealed a few days after the former Nvidia chief’s confession, leading to his resignation from the company a few hours after that. The news sent Nvidia stock down to $151.09 — a nearly 25% drop from before the scandal. It recovered somewhat after the initial shock, climbing to nearly $170 before Friday’s trading, but took a beating again soon after the market opened.
Despite the sale, Stevens still holds about 27.5 million Nvidia shares, making him the second-largest shareholder after Jensen and Lori Huang. The price difference between today and September 18 is around $57, meaning he would have lost 25% of the value if he had sold after the scandal broke. Furthermore, his remaining Nvidia shares have lost more than $1.5 billion in value in just one quarter. Still, selling one’s holdings in a company as a board director does not constitute a crime. After all, it's unlikely that one could have predicted how hard Jensen Huang would fall in such a short time.

Looking at the official 2022 census data for the USA (the most recent ones I found in a quick search), we have:
1) Total population: 333’287’557.
2) Population of agricultural producers (computed from the 2022 agricultural demographic report): 3’519’511.
3) Amongst those producers, about 42% have agriculture as their main occupation, 58% another activity as their main occupation. The farms are family farms in 92% to 96% of the cases.
4) Let us assume that the producers are couples. Since these are family farms, and more than half the producers are not primarily occupied in agriculture, the assumption should be a good approximation of the situation.
5) The census statistics for 2022 give the average size of a family household in the USA, including non-family members: 3.13. The total number of producers results in a total number of population in farm households of 5’508’035.
Conclusion: according to that computation, the share of the population living in farms is estimated at 1.6526% (1.056% if counting only the producers). Claude’s assertion that it is under 2% is correct, but actually it should be correctly stated as “significantly under 2%”.
You are free to delve into the census figures to arrive at more accurate statistics, but this should be enough at this point.
Let me add that if one considers family households including non-family members (average size overall 3.2), the estimate is marginally different at 1.6896% (very few households live with non-family members, apparently). Contrarily to what I stated above, the figure of 1.6526% corresponds to households with only family members.

    In 2021, House and Senate members sought to push legislation that would have addressed some of the market power problems that subcommittee uncovered (and that we’re now dealing with in the form of giant AI monsters.) The bills involved things like antitrust as it applies to platforms, interoperability, app stores, advertising technology, basically the underpinnings of the big tech monopoly profits strangling our economy. Some of the legislation was great, some was poorly drafted, but it had genuine political support from 80% of Dems and 20% of Rs.
    The legislation all passed out of the Judiciary Committee, much of it carried by @amyklobuchar. Schumer leaked to Axios that it would get a vote on the Senate floor in the summer of 2022. A lot of Senators would have supported it if forced to take a vote, but they didn’t want to have to do that because they didn’t want to anger powerful interests. However, pushing a floor vote is what you do if you are interested in making Congress a functional body to address social problems.
    What did Schumer do? Well, Schumer told Klobuchar that to get a vote, she needed to get all the Democrats publicly on board. And Klobuchar couldn’t, because that was impossible. He knew that would kill the bill. And coincidentally, Schumer then went to Seattle and raised dark money from Microsoft and other tech people for the 2022 election.

Statement from. On Friday’s Groundstop by the FAA union (or the remainingworkers DOGE didn’t decimate) h/t OMAAT
PASS On the Nationwide Ground Stop
Yesterday, the U.S. air traffic control system experienced only the second nationwide ground stop since the terrorist attacks of September 11, 2001. PASS is grateful to the dedicated federal employees it represents at the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) who worked tirelessly to assess the situation and help get to the root of the problem. They participated in multiple phone conferences and were testing the systems to get them back online and planes back in the air as quickly and safely as possible.
The issue was isolated to two contractor owned and maintained routers that were being updated mid-day. Historically contractors have followed a protocol where FAA employees conduct a risk assessment and coordinate the maintenance of the system or equipment. PASS-represented technicians were not involved in the upgrades that led to the ground stop yesterday. [Emphasis added] 
PASS fully supports the administration’s efforts to modernize the nation’s air traffic control system. It must be recognized by the FAA that modernization will move more quickly with fewer disruptions if PASS is fully engaged.The employees represented by the union are on the front lines and they have the skills and expertise to make the Brand New Air Traffic Control System (BNATCS) a reality. These employees should receive training on new equipment and could ensure more robust installation and optimization—if the workforce were properly staffed.
The FAA acknowledges that the technician workforce is understaffed and released a workforce plan in May 2026 calling for an increase of at least 750 additional certified professional technicians (CPTs) by 2030. Additionally, the plan creates a process to hire new technicians in advance of retiring CPTs to ensure the FAA isn’t losing experience and expertise while increasing the workload of those who remain. However, little progress has been made on hiring those new technicians.
PASS and the public servants it represents are ready, willing and able to modernize the air traffic control system in the safest and most efficient manner.
Why should the FAA own, install and maintain critical infrastructure with their own engineers and technicians when it can be outsourced? Reinventing Government indeed!
Those routers must have been two honking hunks of electronics to take out the entire continental USA, just between the two of them. 
Or I just don’t understand.
Think of it like the 2003 blackout in the US northeast.
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_blackout_of_2003#Voluntary_Blackout_Day_commemorations
“The blackout was due to a software bug in the alarm system at the control room of FirstEnergy, which rendered operators unaware of the need to redistribute load after overloaded transmission lines dropped in voltage. What should have been a manageable local blackout cascaded into the collapse of much of the Northeast regional electricity distribution system.”
Or the 1997 NYC blackout caused by a cascading effect of small tech failures that overloaded the entire system.
The point is that seemingly small tech failures can cascade into an entire system.
This is just my guess, of course, about why two routers could hypothetically cascade their errors into an entire system. I think that if the FAA techs had tested these devices and tested the upgrades before installing this might have been prevented.  To be ridiculously obvious about it: SW is not like Lego blocks.  SW installed on a complex system has to interact with everything else on that system.  Everything else.  FAA techs will be aware of the “everything else.”  Private contractors may only be aware of their own product and make unwarranted assumptions about the rest of “everything else” in a customer’s system.  I’ve seen it happen, on a very small scale of course.

My experience with public agency infrastructure builds is the infrastructure is built to a specification and approved by the agency or the engineering firm they employed to write the spec. The contractor that is awarded the job then owns the equipment being installed until it is bought off by the agency.
That said, when the personnel who actually use the stuff take over, they are often disappointed no one asked for their input in the process because it is usually lacking practicality in some areas and overbuilt in others. In any case there is always testing and integration.

The “Bank of Nvidia” (now helmed by interim CEO Colette Kress) reportedly has $164.5 billion in total financial commitments. Not exactly chump change.
Of that 164.5 billion, 105 billion is a “residual value guarantee” on OpenAI’s Ohio Campus. It seems that if OpenAI defaults on its twenty-year leases, Nvidia must cover the shortfall between the guaranteed minimum lease value and what a re-lease or sale recovers. Next, there are $36 billion in commitments over six years to buy unsold GPU compute capacity from specialized AI cloud providers. And there is also $3.5 billion in data center shell and power leases for some AI cloud providers, alongside roughly $20 billion in direct lease commitments.

I'm ranking Xbox controllers once again, and it's about to get messy.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Back in 2023, I took on the impossible task of ranking the Top 10 Xbox Special Edition controllers of all time. Three years later, as we celebrate Xbox's massive 25th Anniversary, it's officially time to revisit that countdown. In the past 3 years, Xbox have released some absolute bangers that deserve a mention, and maybe even dethrone some of my original picks. Microsoft might still refuse point-blank to update its first-party controller tech with some Hall Effect thumbsticks to save us from stick drift, but at least they are holding it down in the aesthetics department. Nobody does limited-edition hardware quite like Xbox. I'm not relying totally on my own personal tastes here, I went to our readers on social media to see which controllers deserved a spot and there were a few that were mentioned passionately multiple times, so some classics have been swiftly kicked out for fresh blood. Without further ado, here's Windows Central's definitive top 10 Xbox Special Edition Controllers of all time.
View Original
View Original
View Original
View Original
The Cyberpunk 2077 controller holds steady at #10, because... well, just look at it. It's still one of the best examples of an industrial, battle-scarred design with the real dents and scratches all over the body of the controller. Cyberpunk 2077 has come such a long way since launch too, with its Phantom Liberty update and now being available on Battle.net as part of a new ongoing partnership between CD Projekt Red and Blizzard.
The controller was part of a Limited Edition Xbox One console bundle, but it was also sold separately.
View Original

View Original
In 2023, the Minecraft controllers didn't quite crack my top 10 and I had to settle for a special mention. Three years later, given the sheer cultural force and scale of the Minecraft franchise, I'm promoting them to the 9th spot. Released in 2017, I'm absolutely mystified as to why Xbox haven't launched new versions of these. The Minecraft movie was incredibly successful, and even Razer have been pumping out Minecraft-themed gaming keyboards. The pink Minecraft pig controller in particular is so sought after it is going for a small fortune on Ebay. The closest we've got to a new Minecraft pad was this PDP Realmz translucent number with a suspended minifig in the grip, but I'm begging, pleading with Xbox to bring back the OG Creeper and Pig in Series X|S or even Elite versions. With the success of the movie, they will absolutely print money.

Our previous list featured the bright yellow-and-pink paint-splashed Forza Horizon 5 controller, but it's officially been overtaken on the track by the Forza Horizon 6 limited edition. Digital content creator JustMeeVicky on Instagram and Twitter, who puts together some incredible images of various gaming accessories, took some awesome photos of this controller at one of the Forza launch events this year. The cyan shell, bright pink thumbsticks and the addition of the winding Touge mountain roads of Japan make this controller a real standout in Xbox's repertoire, and I'm surprised you can actually still buy this, on sale for Black Friday right now, no less!
The classic Xbox Series X|S controller in a special edition colorway for Forza Horizon 6 fans, and if you really want to go all out, 8BitDo have a matching charging dock.

The Halo 4 Edition controller is legendary with its blue-black hues and transparent faceplate, and unfortunately we didn't get an updated Halo Special Edition for Halo: Campaign Evolved. Xbox, what gives? Our Richard Devine did go full nostalgia bomb by playing the game with a Hyperkin Duke though, so there's always that option. The Xbox 360 had many wild and wonderful special edition controllers, but some of them look tacky by today's standards. The Halo 4 Edition, however, has a timeless appeal and still looks cool as hell today.

The DOOM: The Dark Ages controller is Xbox at its finest, balls-to-the-wall design going all in on the game theme with its Slayer-armor green, actual 3D silver helmet spikes along the top, blood splatter across the left grip, Sentinel rune ABXY buttons, and brown leather-like grip.

In fact, Xbox knocked it out of the park these last few years with the translucent Cipher series, which gave us Blue, Red and a Ghost variant, all of which would be worth a spot here, but we were all clamouring for the green, which Xbox savvily waited until the 25th Anniversary to give us. 39,2026-11-15,"By Thursday night, a day after 1321 Flatbush Avenue, a three-story building in Brooklyn, collapsed, the site was strictly a salvage job. Rain poured down and lightning flashed as a demolition crew used an enormous yellow excavator to hoist debris into red dumpsters. The excavator took giant bites of the pile, pulling up gnarls of sheet metal, insulation, and electric wiring. Poking out from the top of one dumpster: a Dunkin’ bag, an empty Styrofoam Cup Noodles, a can of Celsius. A few local television reporters were huddled up across the street, the lights of their cameras trained at the disaster area, but the F.D.N.Y. was already rolling up soggy tents. In Maribel’s, a hair salon across from the site, women craned their necks to watch the television above the door intently, gazing at news footage from the scene just outside.
The LedeReporting and commentary on what you need to know today.
In the steady rain, lit up by N.Y.P.D. spotlights, the hushed site could have been a movie set—someone’s idea of a New York City calamity. “It looks like 9/11,” Novella Spence, a witness to Wednesday’s tragedy, told Curbed. “There was mist from the crumble, and the side of the building was gone.” 1321 Flatbush Avenue, which had stood since 1930, most recently with a façade of dusty white brick, housed a medical-uniform-and-supply store on its first floor and two floors of apartments above. After nearly being crushed by the house-slide above them, construction workers, who had been digging the foundation of a new mixed-use development going up next door, immediately began combing through the wreckage for survivors. They were soon joined by firefighters, E.M.S., and personnel from several other city agencies, in addition to K-9 dogs from both the N.Y.P.D. and the F.D.N.Y. “They looking for bodies, they looking for more bodies,” a d.j. named Lexx Emmanuel said in a video that he posted to Instagram on Wednesday night. In the video, shot from a high floor in a building directly overlooking the disaster area, dozens of first responders can be seen scurrying below. At least one miracle occurred: a pregnant woman was pulled from the rubble alive.
As a result of the collapse, eight people died—including a nine-year-old girl and a ten-year-old boy—and nearly twenty people were injured, including four firefighters. The hope of additional rescues was dashed on Thursday afternoon when heavy rains began. “Rain can flood those voids where they are still searching and destabilize the ground they are on,” Laura Kavanagh, a former F.D.N.Y. commissioner, said; she explained that the death toll was likely so high because people were home from work and school on Veterans Day. “Before all this happened, we used to feel shakes in our building, like, it used to start shaking when they was working,” Zariah Coleman, a teen-age resident of the adjacent building, told reporters at a press conference on Friday, in tears. “I just really hope they come and get our cat, ’cause he’s inside the house right now, and they said they went inside the house to try and find him, but I don’t think they really did.”
We are in something of an era of building collapses in New York City—just this past July, the former midtown offices of Pfizer partially caved in when columns buckled during an office-to-residential renovation. In April, 2023, a concrete parking garage near City Hall pancaked, killing one and injuring seven. Three years before that, a vacant old tenement building crumbled near the entrance to the Queens-Midtown Tunnel. “These buildings that have been around since the early nineteen-hundreds, they’re beautiful and they have character and charm in many regards,” Melanie La Rocca, a former commissioner of the city’s Department of Buildings, told me. “What’s the old saying? They don’t make them like they used to,” La Rocca said. “Who knows what people thought back then?”
Many of New York City’s old row buildings have been standing for longer than anyone who constructed them could have imagined; many have been poorly maintained. “How do we create an environment where the housing stock can and is prepared to exist for the next hundred years?” La Rocca, now the chief operating officer of Riseboro, a large Brooklyn community-services provider, said. Close your eyes and imagine New York City in a hundred years. Are the nineteenth- and twentieth-century row houses and tenements still standing? Is that a good or a bad thing?
Mayor Zohran Mamdani and other city officials have vowed a full investigation of what happened. “There are no words for a loss of this magnitude,” Mamdani posted on X. Olufemi Falade, the landlord of 1321 Flatbush Avenue, is a notorious slumlord and a former New York State Department of Environmental Conservation engineer. He was once sent to prison for twelve days for refusing to fix eighteen hundred violations in twelve buildings he owned. “How can a landlord fix if the tenant does not pay rent?” Falade told the Village Voice, in 2006—echoing a complaint that many landlords of the city’s rent-stabilized apartment buildings have voiced in the ongoing debate about Mamdani’s rent freeze.
The construction site next door to 1321 Flatbush Avenue is owned, meanwhile, by an L.L.C. tied to developers, including Robert Fischman and Judy Swimer, known for “cutting corners” at the site, according to Gothamist. The Department of Buildings “had previously issued the stop work order at the lot during demolition of an existing one-story building in March after determining that contractors had failed to install required safety precautions,” the website reported. An engineering firm recommended “extensive excavation and bracing work to prevent neighboring buildings from collapsing, including steel, timber and concrete shoring,” and it is unclear if those precautions were taken. The developers had initially said that they wanted to build a three-story structure on the site but had recently told the city that they were now aiming higher: a nine-story building.
Gentrification often exacerbates economic, political, and social imbalances; sometimes it contributes to structural imbalances, too. Much of the Flatbush section of Brooklyn has long been known as Little Haiti, for the immigrant community that put down roots in the area beginning in the nineteen-sixties. More recently, glass-and-steel apartment towers have been going up, as have the neighborhood’s prices and rents. “They were digging down,” Ahmed Tigani, the current commissioner of the Department of Buildings, told reporters at a press conference on Wednesday, when asked what the workers on the construction site were doing when the collapse occurred. (Neither Falade nor the developers of the construction site could be reached for comment.)
The makeup of Little Haiti is changing in other ways, too. Mamdani noted that the victims at 1321 Flatbush Avenue were mostly from South and Central American countries, including Mexico, Ecuador, and Colombia—in what once had been a cradle of Caribbean communities in the city. Two of the dead were Guinean immigrants from West Africa, like many who arrived in great numbers during the city’s “migrant crisis” in 2023, which the then mayor, Eric Adams, warned could “destroy the city.” But, of course, when the crisis ended, New York was still standing. Many of the new arrivals whom Adams so bemoaned wound up living, as new arrivals in New York have for centuries, in the kind of cramped and dilapidated housing that poses more of a threat to them than they ever will to the city.
Officials cautioned, as they always do, that the investigations will take time. A ballroom rises in Washington, the slums fall in Flatbush, who can blame people for not waiting patiently for the system to render judgment? When 1321 Flatbush Avenue collapsed, it took its remaining party wall with it, exposing the apartments next door to the world like an ant farm: clothes still hanging in closets, furniture carefully arranged. “I lost everything,” one resident said, after emerging from the building with a neighbor’s child in his arms. “My computer, my clothes.” By the weekend, traffic on Flatbush Avenue was open again. ♦

40,2026-11-15,"Judge Noël Wise found buyers plausibly alleged the chipmakers conspired to cut conventional DRAM supply and shift capacity to HBM, distinguishing the Ninth Circuit's DRAM II.
A federal judge on Nov. 14 refused to dismiss a proposed class action accusing Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron of conspiring to restrict the supply of conventional dynamic random-access memory, or DRAM, the standardized memory chips used in computers, phones and servers.
U.S. District Judge Noël Wise of the Northern District of California denied the companies' joint motion to dismiss in full, finding the complaint plausibly alleged an agreement in violation of Section 1 of the Sherman Act. The allegations, accepted as true at this stage, have not been proven.
The companies argued the case was a rerun of DRAM II, in which the Ninth Circuit in 2022 affirmed dismissal of claims against the same three companies over a 2016–2018 supply restriction. ""The Court finds that it is not,"" Judge Wise wrote.
The suit was filed June 25, 2026, by 17 consumers and small businesses from five states. It concerns conventional DRAM, which the complaint describes as ""standardized memory built to industry specifications and sold for ordinary purposes,"" including DDR5 generation modules built for computers.
According to the complaint, the three companies controlled more than 91% of global DRAM revenue as of the third quarter of 2025. They allegedly cut production starting in late 2022 and, beginning in 2023, shifted capacity to high bandwidth memory, or HBM, a specialized DRAM product for artificial intelligence accelerators that ""requires approximately three to four times the silicon wafer area per gigabyte compared to conventional DDR5 DRAM.""
By the third quarter of 2025, conventional DRAM prices had risen 171.8% in one year, according to the complaint, which alleges a 697% increase over 18 months. Yet ""not one of the three Defendants expanded or announced plans to meaningfully expand commodity DRAM production capacity,"" the complaint alleges.
Without direct evidence of an agreement, the plaintiffs needed plus factors, additional facts suggesting a preceding agreement rather than conscious parallelism, in which competitors independently make similar moves.
Judge Wise rejected the companies' argument that timing gaps undercut parallel action. SK Hynix and Micron announced cuts three weeks apart in late 2022, she noted, and Samsung's lag of about four and a half months, until April 7, 2023, was ""not such an unreasonably long delay that it defeats an allegation of parallel conduct on a motion to dismiss.""
She found the complaint plausibly alleged that the three companies acted in parallel when they cut supply, shifted production from conventional DRAM such as DDR5 to HBM, and wound down DDR4.
Under Ninth Circuit precedent, action against self-interest must be ""so perilous in the absence of advance agreement that no reasonable firm would make the challenged move without such an agreement."" Judge Wise wrote that a pivot to HBM by itself ""might not be sufficient,"" but that ""the related conduct tips this factor over the edge to weigh in Plaintiffs' favor.""
According to the complaint, all three signed long-term contracts locking in HBM supply, Samsung and SK Hynix reportedly refused long-term agreements with major conventional DRAM customers, and Micron announced on Dec. 3, 2025, that it would close Crucial, its 30-year-old consumer brand. Those moves, which she called ""difficult if not impossible to unwind,"" plus the failure to expand despite record prices, ""are the sort that at the pleading stage, it appears no reasonable firm would make without a prior agreement,"" she wrote.
She applied similar reasoning to allegations that Samsung and Micron delayed or halted new DRAM plants, citing the Ninth Circuit's 1990 Petroleum Products decision that ""Changes in refinery capacity are not readily reversible."" ""The Court views the commitment to build or not build manufacturing capacity as analogous,"" she wrote. She also counted the companies' alleged same-quarter 2025 decisions to wind down DDR4 as a plus factor, citing the complaint's allegation that ""older, slower DDR4 came to cost more per gigabyte than newer, faster DDR5.""
Judge Wise found that near-identical customer-vetting practices all three companies allegedly adopted in January 2026 ""strongly support a plausible inference of conspiracy."" She rejected the companies' ""obvious and non-conspiratorial explanation"" of booming AI demand, noting that the vetting began after the AI boom and the HBM pivot were underway.
The court gave no weight to three other arguments. Judge Wise held that investor statements, including Micron's 2022 reference to ""supply discipline,"" did not distinguish the case from DRAM II. Though the conference-meeting allegations went beyond those in DRAM II, she wrote that DRAM II ""seemingly all but forecloses a trial court from giving any weight to trade conference meetings"" absent specifics, and she called the market's oligopolistic structure ""neutral in the analysis.""
According to the complaint, the companies and two other manufacturers formed a criminal cartel to fix DRAM prices from 1998 to 2002; Samsung paid a $300 million fine, Hynix paid $185 million, and Micron received immunity for cooperating as a whistleblower. Judge Wise, like the DRAM II court, rejected the argument that the now-20-year-old history should not count.
At the hearing, the companies quoted the late Judge Posner's line that competitors in concentrated markets ""watch each other like hawks."" Judge Wise wrote: ""But Plaintiffs' allegations go beyond hawks watching each other; their contentions are more akin to hawks flying in formation – an activity that hawks ordinarily do not do.""
The plaintiffs are represented by Bathaee Dunne LLP. Samsung is represented by O'Melveny & Myers LLP, SK Hynix by Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton LLP, and Micron by Gibson, Dunn & Crutcher LLP.
Judge Wise ordered the companies to answer by Dec. 7, 2026. Because the court had reset argument by about three weeks, she also granted the companies' unopposed administrative motion to continue early case deadlines and reset the initial case management conference for Jan. 12, 2027, at 9 a.m.
If the document does not display, use Open PDF or Download PDF.
Marc Garciaguirre, et al. v. Samsung Electronics Co., Ltd., et al.
