Shipments of data center CPUs set records.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Shipments of x86 CPUs declined quarter-over-quarter and year-over-year in the third quarter as demand for client processors dropped sharply due to high component prices, while server CPU unit sales grew by a whopping 20% year-over-year, according to the latest report from Mercury Research. Intel regained overall CPU market share from AMD for the first time since Q2 2025, largely due to surprisingly strong performance in desktop PCs. Meanwhile, AMD continued to set new records in the mobile and server CPU segments, the report claims. In total, the industry supplied 68.93 million x86 CPUs, Jon Peddie Research reports.

The overall CPU market contracted both sequentially and year-over-year in unit shipments in Q3 2026, after a much stronger-than-expected second quarter, according to Mercury Research. AMD and Intel shipped 60.8 million client CPUs and 8.13 million data center processors during the quarter. One thing to keep in mind about the reports from JPR and Mercury Research is that they count CPU shipments, which happen months before those CPUs are sold in actual systems.
""After a far better than expected second quarter market for client processors, the third quarter PC CPU market declined far more than expected, entirely due to a downturn in mobile CPUs — this was the same segment that was unexpectedly higher last quarter, hinting that part of the downturn may have been due to CPU demand pulling in sales from the third quarter,"" said Dean McCarron, principal analyst at Mercury Research.
""Both Intel and AMD indicated in their earnings results that there was a rapid softening in PC demand late in the third quarter that impacted client processor shipments and that the outlook for client CPUs in the fourth quarter will be a decline in excess of 20%. Unlike client CPUs, however, server processors continued to grow strongly in the past quarter, as did embedded CPUs.""
 



Quarter over quarter, the decline was driven primarily by significantly lower demand for mobile processors, as 86 notebook CPU shipments dropped by more than 10%. By contrast, desktop CPU shipments recovered, largely thanks to Intel, while server and embedded CPU shipments continued to grow strongly. Excluding IoT, semi-custom SoCs, and embedded products, both AMD and Intel shipped fewer x86 processors than in the previous quarter, though AMD's decline was larger than Intel's.
Compared with the third quarter of 2025, CPU shipments declined across all major market segments except servers. Desktop and notebook CPU shipments decreased due to weaker PC demand amid rising component costs, particularly memory prices. Embedded processor and semi-custom SoC shipments also declined, primarily due to AMD's shrinking game console business. By contrast, fueled by the ongoing buildout of AI infrastructure, server CPU shipments increased by more than 20% YoY, their highest annual growth rate in nearly five years.
""Client CPU shipments softened in Q3 as OEMs kept inventories lean and the DIY channel remained weak,"" said Dr. Jon Peddie, president of Jon Peddie Research. ""Server CPUs continued growing, with AMD gaining share and AI-related demand supporting the market. We expect PC conditions to remain difficult into the first half of 2027.""
Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.
Due to weakness in the client PC space, AMD's overall x86 market share (excluding embedded and semi-custom SoC) declined by 0.6% QoQ for the first time in years, but still grew by a significant 4.5% YoY, according to Mercury Research.
In the x86 client PC segment, Intel regained some ground on AMD in Q3 2026 as overall client CPU shipments declined more sharply than expected, according to both Jon Peddie Research and Mercury Research. JPR estimates that AMD and Intel shipped 60.8 million client processors in Q3 2026, down from 62.93 million a quarter before and roughly 65 million in Q3 2025.
 



AMD's client CPU unit share dropped to 29.3%, down from a record 30.3% in Q2, but still notably higher than the 25.4% it commanded a year earlier. Meanwhile, Intel increased its share to 70.7%, though its position remained considerably weaker than in Q3 2025, when it controlled 74.6% of the market.
Intel's sequential market share gains were driven entirely by a substantial increase in desktop CPU shipments, while AMD continued to gain ground in notebooks, albeit at a much slower pace than in previous quarters. Mercury Research believes that weaker demand for mobile CPUs may have enabled Intel to allocate more silicon to desktop processors, a market that the company had undersupplied for some time.
However, the outlook for both companies' client CPU businesses is rather gloomy, as rising component costs (especially DRAM and 3D NAND prices) are weighing on PC demand. Both AMD and Intel anticipate a sequential decline in client CPU shipments of more than 20% in the fourth quarter and further declines in the year to come. Meanwhile, one has to keep in mind that CPUs sold in Q4 are meant for systems to be shipped in Q1, which is traditionally weak. Hence, while the 20% YoY decline is extraordinary, a substantial part of that decline is seasonal.
In the desktop PC segment, Intel made a spectacular comeback in Q3 2026, reclaiming 5% of the market in one quarter. Intel's desktop CPU unit share climbed to 70.1%, up 5% sequentially and 3.7% in the same quarter a year ago. By contrast, AMD's share dropped to 29.9%, down from 34.9% in Q2 2026 and 33.6% in Q3 2025, meaning the company wiped out its market share gains over the past year.
 



Mercury Research speculates that weaker demand for notebook processors enabled Intel to allocate more silicon to mainstream desktop CPUs, a market that both companies have underserved for some time. Another factor behind AMD's poor performance was its greater exposure to the gaming PC market, which suffered from limited availability of gaming graphics cards and unusually high costs for other components, such as memory modules and SSDs.
Yet, desktops became the only major x86 CPU market segment where AMD lost unit share both sequentially and year-over-year.
In the mobile PC segment, both AMD and Intel saw a substantial decline in CPU shipments in Q3 2026 as the market contracted more than 10% sequentially, according to Mercury Research. However, AMD's shipments declined slightly less than Intel's, which enabled the company to increase its mobile CPU unit share to a record 29.2%, up from 28.9% in Q2 2026 and 21.9% in Q3 2025. Intel remained the dominant supplier with a 70.8% share, down from 71.1% in the previous quarter and 78.1% a year earlier.
 



Although AMD's sequential market share gain of 0.2% was negligible, its 7.2% YoY gain was impressive, especially given Intel's historically strong position in the notebook market. Mercury Research attributes the sharp sequential decline in mobile CPU shipments partly to PC makers pulling forward their CPU procurements into the unusually strong second quarter.
Unfortunately for both AMD and Intel, the outlook for the fourth quarter is gloomy, as rising memory and other component prices drive up notebook prices and reduce end-user demand.
Unlike the bleak client CPU market, server CPUs are thriving, with shipments of data center processors reaching 8.13 million units in Q3 2026, according to Jon Peddie Research. The market again grew 20% year-over-year, the highest annual growth rate in nearly five years, according to Mercury Research.
In the server CPU segment, AMD continued its winning spree as its unit share reached a record 35.5%, up from 34.5% in Q2 2026 and 27.8% in Q3 2025. Intel remained the dominant supplier with a 64.5% share, down 1% sequentially and 7.7% year-over-year.
 



Interestingly, Intel managed to slow AMD's advance in the core data center CPU market by apparently substantially increasing shipments of its Xeon SP processors, McCarron believes. When the comparison is limited to AMD's EPYC and Intel's Xeon SP processors, AMD's unit share reached 46.8%, up just 0.4% sequentially, which represents AMD's smallest sequential gain over the past year, as the company's quarterly share gains by this metric averaged 3% over the preceding three quarters.
Unlike the client CPU market, the outlook for the server processor market remains particularly favorable for both AMD and Intel. The ongoing buildout of AI infrastructure drives demand not only for conventional data center-grade processors, but also for workstation-class CPUs used in smaller departmental AI servers.
Shipments of x86 CPUs declined both sequentially and year-over-year in Q3 2026 to 68.93 million units as rising component prices weighed on client PC demand, while server CPU shipments grew more than 20% year-over-year.
Intel regained overall x86 CPU market share from AMD for the first time since Q2 2025, largely thanks to a spectacular comeback in the desktop CPU segment, where its share reached 70.1%. Meanwhile, AMD gained 0.3% of the mobile CPU market, now commanding 29.2%, its highest share ever. Furthermore, AMD reached a record unit share in the server CPU segment, where it now commands 35.5%.
Both companies expect client CPU shipments to drop by more than 20% sequentially in Q4, whereas demand for server processors continues to grow due to the ongoing buildout of AI infrastructure.

Nvidia’s board announced that Jensen Huang had resigned 33 years after he co-founded the company.
What happens to a company when a visionary CEO is gone? Most often innovation dies and the company coasts for years on momentum and its brand. Rarely does it regain its former glory.
That’s the danger facing Nvidia. Here’s what their board should be considering.
Here’s why.
Jensen Huang reinvented Nvidia three times. In 2006 he launched CUDA, turning a graphics chip for PC gamers into a general-purpose computing platform – and funded it for years ahead of any revenue. In 2012, when researchers used Nvidia chips to win the ImageNet contest, he pointed the company to deep learning. In 2020, with the acquisition of Mellanox, Nvidia went from selling boards with GPUs to selling integrated racks that power AI datacenters. 
These were not just product transitions, but radical business model transitions; new channels, new customers and new markets. Nvidia pivoted from a vendor of graphics boards, to a software platform, and then to systems company. It’s hard to imagine a CEO focused on executing an existing business model funding CUDA through those years.

Steve Jobs did the same at Apple, transforming it from a niche computer company into the most profitable company in the world. Between 2001 and 2008, Jobs reinvented the company three times.
Visionary CEOs are not “just” great at assuring world-class execution of a successful business model, they are also world-class innovators.
The best are agile and know how to pivot, making substantive changes to their business model while or before their market has shifted. The very best of them shape markets – they know how to create new markets by seeing opportunities before anyone else. They remain entrepreneurs.

Visionary CEOs don’t need someone else to demo the company’s key products for them. They deeply understand products, and they have their own coherent and consistent vision of where the industry/business models and customers are today, and where they need to take the company. They know who their customers are because they spend time talking to them. They use strategy committees and the exec staff for advice, but none of these CEOs pivot by committee.
Microsoft and Apple are two examples of what happens when a visionary CEO is replaced by an execution CEO.
Microsoft and the Cost of Missing the Next Market
Microsoft entered the 21st century as the dominant software provider for anyone who interacted with a computing device. After running Microsoft for 25 years, Bill Gates handed the reins of Microsoft to Steve Ballmer in January 2000. Ballmer went on to run Microsoft for the next 14 years. 
Ballmer organized the company around execution of its then current strengths – Windows and Office businesses. He tripled Microsoft’s sales to $78 billion and profits more than doubled from $9 billion to $22 billion, but those results hid missed opportunities that would reshape computing.
Ballmer failed to understand and execute on the five most important technology trends of the 21st century: in search – losing to Google; in smartphones – losing to Apple; in mobile operating systems – losing to Google/Apple; in media – losing to Apple/Netflix; and in the cloud – losing to Amazon.
In early 2014, Satya Nadella took charge. Nadella got Microsoft organized around the cloud (Azure), freed the Office and Azure teams from Windows and killed the phone business. Nadella saved Microsoft from irrelevance. Today Microsoft is worth about $4 trillion. It is one of the few companies that regained its former glory, after its board replaced a salesman with a product and engineering insider.
Innovation CEOs Hire Execution VPs
Successful visionary CEOs build an executive staff of world-class operating executives and technical leaders (and they unconsciously force out any world-class innovators from their direct reports). The problem is in a company driven by a visionary CEO; there is only one visionary.

Nvidia is the extreme case. Jensen had about 60 direct reports and discouraged one-on-one meetings. No long-term plans. Much like Jobs, he made himself the point where technical, product and business decisions came together. Replacing that role is harder than filling a box on an organization chart. He said he didn’t believe in succession planning.

When visionary founders depart (resignation, death, firing, etc.), their operating executives believe it’s their turn to run the company. At Microsoft, Bill Gates chose Steve Ballmer, and at Apple Steve Jobs chose Tim Cook. Jensen anointed no one.
Once in charge, one of the first things these operations/execution CEOs do is to get rid of the chaos and turbulence in the organization. Execution CEOs value stability, process and repeatable execution. On one hand that’s great for predictability, but it often starts a creative death spiral – creative people start to leave, and other executors (without the innovation talent of the old leader) are put into more senior roles – hiring more process people, which in turn forces out the remaining creative talent. This culture shift ripples down from the top and what once felt like a company on a mission to change the world now feels like another job. 
(Amazon is a counterexample. Before succeeding Jeff Bezos, Andy Jassy had already founded and led AWS. He was an innovator who had built a new business inside the existing company.)
At Nvidia this step isn’t optional. No one else can run a company with 60 direct reports, so the next CEO’s first move will be to add layers of management.
Why Tim Cook Was the New Steve Ballmer
Tim Cook ran Apple for 15 years. Apple under Cook grew its revenues from $108 billion to $416 billion and its market value from $350 billion to about $4 trillion. The iPhone continued its annual upgrades of incremental improvements. The transition to Apple silicon showed that major technical change on the chip level was possible under his leadership. But Apple silicon was a better engine for the existing products Apple already had, not a new market.
Apple stumbled badly on the one major product category launched under Cook – Apple Vision Pro and missed badly on AI (Siri was actually first with a Chatbot.) This year it turned to Google to help fix Siri. In September 2026 Cook handed the job to John Ternus.
How Nvidia Could Miss the Boat
The threat to Nvidia is already visible: its biggest customers are becoming its competitors. Google, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta, OpenAI and Tesla are all building their own AI chips. Jensen’s answer was to keep innovating and bring to market a new generation of chips every year, build a moat around an integrated hardware and software platform (CUDA), and invest ~$50 billion in the AI labs that run on Nvidia. 
It’s likely these are the bets an execution CEO trims first.
The irony is that in the 21st century, the tighter you hold on to your current product/markets, the likelier you will be disrupted. The rapid change of technology will create new disruptions that will force “bet the company” decisions about product direction, markets, pricing, supply chain, operations and the reorganization necessary to execute a new business model. In the GPU market, the difference between market dominance and Chapter 11 may be a hands-on product/customer, and business model-centric CEO with an entrepreneurial vision of the future.
Four Challenges for the Board of Directors
The dilemma facing the board at Nvidia or any board of directors on the departure of an innovative CEO is strategic: Do we still want to be an innovative, risk taking company? Or should we now focus on execution of our core business, reduce our risky bets and maximize shareholder return. Ultimately a board would like both – preserving the speed and ambition of Jensen’s decision-making while distributing enough authority for the company to function without him.
Tactically, that question results in asking: Do you search for another innovator from outside, promote one of the executors or go deeper down the organization to find an innovator?
Herein lies four challenges. 
Lessons Learned
 

						Filed under: National Security |

There is no question that Jensen Huang is a brilliant innovator, visionary, and company builder, but he is also a man filled to overflowing with hubris and a reckless disregard for his fiduciary duty to his shareholders.
He held views as to Chinese use of Nvidia’s bleeding edge chips with which reasonable men can disagree with equally sound logic. 
While all that you say in regard to Apple/Jobs and MS/Gates is true, the performance of both of those companies post founder departure was and is superb. If those exemplars define the downside, I will load up on Nvidia before their earnings call on Tuesday.

43,2026-11-15,"There’s no denying Vespas are one of the coolest two-wheelers out there. The Italian scooter has practically cornered the retro-chic market, but its price tag isn't exactly pocket change. Not everyone wants to pay Vespa money to look like they're riding a Vespa.
But there’s good news: there's a growing crop of alternatives that borrow liberally from its design playbook without asking you to cough up the premium. Some are shameless throwbacks, while others put their own spin on the familiar rounded bodywork, curvy fenders, and step-through silhouette. Call them tributes, alternatives, or outright copycats – there's no denying that the Vespa aesthetic has become a genre all of its own.
We’ve collated a list of five such scooters that technically let you buy a Vespa … without actually buying a Vespa.

Royal Alloy is a British scooter brand known for classic 1960s retro design paired with modern tech and proper metal construction. The GT2 125 is its latest entry-level model that sheds some of that weight with a plastic body instead, while still boasting a familiar 60s Vespa-like silhouette. 
Meanwhile, a modern, water-cooled single-cylinder swingarm-mounted 125cc engine powers the scoot, producing 13.6 hp at 8,500 rpm and 8.9 lb-ft (12 Nm) of torque at 6,500 rpm. The motor is paired with a variable transmission (CVT) and centrifugal clutch, offering more than enough power for anything city traffic might demand.
It comes in four trims – the base ""Fun"" model offers a 3-inch TFT display allowing for smartphone connectivity, while also boasting dual-channel ABS with traction control, tire pressure monitoring, start/stop system, heated grips, and keyless entry. The ""Sport"" trim adds heated seats to the equipment, and the TFT display grows from three to five inches.
The ""Elegance"" trim adds more chrome and classic touches with different painted seats, while the top-of-the-line ""Sport Touring"" model comes equipped with practical front and rear luggage racks and a small windscreen alongside a dashcam.
At 300 lb (136 kg) ready to ride, the GT2 125 is refreshingly light, thanks to its lightweight body construction. It also offers a low seat height of 29.5 inches (750 mm), and, combined with its flat running boards and open step-through design, getting on and off should be incredibly easy.
Starting at €3,400 (US$3,800), Royal Alloy’s GT2 125 goes all the way to €4,050 (US$4,515) – which is still cheaper than Vespa’s mid-range models.
Now, Morbidelli may sound like it hails from the same land as Vespa, but it's actually owned by the Chinese two-wheeler giant QJ Motor group. But every angle of the SC125RE screams Vespa. The only point of difference is the stubby rear end, which is more Lambretta than Vespa.
Powering this particular scoot is another 124cc liquid-cooled single-cylinder engine. This one produces 11 hp at 7,500 rpm and 8 lb-ft (11 Nm) of torque at 5,500 rpm. An automatic CVT transmission and a V-belt drive make sure power delivery and shifting are as smooth as it can get.
Mechanically, you get a conventional telescopic front fork with 3.3 inches (85 mm) of travel, and a 5-stage preload-adjustable monoshock at the rear with 3.4 inches (88 mm) of travel. It rolls on 12-inch 16-spoke aluminum alloy wheels, which are adorned with a 200 mm disc at the front and a 190 mm disc at the rear.
Of course, its bodywork is reminiscent of Vespas, with a split-design headlight really giving it away at the front. But features like LED lighting, ABS, an LCD screen, a USB charging port, disc brakes, and alloys do give it a modern touch.
This one comes priced at £2,699, which translates to US$3,600, which is even cheaper than the Royal Alloy Vespa lookalike.
I swear I didn’t intend to make this an all-125cc scooter list when starting out, but nevertheless, here’s another one: the Django Evo. Now, Django might not be as well-known as others on this list, but it’s actually a scooter model line from Peugeot Motorcycles. It has been in existence since 2014 and has been known for its neo-retro aesthetics.
Powering the Evo lineup (there are four different trims) is yet another 125cc liquid-cooled single-cylinder motor producing 13 hp at 8,500 rpm and 9 lb-ft (12 Nm) of torque at 6,500 rpm.
A 32-mm hydraulic telescopic fork at the front offers 3.3 inches (85 mm) of travel, while the 47-mm rear shock absorber mounted on the left-hand side offers 3.1 inches (80 mm) of travel. The wheels measure 12 inches front and back and come equipped with dual disc brakes, featuring a 190-mm disc at the rear, both clamped by a hydraulic caliper.
The equipment list isn’t too bad either: dual-channel ABS, water- and wear-resistant tires, dual lion-eye LED headlights with DRL, dynamic turn signals, tinted windscreen, a 5-inch TFT screen with Bluetooth connectivity, a smart key, a USB fast-charging port, among others.
But if you’re looking for a rather affordable Vespa lookalike, it ticks most boxes: rounded bodywork, flowing side panels, a generous front apron, a wide handlebar-mounted headlight and plenty of chrome detailing. The two-tone paint schemes, sculpted waistline and old-school proportions help it look like something that could have rolled out of an Italian scooter factory sometime in the 1960s.
The price? Starting €3,199 (US$3,560). Not bad at all.


Moving away from a 125, we’ve got the Cyclone RT3E (zero marks for that naming), which comes powered by a 250cc, water-cooled single-cylinder engine producing 24.1 hp and 18.1 lb-ft (24.5 Nm) of torque. Manufactured by China's Zongshen and popular in the Malaysian market, this offering has the familiar retro-scooter silhouette, with proportions and overall styling that have a distinctly European, old-school scooter flavor, even if it is considerably more substantial than your typical city runabout.
It also packs dual-channel ABS, keyless ignition, full LED lighting, an LCD instrument panel and generous under-seat storage. With a 3.4-gallon (13-liter) fuel tank and a relatively accessible 29.5-inch (750-mm) seat height, it looks like a retro scooter that wouldn't mind doing more than just posing for Instagram pictures.
Although primarily available in Malaysia and select parts of Asia, the scoot is priced at RM12,888 – that’s US$3,175. It’s a scoot that isn't particularly shy about wearing its retro influences, but there's enough modern detailing to stop it from looking like a museum piece.
The RT3E is a reminder that the Vespa-inspired formula doesn't have to mean small engines and leisurely urban commutes. It brings a little more performance and contemporary equipment to the familiar silhouette, making it an interesting alternative for riders who like their scooters with plenty of usable power.

Last on this list is a scooter from a rather well-known scooter brand, Lambretta. And the G350 might just be the most subdued model we’ve seen in their lineup in recent years. That’s considering just how insane some of its recent builds have been of late.
This one, of course, as its name suggests, sports the biggest motor in this list – a 330cc liquid-cooled single-cylinder delivering 26 hp at 7,500 rpm and 19 lb-ft (26 Nm) of torque at 6,250 rpm linked to an automatic CVT transmission. The chassis comes in the form of a semi-monocoque steel frame suspended on a double trailing-link front system with two spring-loaded shocks, and two spring-loaded shocks at the rear, with the whole thing tipping the scales at 403 lb (183 kg).
If the Vespa is the curvy, fashion-conscious Italian scooter, the Lambretta G350 is its slightly more muscular, sharply dressed cousin. It doesn't really do the whole soft, rounded thing, instead leaning into the more angular design language that made classic Lambrettas stand apart in the first place.
There's a long, low-slung body, a squared-off front apron, a distinctive hexagonal headlight, a fixed front fender, and those signature interchangeable steel side panels. It's got a bit more visual heft than a Vespa, too, with its broad shoulders and stretched proportions giving it an almost motorcycle-like presence.
The G350 has a more cohesive design identity, and that’s why this one would be my personal pick, even though it is further away from others on this list when it comes to simply mimicking Vespas. As for pricing, the Lambretta G350 is the priciest of the lot, coming in at £5,699 (US$7,620).
The interesting bit is that these aren't all cheap knock-offs trying to cash in on an Italian icon. Some come from brands with their own history, engineering ambitions, and rather different ideas about what a scooter should be.
The likes of Lambretta, Peugeot, and Morbidelli, and other newer names have figured out there's a market for retro scooters. You might get a different badge, a different engine, and a different riding experience, but the basic promise remains the same: old-school charm without necessarily buying into the Vespa lifestyle.
Of course, imitation is a funny business. Get too close to the original, and you're accused of lacking imagination; stray too far, and you lose the very appeal that brought buyers through the door. But if what you really want is a handsome little city runabout with a generous helping of retro nostalgia, there are plenty of ways to scratch that itch.
Source: Royal Alloy, Morbidelli, Peugeot, Cyclone, Lambretta

46,2026-11-15," According to the Washington Times, Huang stepped down as Nvidia CEO on Thursday, Nov. 12. His resignation followed revelations of his extramarital affairs with a Chinese executive believed to be spying for Beijing, and a company executive who reported to him [1]. The company said Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress will serve as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent successor [2][3]. Nvidia shares finished 4.3% lower on Thursday, off the day's lows, according to market data [2]. U.S. stocks ended a volatile session with modest losses, while U.S. Treasury yields climbed [2]. The departure followed a board investigation announced Sunday into reports that Huang was targeted by a foreign intelligence operation [4][5]. Nvidia's board accepted Huang's resignation on Wednesday, Nov. 11, according to a Securities and Exchange Commission filing [3].Market Indicators, Dot-Com Comparison, and Dissenting View Desmond Lachlan, senior fellow at the American Enterprise Institute, warned that the ""chaos"" at Nvidia has serious repercussions outside the company's walls [1]. ""If youâve got chaos at Nvidia, it could scare off investment at other companies because of their interconnected finances,"" he said. ""It adds to several factors suggesting a high chance that the AI bubble will burst within the next year, if not sooner."" ""The values of many of these companies have risen thirtyfold, from the millions to the trillions. It wouldn't surprise me if you saw a 30% decline in AI stock values over the next couple of months,"" Lachman added. Dallas-based financial adviser Bill Dendy remarked that Nvidia's struggles contribute to the ""risk of a big AI disappointment"" similar to the dot-com boom of the early 2000s [1]. ""The burst wasn't a loud pop; it was a bleed-down, and that's often the way these things happen,"" he pointed out. 'It's hard to know which companies will survive, and it's not obvious that people need to exit until itâs too late."" Sean Williams, contributing analyst at The Motley Fool, meanwhile wrote that ""the odds of an AI bubble-bursting event appear to be rising by the day."" But Arnold Platz, founder and CEO of the electronics recycling firm General Future Holding, was more optimistic in tone [1]. Platz predicted that the AI boom would outlast the embattled Nvidia and prove more durable than the dot-com boom [1]. ""The big buyers are profitable and the hardware is real,"" he said in an email to the Washington Times.Midterm Elections and Data Center Regulation Add to AI Stock Sell-Off On Nov. 3, Americans took to polling places to vote in the 2026 midterm elections. The elections saw Democratic Party victories in different states, alongside opposition to data centers. The regulatory shift adds a new layer of uncertainty to a buildout that has already faced high-profile setbacks, including Oracle Corp. and OpenAI abandoning plans to expand a flagship AI data center in Abilene, Texas [6].Outlook Uncertain as Analysts Watch Nvidia and AI Stocks Huang remains entangled in the fallout. He agreed to brief Congress on his relationship with the woman identified by U.S. intelligence as an alleged Chinese spy. The episode arrives at a time when AI spending has become a load-bearing wall of the U.S. economy.References   Resignation of Nvidia CEO pushes AI bubble closer to bursting, analysts warn. https://www.washingtontimes.com/news/2026/nov/12/resignation-nvidia-ceo-pushes-ai-bubble-closer-bursting-analysts/  Wall Street Ends Lower as Nvidia CEO Resigns; US Yields Up. https://www.ntd.com/wall-street-ends-lower-as-nvidia-ceo-resigns-us-yields-up_1185109.html  Nvidiaâs Jensen Huang resigns, chipmaker names CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO. https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/12/nvidias-jensen-huang-resigns-chipmaker-names-cfo-colette-kress-as-interim-ceo/  Nvidia CEO Huang resigns after reports of extramarital relationships with suspected Chinese spy. https://justthenews.com/nation/technology/nvidia-ceo-huang-resigns-after-reports-extramarital-relationships-suspected?utm_source=justthenews.com&utm_medium=feed&utm_campaign=external-news-aggregators  You Vote: With the ousting of Jensen Huang as CEO of Nvidia, what is your biggest takeaway? https://justthenews.com/government/security/you-vote-ousting-jensen-huang-ceo-nvidia-what-your-biggest-takeaway?utm_source=justthenews.com&utm_medium=feed&utm_campaign=external-news-aggregators  Oracle OpenAI Scrap Texas Data Center Expansion Plan AI Stocks Decline - NaturalNews.com, March 11, 2026. ""It adds to several factors suggesting a high chance that the AI bubble will burst within the next year, if not sooner."" ""The values of many of these companies have risen thirtyfold, from the millions to the trillions. It wouldn't surprise me if you saw a 30% decline in AI stock values over the next couple of months,"" Lachman added. Dallas-based financial adviser Bill Dendy remarked that Nvidia's struggles contribute to the ""risk of a big AI disappointment"" similar to the dot-com boom of the early 2000s [1]. ""The burst wasn't a loud pop; it was a bleed-down, and that's often the way these things happen,"" he pointed out. 'It's hard to know which companies will survive, and it's not obvious that people need to exit until itâs too late."" Sean Williams, contributing analyst at The Motley Fool, meanwhile wrote that ""the odds of an AI bubble-bursting event appear to be rising by the day."" But Arnold Platz, founder and CEO of the electronics recycling firm General Future Holding, was more optimistic in tone [1]. Platz predicted that the AI boom would outlast the embattled Nvidia and prove more durable than the dot-com boom [1]. ""The big buyers are profitable and the hardware is real,"" he said in an email to the Washington Times.Midterm Elections and Data Center Regulation Add to AI Stock Sell-Off On Nov. 3, Americans took to polling places to vote in the 2026 midterm elections. The elections saw Democratic Party victories in different states, alongside opposition to data centers. The regulatory shift adds a new layer of uncertainty to a buildout that has already faced high-profile setbacks, including Oracle Corp. and OpenAI abandoning plans to expand a flagship AI data center in Abilene, Texas [6].Outlook Uncertain as Analysts Watch Nvidia and AI Stocks Huang remains entangled in the fallout. He agreed to brief Congress on his relationship with the woman identified by U.S. intelligence as an alleged Chinese spy. One agency's data on 14 B2B advertisers finds the same non-brand Google Ads keywords cost more per click, even after controlling for their own spending.
To determine how much more the same non-branded Google Ads keywords cost 14 B2B advertisers per click during the months of April through September compared to the same time period last year, Grey Matter reviewed their Google Ads Search campaigns.
According to the agency report by Grey Matter issued in October, there was an increase of about 21% in cost-per-click (CPC) for the same keywords used by these 14 B2B advertisers, after accounting for changes in their spending, ad position and Quality Score. The report gives a likely range of 1% to 44% for that figure. It is based on client Google Ads Search campaign data only, so it does not include Performance Max, Shopping or Display campaigns. All 14 come from the agency’s client base, and its search team runs SEO and paid search programs for B2B companies.
Andy Hendricks, Grey Matter’s head of search, writes that he wanted to isolate what portion of the overall price change all advertisers experienced versus what portion was due to actions taken by each advertiser individually. Most available CPC benchmarks, such as published averages, do not account for this differentiation and simply provide an overall average CPC.
Grey Matter excluded broad match keywords since the searches they get matched to can change over time; keywords within Maximize Clicks campaigns also do not fit into the analysis. Brand and competitor keywords were also excluded from the overall estimate.
Grey Matter’s analysis is based on tracking 1,349 non-brand keywords, each within one account, for six quarters (April 2025 to September 2026). The year-over-year comparison covers April through September 2026 versus those months a year prior.
The headline estimate was derived from a regression model using data from all 14 advertisers in the report which controlled for campaign spend, impression share lost to budget, top-of-page position, and Quality Score. Every advertiser was given equal weight regardless of how much it spends.
With no controls applied, the same keywords cost 29% more year over year. Adding the two spend-related controls lowered that to 22%, and position and Quality Score took it to 21%.
A second estimation technique used in this analysis was a keyword price index. This measure covered 11 advertisers who had sufficient matching keywords from quarter to quarter.
The report’s regression ties every 10% increase in campaign spend to about 1.4% higher CPCs, on top of the market increase, or roughly 11% for doubled spend. The report doesn’t give a likely range for that figure.
The most extreme case in the sample nearly quadrupled its non-brand Search spend year over year, and its same-keyword CPCs rose 132%. The report says that advertiser’s brand keywords, on a flat budget, rose about 4% year over year in the third quarter.
The report sees a correlation between this pattern and automated bidding. It explains that a campaign using a strategy like Maximize Conversions spends a bigger budget by entering auctions it previously skipped, including higher positions, more competitive searches, and pricier times of day.
For the 11 advertisers covered by the keyword price index, the overall year-over-year increase in average non-brand CPC was 36%, including the advertisers’ own choices. Of that, 22 points were attributed to the market, and three additional points were attributed to increased spending. There was a three-point deduction due to various types of keyword, match type and bid strategy changes, leaving 14 points that the report’s first three parts don’t explain.
Grey Matter illustrates that breakdown in the chart below.
The majority of those 14 points occurred at a few of the advertisers who saw an approximate doubling of their CPCs, which the report said probably represented competition in their respective categories.
Actual CPC changes ranged from a 30% decrease to a 121% increase. Ten of the 11 paid more for the same keywords, and nine paid more overall. One advertiser kept its spending flat and saw the same keywords rise by 20%. After shifting some of its budget to pricier phrase-match keywords, its overall CPC increased by 40%.
With only 14 advertisers in this report, the likely range for the 21% estimate is wide. When the biggest spender is removed from the report’s data, the estimate drops to around 17%, and its likely range of -2% to +40% includes zero. When broad match keywords are included, the estimate rises to around 31%. According to Hendricks, compared with a year ago, that same click most likely costs about 20% more currently, and the true figure “could be meaningfully higher or lower”.
The report calls its findings correlation (and not proven causation). There are no references on the report page or in the PDF to the industries or markets of the advertisers used in the report, nor their currency.
The report doesn’t provide any conversion rate or cost per lead information; so, it can’t show whether these advertisers saw better or worse results along with higher CPCs. Jyll Saskin Gales argued in an April column for Search Engine Journal (SEJ) that under Smart Bidding, a high CPC is often a sign of a healthy account, and that accounts should be evaluated based on CPA or ROAS.
Alphabet’s third-quarter 10-Q reports that cost-per-click on Google Search & other properties rose 4% year over year in the quarter. Alphabet has defined this metric as click-driven revenue divided by total paid clicks and has cited several factors such as changes in the devices users access the internet on, geography, advertiser spending, and product mix as variables that affect it.
The metric is based on paid clicks through Google Search properties and other platforms owned by Alphabet, so it will track with the price of the clicks and also the mix of clicks being counted. Grey Matter’s estimate instead tracks the same keywords across 14 advertisers.
There is no direct confirmation or contradiction between the two figures since they measure entirely different things.
Grey Matter’s report holds the keyword and the account constant, while raw CPC averages also rise when advertisers buy different clicks. With 14 clients of one agency and a range from 1% to 44%, the report is a reference point for B2B non-brand Search, not a forecast for any single account.
You can run a same-keyword check on your own account by comparing the year-over-year change in what the same non-brand keywords cost with the change in the account’s average CPC. The report reads the gap between those two as the effect of keyword, match type and bid strategy changes. A same-keyword rise can still include the effect of higher spending.
Grey Matter’s data runs through September, so it doesn’t include fourth-quarter auctions. For 2027 budgets, the report’s guidance is to scale them gradually and assess each step by how much the additional clicks cost. For campaigns on automated bidding such as Maximize Conversions, the report also suggests deciding what the additional conversions are worth before raising the budget, and setting a target to match.

49,2026-11-15,"Oz magazine is remembered today as one of the most notorious counterculture publications. Launched on April 1, 1963, by Richard Nevile, Richard Walsh, and Martin Sharp in Sydney, Australia, it was a satirical, psychedelic provocateur whose legal battles still feature in free-speech debates, and whose archives historians and designers now study.
Pre-Oz, Western society heavily operated on traditional post-World War II values. In other words, the status quo was staunchly conservative and rigid, with substantive dissent either intolerable or criminalised.
Staying deferential wasn’t merely a choice for the mainstream press; it was driven by routine censorship. It’s a climate that saw Britain’s Lord Chamberlain vet stage plays before they could be performed, Australian customs officials screening books and films, homosexuality outlawed, and obscenity trials happening as late as 1960.
Despite this hostility to the underground press, Oz thrived for a decade (1963-1973), and across continents. It proved that a market existed for anti-establishment satire.
 The first step toward controversy is to stand out, and in media the surest way to do that is to publish what everyone else avoids. Long before Oz became famous for psychedelic London posters or the “Schoolkids” trial, it built its notorious reputation in Sydney, Australia, by printing what polite society only whispered about.
The debut April 1963 issue tackled the largely avoided subject of illegal abortion head-on, exposing underground abortion clinics and the corrupt police system that protected and profited from it. Included in the mix were incendiary takes on homosexuality, then both criminalized and deeply sensitive, along with provocations such as bodily tattoos, chastity belts, and Australia’s White immigration policy.
Oz added deadpan hoaxes that mocked public authority and symbols of sacred cultural status, such as a report of the Sydney Harbour Bridge collapsing. That was the equivalent of Parisians waking to (fake) news that the Eiffel Tower had crumbled, or New Yorkers reading on the front page of The New York Times that the Brooklyn Bridge had fallen.
If that doesn’t seem audacious enough, consider the cover of Oz’s Issue No. 6 in February 1964. It featured a photo of editor Richard Neville and two friends pretending to urinate into a newly unveiled wall fountain designed by prominent sculptor Tom Bass, a work that had just been inaugurated by Prime Minister Robert Menzies.
Oz’s early work in Sydney set the tone for its shock-and-satire identity. It also showed readers that public authorities and the mainstream press could fool them just as easily as the magazine did with its hoaxes.
Unsurprisingly, the reaction was as polarized as it was immediate. Newsstand bans did nothing to dissuade buyers who cleared all 6,000 debut copies. Establishment institutions hit back fast: advertisers pulled their contracts, writers’ jobs were threatened, and the editors were evicted from their government-owned Sydney office.
 Oz had jolted Sydney’s readers, but more than 10,000 miles away in London, its 1967 relaunch as Oz London hit even harder. And it was a huge success: circulation doubled from Sydney’s 40,000 peak to 80,000 copies.
Much of the credit goes to Martin Sharp, who harnessed photographic offset lithography, freeing designers to experiment with neon colour, spiral layouts and collage. In modern terms, it was like leaping from a plain-text newsletter to a neon, meme-saturated TikTok feed.
Rather than arguing solely in neat columns as in Sydney, Oz London utilized countercultural provocations, especially with revered art and public faces. Nothing moves mouths quite like a magazine with the Mona Lisa smoking a joint, Bob Dylan as a swirling psychedelic portrait, or Vincent van Gogh as a modern hippie on its cover. That’s exactly what Oz London did.
For the average 1960s British youth, the radical art was bedroom wall poster-worthy. And a copy in hand (laden with topics such as feminism, drug laws, and the Vietnam War) became tantamount to being the cool, rebellious mind the age grade so relished.
 Audacious as it was, the May 1970 issue of Oz was downright impudent. Bored with their own output, editors Richard Neville, Jim Anderson, and Felix Dennis handed the reins of an entire edition to under-18s, to test the limits of the rawness and rebellion of British adolescence. Pitch that to a modern magazine today, and the legal department would hyperventilate.
Predictably, the teenagers’ finger and mind prints were all over the resulting 48-page Issue No. 28. However, the nude cover and explicit personal ads in the back were entirely the adults' idea.
The juveniles aired their grievances, attacking the British school system’s authoritarian headmasters, corporal punishments, uniform rules, and exams. Alongside these critiques were their unfiltered views on teenage sexuality, contraception, recreational drugs, and countercultural music. Far from an orderly debate, their confrontation was chaotic and deliberately juvenile. It peaked with what is now infamously known as “The Rupert Bear incident.”
In that same issue, 15-year-old Vivian Berger made a notorious collage: she pasted the cheerful head of children's icon Rupert Bear onto a sexually explicit body of an X-rated comic by American underground cartoonist Robert Crumb, with the nursery rhymes clearly still visible underneath.
Vivian Berger’s shattering of the innocence associated with Rupert Bear (the ultimate symbol of wholesome, middle-class British childhood) was rude at best. To British authorities, though, this crossed an unforgivable line. By indulging teens in the magazine's production and publishing Berger’s explicit collage, Oz had committed cultural sacrilege in a deliberate attempt to corrupt Britain’s youth.
The police, who had hitherto restricted their raids to Oz’s offices, arrested Neville, Anderson, and Dennis and confiscated all printing materials at the Oz headquarters. This set the stage for the historic 1971 Old Bailey obscenity trial.
 Anyone in London (the wider British public and International counterculture inclusive) who hadn’t heard or been bothered by Oz did so following the “Schoolkids Issue.” And the few still unbothered couldn't ignore the resulting trial that began on June 23, 1971, inside Court No. 2 at London’s Old Bailey.
This six-week trial was the longest obscenity trial in British legal history. And it was a public affair, so much so that thousands rallied in support outside the courthouse and John Lennon and Yoko Ono recorded a charity single, ""God Save Us,”
Even with such a high-stakes trial, including a “conspiracy to corrupt public morals” charge that carried a life sentence, the defendants did what underground counterculture did best: mock authority. They arrived at court rocking psychedelic shirts, in schoolgirl uniforms, and having Richard Neville defend himself in court while wearing a long, flowing velvet jacket.
More importantly, it was a cultural clash between the British establishment, wielding the 1959 Obscene Publications Act under which the defendants were tried, and counterculture, represented by defendants Richard Neville, Jim Anderson, and Felix Dennis. In the end, both sides won and lost, though to varying degrees.
Neville, Anderson, and Dennis lost when they were acquitted of conspiracy but convicted of publishing and posting an obscene article, receiving sentences of 15, 12, and 9 months respectively. This was not before they were remanded for psychiatric evaluation and their signature hairs shaved.
The law’s victory was short-lived and pyrrhic. Following intense public outrage, the convictions were overturned on appeal in November 1971, and the trio walked free. Subsequently, the British justice system lost the political appetite to prosecute obscene publications, even as Oz itself buckled in 1973 under the weight of legal debt.
In the end, though, the real winner was Oz. They emerged as free-speech folk heroes whose legal victory permanently broke the back of British print censorship and paved the way for modern media freedom. Wealth flowed from it too: lessons learnt from Oz were vital in Dennis establishing a publishing empire.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Weekly poll

Six years ago, we asked you “how often do you change your phone?”. The most common answer back then was once every three years, with once every two years coming in a close second. So, if the statistics hold up, most of you reading this have changed your phone two or three times since 2020.
But back then AI was just a curiosity. No one could have predicted the effect it would have on RAM and storage prices in just a few years. Yet here we are in a time when the prices of phones go up instead of down after they launch.
Okay, if we look past the doom-and-gloom state of the memory market, we’ll see some positive changes too. Phones with 6 or 7 years of OS updates are quite common now, while they were quite rare back then. Such a level of support was nigh impossible for anything without an Apple logo on the back.
Chipset performance, battery capacity and image sensor capabilities keep improving every year – these are normally incentives to swap your old phone for a new one. That said, some people feel that we’ve hit a point of diminishing returns – an old phone remains “good enough” for several years.
On the flip side, the rapid advancement of technologies like Si/C batteries, 2nm chips, LOFIC image sensors, Privacy Displays, foldable designs and so on makes it that much more tempting to pick up a new phone. This way you will enjoy the cutting edge features now instead of a few years from now.
Here’s what we want to ask this week – how long do you plan to keep your current phone? One or two years, maybe more?
We want to try something a little different this time, since the answer very much depends on whether you have a flagship, a mid-range or an entry-level phone.
The poll this week has three questions – one for each category. Use the “I don’t have an X” to skip the question in the categories that are not relevant to you and pick the answer for the one that is.
I bought my current phone for $159 using my friend's card discount, and now the same phone is selling for $297! It’s crazy what’s going on—I'm definitely sticking with mine.

51,2026-11-15,"TOPICS

                    off-prem
                

        ASIA IN BRIEF
    

        PLUS: SEGA Japan suffers outage and data leak; India bans dead battery exports; Alibaba chip unit to float; and more
    
Microsoft has announced massive price rises for commercial cloud services in India and New Zealand, but a substantial reduction in China.
The software giant sells its cloudy services in many local currencies, at an equivalent rate to the prices it charges in the USA.
As exchange rates shift, so do the prices Microsoft charges around the world.
Microsoft last week announced the new charges that will come into effect from January 1, 2027, and the news isn’t pleasant for customers in India, who face an increase of 15.7 percent for cloud services and up to 20 percent for some on-prem offerings. New Zealand customers face a ten percent price hike.
Customers in China, where Microsoft relies on a local partner called 21Vianet to deliver its cloud, will see costs fall by up to 8.5 percent.
Australian buyers will see costs fall by 4.5 percent. The only nation not in Asia on Microsoft’s list is Norway, where prices will ease 5.9 percent.
Sega has just had a rotten week that saw it battle a data leak and an outage.
The outage impacted the company’s ALL.Net and Aime services, which connect arcade games across different locations. The services include administrative features for arcade operators, plus services for gamers including pre-payment to play, high score and profile recording, and networked gaming.
Sega suspended some ALL.Net and Aime services on November 1 after discovering unauthorized access to servers. At least thirteen games and services were then unavailable until around November 15.
The company hasn’t explained what went wrong, but assured customers the incident was not a data breach and that payment information was safe.
But data did leak for those who subscribe to some Sega newsletters. Sega uses a third-party outfit called Arara Co. to send some emails, and it leaked email addresses. No other personal data leaked from Arara. Sega has nonetheless warned newsletter recipients to watch out for suspicious messages.
Chinese tech giant Alibaba last week announced its intention to float its T-Head chip design biz on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange.
The filing announcing the spinoff describes T-Head as “a leading provider of full-stack data center chips which enable system-level compute solutions in China,” suggesting Alibaba doesn’t think buyers in the rest of the world are keen to acquire its chips.
T-Head going public “allows a more efficient deployment of Alibaba Group’s financial resources,” the filing states, and should also mean the chip design outfit can more easily find third-party customers.
The government of India last week banned the export of “Lithium-Ion battery Black Mass” – a powdery substance created during the process of recycling batteries.
“It contains recoverable quantities of critical minerals, including lithium, cobalt, nickel and manganese,” according to the government’s announcement of the ban, which argues the measure “will increase domestic recovery of lithium, cobalt, nickel and other critical minerals, thereby reducing import dependence and the associated foreign exchange outgo. It will also strengthen supply chains for battery manufacturing, electric vehicles, energy storage and clean-energy technologies.”
“Further, the measure will advance the circular economy and ‘urban mining’, reducing reliance on primary mining, while supporting environmentally sound recycling of this hazardous material within the country in accordance with applicable environmental regulations.”
Five giant Japanese companies – NTT, Mitsubishi Electric, MUFG Bank, the Development Bank of Japan, and telco NTT DOCOMO – last week signed a memorandum of understanding that will see them jointly explore development and operation of a mega-constellation of satellites.
The five “will examine the technical and business feasibility of commercializing a diverse range of services enabled by mega-constellations, including communications, Earth observation, satellite-based positioning, navigation, and timing (PNT), and space data centers.”
Mitsubishi already works with the Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA) to jointly develop and manufacture rockets. The two banks regularly back big infrastructure projects, while the two NTT entities have advanced comms expertise.
The five companies didn’t set a deadline for delivery of a plan.
If they do decide there’s a business opportunity, they’ll likely need to look beyond Japan for launch capacity as JAXA does not currently possess a re-usable launch vehicle and operates at a modest launch cadence compared to private launch operators. ®

52,2026-11-15,"Ever dreamed of visiting a cozy Christmas village in Austria or a luxury ski resort in the Alps? Really, who hasn't? The cost of such a trip is often way beyond an average family's budget, so most of us believe that this must remain a dream. But there is another way to get your perfect winter vacation in the U.S.
Several towns nestled within American mountain ranges have been designed to mimic the European villages that tourists love, allowing those of us who aren't able to fly internationally to still partake in our very own winter wonderland. These towns have all the charm and whimsy of the European originals, so your dream winter trip could be a reality without spending any euros.
Nearly 30 miles outside of Salt Lake City, Midway is Utah's ""Little Switzerland."" In the 1850s, Swiss and German immigrants settled in the area, influencing everything from the architecture to cultural celebrations. A stay at the Zermatt Utah Resort and Spa will have you questioning whether you're not actually in a fancy European ski lodge.
Speaking of skiing, Midway's Soldier Hollow was a site for the 2002 Winter Olympics and is now a great place to ski, snowboard, snowshoe, or tube sled. There is also Swiss influence in the food, with plenty of chocolate and artisanal cheeses that taste as authentic as if they were made in a small Alpine village available.
Leavenworth almost became a ghost town after the railroad was rerouted, and the townspeople were desperate to find ways to attract tourists. In the 1960s, Leavenworth was completely redone to emulate a Bavarian village in Germany. The entire downtown area was rebuilt with chalets and cozy shops filled with German schnitzel, ale, and goods.
During the winter, Leavenworth has two festivals that have made it world-famous: Christmastown and the Winter Karneval. These festivals are filled with lights, music, and events like ice sculpting and a gingerbread house contest. They also have a reindeer farm, a nutcracker museum, and bands dressed in lederhosen playing German folk songs.
Boasting the title of ""First American Destination Ski Resort,"" Sun Valley, Idaho, has been famous since 1936. Ernest Hemingway even wrote For Whom the Bell Tolls while staying at the Sun Valley Resort, and many other celebrities came to ski at the place that rivaled Europe's resorts.
You can enjoy the Winter Wonderland festival between Thanksgiving and New Year's, where they have a holiday market, ice skating, sleigh rides, a tree lighting ceremony, and so much more. And, of course there's top-notch ski slopes on both Bald Mountain and Dollar Mountain.
Stowe Mountain Resort was built just one year too late to be the first American ski resort, but it still earned its namesake the nickname ""Ski Capital of the East."" The slopes are some of the best in the Northeast U.S., and the skyride gondolas provide a magnificent view of Vermont. Other winter activities in Stowe include dog-sledding, ice skating, ice climbing, and snowshoeing.
Another lodge in Stowe is famous for its Austrian style, having been founded by the von Trapp family themselves. After the real-life events on which The Sound of Music was based occurred, the von Trapp family made it to America and opened the von Trapp Family Lodge and Resort and von Trapp Brewing. If you're looking for an authentic Austrian feel, or want to see the American hills come alive, Stowe is the place to visit.

53,2026-11-15,"The M-VAVE FM-1 is a pocket FM synthesizer that sells for about $70, and almost nobody who owns one still runs the firmware it shipped with. Owners started writing their own in September. By October there were too many to cover in one article: a virtual analog synth, a multitrack groovebox, a dark ambient sampler, and a port of Doom. Yes, the video game. Going by what the developers post on the forums, nearly all of them were written with AI coding tools.

Video: Custom Firmware Tierlist by AudioPilz, ranking FM-1 firmwares.
Then M-Vave did something uncommon for a hardware company. In October it published its own firmware as open source under GPLv3, along with a development kit and a browser emulator for testing custom builds before you flash them.
I think this is where most consumer gadgets that run their own firmware are headed: synths, TVs, routers, printers. When software is cheap to write, the firmware a device ships with is just the first version. Devices that let owners replace it will sell better than devices that don’t. Most buyers won’t care about that. Their AI agents will, and more buying decisions are going to run through agents.
When a device doesn’t do what its owner wants, most people put up with it. Some replace the software, and in a few categories that’s already normal.
Cheap retro handhelds from Anbernic and others ship with firmware that buyers usually replace within days, and muOS and Knulli are the standard recommendations. Pebble came back as a fully open project. Core Devices published the OS, the phone apps, and the hardware schematics, and the phone app can install from app stores anyone can run. I replaced the home screen on our Google TV, which runs Android, with a custom launcher built for our family, with no ads and no tracking. Ubiquiti owners hook their network controllers up to AI agents through community-built servers and have the agent audit firewall rules and tune the network. That isn’t new firmware, but the agent is already running the device.
Writing firmware used to mean reverse engineering the chip, learning a vendor’s toolchain, and weeks of trial and error. The FM-1 runs on a JieLi chip with little public documentation, and owners still had working firmware within weeks.
AI made each part of that cheaper. It can read undocumented code, write new code for an unfamiliar chip, and build the editors, installers, and emulators around it. A motivated owner can now get through all of it in a weekend. Once that’s true, any device with writable flash is a platform, whether the maker planned for it or not.
Most people don’t think about improving their devices. They use what they get. So right now custom firmware reaches a small group: a few people write it, and a somewhat larger group finds it and installs it.
I don’t expect that to last. As agents take on more everyday work, the owner stops being the one who installs things. You ask your agent to fix something that bugs you about a device. It tries a setting first. If that doesn’t work, it looks for firmware someone else wrote, forks the closest match, or writes its own. You see that the problem went away, and you may never know the device is running custom code.
How fast this happens depends more on the hardware than on AI. The device has to accept new firmware, and signed firmware and locked bootloaders stop that. The agent needs a way in, like USB or a browser tool such as M-Vave’s Workbench. And a bad install has to be recoverable, since an agent can’t open the case and fix a bricked board. M-Vave added a tool that puts the stock firmware back in about ten seconds.
This changes how people buy hardware. Ask your agent which TV, router, or synth to get, and it will lean toward the one it can change, because that’s the one it can fix later. The buyer never has to think about openness for it to matter.
That pressure is what gets makers to open up, and it works fastest in categories where people compare options every time they buy: synths, TVs, printers, routers. Music gear is already moving. M-Vave opened its firmware within weeks of owners starting, and within two weeks after that Behringer published firmware source for its upcoming KICK sampler and IGNITE sequencer. Its parent company said it will open selected designs so owners can use AI to write their own firmware, and Akai and Erica Synths announced the same for some of their gear.
It will be slowest where switching is hard. People don’t shop for a new phone platform every year, because their messages, photos, and family accounts keep them on the iPhone. But Apple still sells to new buyers and to people replacing old phones, and more of those decisions will go through an agent. I expect Apple and other makers whose customers rarely leave to answer with deep, approved agent access to the OS, and to keep the firmware closed.
An open device is only useful if it works with everything else the owner has: their phone, their accounts, their cloud services. Pebble needed a working iPhone app before anyone would buy the watch. Home Assistant is useful because it connects to devices from companies that never planned for it. That’s where closed companies keep control, since they decide who can connect. Chamberlain cut Home Assistant off from its MyQ garage door openers in 2023.
The reason closed companies usually give is security. Bambu Lab added server authorization to its printers’ firmware as a security update. Owners read it as lock-in, and some well-known customers said they would stop buying. Google now requires app developers to verify their identity before their apps can be installed on certified Android devices, a rule in effect in four countries since September 30 and planned worldwide for 2027. Google’s stated reason is malware, and cheap code makes that argument stronger: harmful firmware is as easy to write as useful firmware.
Some of this is real security and some of it is lock-in, and you can usually tell which by asking who controls the signing keys. A device can refuse unsigned firmware and still let its owner add their own key. Pixel phones already work this way. If a maker blocks code the owner chose and signed, that block is protecting the maker’s business.
Open firmware might even make things safer. When millions of devices share the same weakness, one attack works on all of them; the Mirai botnet did exactly this in 2016. A mix of different firmwares makes that harder, though AI that finds bugs cheaply also makes it cheaper to go after many variants at once. The bigger risk is malicious firmware dressed up as something useful. Verified installers, owner-held keys, recovery modes, and agents that check firmware before they install it all help with that, and none of them require locking the device. I’d expect agents to favor devices that have them.
Custom firmware also changes what a maker knows about its own product. Right now a modified device is a support cost. Soon the owner’s agent will contact the maker’s support agent, explain what it changed and what broke, and get advice drawn from every other owner who tried something similar. A maker with open devices finds out what people want much faster than it would from surveys. A closed maker doesn’t get that information at all, because nobody is building on its devices.
This only works if the agent works for the owner. An agent that recommends the device it can fix pushes makers toward openness. An agent built by a company with its own devices to sell may not. Who your agent works for will decide whether this helps consumers or just moves the lock-in somewhere harder to see.
If I made hardware, I’d plan for the firmware I ship to be replaced, and I’d make replacing it easy and safe to undo. Those are the devices agents will recommend.
©  Geoff Stearns"
54,2026-11-15,"Sell a game outside Steam with clear choices between Steam keys, itch.io downloads and your own licensed build. Plan payment, delivery and support.
To sell a game outside Steam, first decide what the buyer receives. A Steam activation code, an itch.io download URL, a downloadable build and a license checked by your application are different products of different systems. Calling all of them a âkeyâ hides the most important part of the purchase.
A separate sales channel can support your own audience, backers or a desktop tool sold alongside a game. It also needs a clear delivery, update and support process. A payment button by itself does not replace the services your current build depends on.
A Steam key grants access through Steam to the relevant platform package. It is requested through Steamworks, not generated by PermitCore. Selling it on your own website still leaves the buyer with a Steam entitlement and the associated platform requirements.
Read the current Steam Key rules and guidelines before planning inventory, discounts, backer rewards or release timing. Requests can be reviewed or declined; do not promise buyers an unlimited supply of future keys. The guidance addresses comparable treatment of Steam customers and distinguishes retail keys from testing access. Release-state override keys are intended for limited testing, not retail sales.
An itch.io download key is a private URL granting access to a project's files. It is not a PermitCore license and does not automatically activate a game on Steam. itch.io also supports separately supplied external keys under its own rules and stock limits.
Its current guidance normally expects downloadable project files when external keys are offered; read the documented exceptions rather than assuming a key-only listing will qualify. The creator FAQ explains its hosting and distribution model. itch.io does not add DRM to your uploaded files. Any licensing code already inside your build is your own integration.
You can distribute a build that does not ask the player to validate a license. Decide how buyers obtain updates and recover their downloads. A restricted download page controls distribution; it does not prevent a downloaded file from being copied. Explain the usage terms and the actual delivery process without suggesting a private link makes the binary unshareable.
You can distribute an independent build whose protected behavior checks a PermitCore entitlement. Your app integrates the SDK, checks the expected product and enforces the result. That key is not redeemable on Steam, and it does not create an itch.io purchase.
PermitCore does not mint Steam keys or provide a built-in Steam entitlement bridge. If you offer more than one kind of access, explain and operate each delivery path explicitly. Do not imply that a PermitCore Store purchase automatically adds a game to another platform's library.
State the supported operating systems, edition, download source, required account or launcher, device allowance, offline behavior and included updates. If the purchase is an independent build, say so beside the price. If it includes a platform-issued key, say which platform and when the code becomes available.
Confirm distribution rights for the game and its bundled assets. Review applicable platform and publisher agreements for a separate build; a generic licensing service cannot decide those rights for you. Keep your checkout description, game UI, support page and refund policy consistent.
Inventory platform-dependent features too. Cloud saves, achievements, workshop content, multiplayer identity and a platform backend do not become independent merely because you change the license check. Test the actual build without the dependencies you intend to remove.
For your own build, product-scoped license features can distinguish access such as a full campaign or an editing tool. The application must gate the corresponding work. A flag does not create Steam DLC ownership or automatically distribute another platform's content.
A public demo can remain usable without a paid key, with the full edition unlocked only after a valid entitlement check. If you use a trial, explain its expiry and the recovery or upgrade path. Early-access and backer promises should specify which build, updates and support are included rather than relying on an ambiguous âlifetime keyâ.
See feature-based licensing and the trial guide for the existing controls. A feature name is an application policy input; adding it in Admin does not implement the game feature for you.
For supported targets and plans, configure trusted public verification keys, a stable device identity and the intended signed offline policy before disconnecting. SDK 2.1.1 does not trust a public key merely because it sits beside a token in an editable cache. No configured trust keys means no cached authorization.
Check product, device and signed deadline as well as the signature. Offline proof does not renew a floating session, discover a refund or immediately learn about revocation. Nor does it make server-dependent game features work offline. A copied identity or patched client remains a separate limitation.
Test a clean installation, restart, suspended session, lost connection and expired proof on your actual Windows, macOS, Linux or handheld build as applicable. A portable library compile is not Steam Deck, console or Unity target certification. Unity WebGL uses the online validation path; consult the Unity reference for target-specific limits.
The offline setup reference and shutdown-planning guide help separate access promises from services the game still needs.
A vendor Store uses one saved provider: Stripe, Paddle or Lemon Squeezy. Choose and configure that account for the whole Store. PermitCore takes no Store commission; provider fees and the terms of your own agreement still apply. Paddle merchant-of-record purchases use provider-issued payment documents. A Stripe setup has its own seller and tax responsibilities; review the provider comparison and current provider terms.
Paddle includes games in its supported software categories, subject to its acceptable-use policy and seller/product review. Confirm approval for your own account and offering before launch. A donation or crowdfunding campaign is not automatically an approved software purchase. Connecting a Store does not bypass that process.
After canonical paid evidence is verified, PermitCore issues the configured license and queues its delivery email. The success redirect alone is not payment evidence, and a queued email is not proof that the inbox received it. Test delivery and give buyers an account/support recovery path.
Existing purchases and subscriptions retain their original provider binding when a vendor changes the Store selection. Test the intended renewal, cancellation and refund behavior. A credit-only or zero-total subscription update is not a new paid game sale. The payment lifecycle guide covers those boundaries.
Configure the underlying product's download URL for your release host when appropriate. The Store can include that link in delivery email; it is not a game CDN or automatic patching service. Operate file hosting, update delivery and download-access controls for your build.
This is the existing Store and SDK integration flow. It does not create an external platform key, install an updater or configure your game's protected features automatically. Follow the Store reference for account setup and the customer portal reference for supported self-service.
Tell the buyer where to find its purchase, license and download, how to replace a device and whom to contact when delivery fails. A Store customer account is not automatically an authenticated identity inside your game. Choose the portal access policy deliberately before sharing a team key.
Removing a device or refunding a purchase does not uninstall a binary already downloaded or erase every offline proof. Your app must enforce the chosen validity policy. Read the key-sharing guide for realistic limits rather than treating an always-online prompt as a complete anti-piracy system.
Create a free PermitCore account, issue a test license, and validate it from your application.

					By Anthony D'Alessandro

	When it came to Johnny Depp’s comeback movie Ebenezer, moviegoers abroad weren’t as cheap as stateside audiences who gave it a discount with a $19M opening. Foreign audiences shelled out $23M in 62 markets for a $42M global opening, which in Scrooge and Paramount’s ledgers is good enough for a movie that cost a net $38M before global P&A spend. 

	Comps are hard here since we haven’t had a ton of mega major studio live-action Christmas movies post Covid (there was Red One which had a reported $26.6M offshore start in 75 markets). At $23M abroad, Ebenezer is ahead of such Halloween-themed movies as Goosebumps ($20.7M foreign opening in like-for-likes), The House With a Clock in its Walls ($19M) and Haunted Mansion ($16.7M foreign start)

He was No. 2 in Germany ($1.5M at 512 sites where he was beat by a local title), in addition to Brazil ($475K at 589 locations), Netherlands ($416K at 103 locations) and Colombia ($262K at 204 sites). 	Market-specific initiatives included a Day of the Dead-themed marigold activation in Mexico City, Ghost of Christmas Past appearances in Spain and Italy, holiday-themed mall and cinema takeovers in Germany, and a collaboration with Japanese comedian Jun Itoda.

	Sony and Prime Focus Studio’s Ramayana: Rise of a Legend saw its global box office jump to $147.5M with a $38.1M second global weekend, a majority of that coming from abroad with $32.6M. After 11 days in release, the Namit Malhotra directed movie is the seventh highest Indian movie of all-time. 

	The overseas rollout included six new markets pushing the overall foreign total to 42. That included Indonesia ($573K), Cambodia ($286K), and Mexico with record-breaking openings for an Indian film.

	India led all markets with a cume of $97.2M powered by the highest second weekend in local history (₹223 crore / $23.6M). Other top-grossing international markets to-date include the UK ($5.5M), United Arab Emirates ($4.7M), Australia ($4.2M), Thailand ($1.5M), Malaysia ($1.4M), and Germany ($1.1M).

Add that second weekend to domestic’s big drop of $8.3M and we’re at an estimated $21.5M second global frame, -66% from its previous No. 1 $63M global opening. Running global cume here is $104.8M on the movie which cost before global marketing around $20M. 

	Warner Bros Pictures Animation’s The Cat in the Hat posted a global weekend of $17M after a great hold stateside of $11.4M, -43% with foreign only delivering $5.6M. Running total is $55.8M WW with $38.2M from North America and $17.6M offshore. Top market totals are Mexico ($2.1M), Spain ($1.8M), Italy ($1.8M), Poland ($1.2M) and China ($1M) — nothing that dazzling for a family at the international B.O.

	Laika’s Wildwood has risen its global cume to $70M after a $3M foreign weekend from FilmNation sold territories and another $4.5M from its fourth domestic weekend. Running foreign total is $22M with several territories left to open including France on Dec. 9 and $48.4M from Fathom’s domestic release. The movie is bound to overtake Travis Knight’s previous 2016 Laika stop-motion movie, Kubo and the Two Strings ($76.2M WW).
