57,2026-11-15,"It's hard to believe that only 60 years ago, American women didnât have the same rights we do today.
Many opportunities and freedoms we consider normal in 2026, like taking maternity leave or getting a credit card, werenât available to women in the 1960s. Some rights existed, but, more often than not, required a manâs permission. Others, such as serving on a jury or being hired without gender bias, were entirely off the table in some states.
Several of these rights werenât legally granted to women across the United States until well into the 1970s, with some states and workplaces moving toward equality faster than others.
While these rights seem standard now, single women, mothers, grandmothers, and great-grandmothers alive today once lived in a world without them.
Here are six things American women were not legally permitted to do in the 1960s.Â 
Before the Equal Credit Opportunity Act of 1974, banks turned women away from receiving loans, mortgages, or credit cards. It was not unheard of for a financial institution to refuse a woman who requested these services unless a man was present to co-sign.
Unmarried women were denied most often, but a father, brother, or male relative could co-sign. Married women needed their husbandsâ signatures.
Before the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978, women could be fired or told to resign if they became pregnant and asked for time off to care for their child.
After Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 was passed, pregnant women were legally required to be treated like other employees. Pregnancy and childbirth were categorized as short-term disability, granting women similar benefits and leave.
Many Ivy League colleges did not admit women until the late 1960s and early 1970s.
According to CNN, the University of Pennsylvania and Cornell accepted female students âon a case-by-case basisâ and âunder special circumstancesâ as early as the 1870s. Princeton accepted its first female student in 1969; Brown in 1971; Dartmouth in 1972; Harvard in 1977; and Columbia in 1981.
These institutions started admitting women after Title IX of the Education Amendments of 1972 was passed. For Harvard and Columbia, merging with their all-women colleges, Radcliffe and Barnard, took more time.
The 19th Amendment to the U.S. Constitution guaranteed white women the right to vote, but many states still did not permit women to serve on juries. The state governments were responsible for legalizing womenâs jury service. Wyoming and Utah allowed women to serve on juries in the late 1800s, and other states slowly followed suit until 1973, when all 50 states allowed it.Â 
But why were women kept off juries in the first place? According to CNN, women were considered primary caregivers at home and thought to be too âfragileâ and âsympatheticâ to remain objective regarding the offenses presented in court.
Not only were women denied maternity leave until the late 1970s, but they could also be turned away from jobs simply because of their sex, at least until the Civil Rights Act of 1964 made it illegal to discriminate based on race, sex, or religion.
When it first passed, this law was loosely enforced, and many women were still excluded from male-dominated fields such as law, medicine, engineering, and architecture. Additional protections, like the Pregnancy Discrimination Act of 1978 and the Pregnant Workers Fairness Act of 2022 (yes, four years ago), furthered workplace rights over time.
The FDA approved the birth control pill in 1957, but initially, it could only be prescribed for âsevere menstrual distress.â In 1960, it became available as a contraceptive but remained illegal in some states and was generally prescribed to married women for âfamily planningâ purposes, following 1965âs Griswold v. Connecticut.
Many pharmacies didnât even stock birth control, considering it âimmoral,â according to CNN. It wasnât until 1972, with Eisenstadt v. Baird, that unmarried couples could legally use contraceptives.

58,2026-11-15,"by Heide Lazaro
Magnific/Reddit
Hotel room rates are usually based on availability and cannot always be negotiated.
The following story is about a front desk employee who welcomed a guest during a night when they weren’t sold out.
The guest kept demanding a lower price despite being offered the best available rate at $150 and $160 per night.
So she was surprised by how the guest treated the hotel rates like flea market pricing.
Can you believe that? Let’s take a closer look!
For context, this is a nicer hotel in a good area. Our rooms average $120+ a night.
We’re sitting at about 85% occupancy on this shift.
Our rooms are running about 150 to 160, depending on the time, with the shiny member discount.
This front desk employee informed the guest that the hotel wasn’t sold out for the night.
A middle-aged guy walks in, staring at his phone.
He stops in front of the desk, and this is how the conversation goes.
Me: “Hi, how can I help you?”
Him: “Are you sold out?”
Me: “Nope, not tonight.”
Him: “What’s the best deal you can give me? I’m a super shiny.”
The guest started negotiating.
Me: “Okay, perfect. It’ll be $150 for a king and $160 for a double queen.”
Him (still hasn’t looked up at all, by the way): “What about $110?”
Me: “I’m sorry?”
Him: “What about $110 for the night?”
Me: “No, the rate is $150 or $160.”
She stood firm that their rates weren’t negotiable.
Him: “Okay, fine. What about $120?”
Me: “Sir, I can’t change our rate. This is the best deal we can offer you.”
He then scoffs and leaves.
I didn’t realize this was a flea market where you could haggle. 😐
Wow! Some guests really do treat hotel front desks like they’re shopping at a street market.

Being a loyalty member might come with discounts, but it doesn’t mean employees can simply lower the published rate whenever someone asks.
OP clearly gave the guest all the available options, so there wasn’t much else to negotiate.

This one shares their personal experience.

Here’s a valid point.

If you enjoyed this story, check out this post about a person who changed their minds about donated items, only to return to the shop to find them all sold.
This one makes sense, too.

Finally, another one chimes in.

Just because you’re a loyal member doesn’t mean you can decide the room rate you want to pay.

The logo of Intel at the company's booth at the 9th China International Import Expo (CIIE) in Shanghai, China, November 5, 2026. REUTERS/Go Nakamura
Nov 15 : Intel has been sued by shareholders who accused the US chipmaker of defrauding them by concealing the impact of a prolonged slowdown in personal computer sales that caused its stock price to plunge nearly 29 per cent in a single day.
In a proposed class action filed on Friday in the San Jose, California, federal court, shareholders led by a Michigan pension fund said Intel was too slow to disclose information about how slowing PC sales and an inventory glut would weigh on revenue.
The shareholders said Intel's problems with PC sales and excess inventory outweighed data center construction and other benefits related to the artificial intelligence boom.
They also said Intel had the motive and opportunity to defraud by raising capital in mid-August, halfway through its ""disastrous"" third quarter, when it sold $23 billion of stock at $95 per share.

Shares of Intel fell 28.6 per cent on October 30, wiping out about $140 billion of market value, one day after the Santa Clara, California-based company projected lower-than-expected fourth-quarter revenue and profit and forecast a prolonged slowdown in PC sales.
Tan told analysts that he was disappointed by manufacturing supply shortages, an ""unprecedented memory crunch,"" and resulting softness in PCs. 
""We need to address these challenges with urgency,"" Tan added.

60,2026-11-15,"My Galaxy S10+ has plenty of useful audio features, but its built-in equalizer's handful of sliders could only do so much for my music. I could boost the bass or brighten up the vocals, but I wanted more precise control over the sound coming through my AirPods Pro 2. That’s how I ended up installing RootlessJamesDSP, a free Android app with so many audio controls that the stock equalizer looks like it’s barely pulling its weight.
It's that one setting you need to tweak.
RootlessJamesDSP is a free, open-source audio app that works without rooting your phone. It grabs the sound from compatible apps and adds processing that goes well beyond Android’s built-in audio effects. The catch is that setup takes a bit more work.
You can download it from the Google Play Store or F-Droid. On Android 11 or newer, the easiest way to set it up without a computer is with Shizuku. You can also use ADB from a PC.
When prompted, allow audio capture, play a song in YouTube Music, and make sure processing is turned on. After you restart your phone, you’ll usually just need to restart Shizuku’s wireless service. You won’t have to go through the whole app setup again.
RootlessJamesDSP can't process audio from every app. YouTube Music, Apple Music, and Deezer are supported, but regular Spotify, Chrome, and SoundCloud block the audio capture it needs.
The Multimodal equalizer gives you 15 different sound sliders and a visual graph where you can drag points around to shape your audio. I bumped up the low end (raising 63Hz by 3.3dB and 100Hz by 3.1dB) while leaving everything else alone. That gave the bass a lot more kick, though the coolest feature was actually hidden under Arbitrary response equalizer > Magnitude response > AutoEQ profiles. Some Android music players already offer powerful equalizers, but RootlessJamesDSP lets me apply these adjustments to compatible streaming apps without switching music players.
AutoEQ uses frequency-response measurements of different headphone models to create presets that bring their sound closer to a target curve. Search for your headphones, choose a matching profile, and load it. My AirPods Pro 2 preset contained 127 adjustment points, far more precise than anything I could dial in with Samsung's basic sliders.
While playing a song on YouTube Music, I flipped the effect on and off at the exact same volume to compare. To my ears, the adjusted audio sounded noticeably cleaner and punchier. Of course, that comes down to personal taste rather than magically unlocking hidden details in the music. Just make sure you use a profile made for your actual headphones; using an AirPods Pro preset on a completely different pair of earbuds won't give you the same results.
RootlessJamesDSP also includes a separate Dynamic bass boost setting. I could hear the low end get noticeably deeper as I turned up the max gain, and it stayed clean up to around 7dB during my test. Pushing it any higher started to make the audio sound blown out and distorted. Start with the lowest boost setting and increase it gradually while replaying the same part of a song. More bass doesn't automatically mean better bass.
If you've already applied an AutoEQ profile, be careful about piling extra bass boosts on top. You can end up pushing the signal into distortion, so start with one effect and adjust the output gain if necessary.
The spatial controls had surprises too. While listening to Stevie Wonder's As, increasing Soundstage wideness made the percussion crashes easier to distinguish. Convolver applies an impulse response to simulate an acoustic or spatial character. Its built-in CorredHRTF_Surround2 preset helped me pick out different instruments, while Church scattered the music into an unpleasant wash of reverberation.
Crossfeed also changes the stereo presentation by blending some sound between the left and right channels. I noticed more of a difference with its stronger presets, but the Dynamic range compander barely changed anything with my settings. I left it off rather than enabling effects I couldn't appreciate.
In Settings, turning on Per-device profiles gives each connected speaker or headset its own setup. I tested it by disconnecting and reconnecting my AirPods, and the app brought back their AutoEQ curve on its own. The processing also kept going after I swiped RootlessJamesDSP out of Recent apps. Suspend audio pipeline while idle can pause the processing when nothing’s playing, and the app lets you back up your settings if you’ve built up several profiles.
I did notice somewhat quicker battery drain during listening, although I didn't benchmark it. That's one reason to avoid running processing unnecessarily. The app can also conflict with other audio-effects apps, including Wavelet, while added latency can make it less attractive for games or time-sensitive video.
This is an app for people who like to tinker. If all you want is a quick bass boost, Samsung’s equalizer may be plenty. But if you stick to supported music apps and want to tune your headphones more precisely, there’s a lot here to dig into.
Make your TV speakers better before you get a soundbar or home theater system.
I doubt I'll spend every listening session moving frequency points around. Once I've found a setup that suits my earbuds, I can leave it running and get back to the music. And when an old favorite reveals an instrument I'd barely noticed before, I don't mind having an excuse to tinker again.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

61,2026-11-15,"
  By AJ Eustace |
  at November 15, 2026 9:12am CDT  
Reports indicate that right-hander Jonathan Loaisiga is receiving widespread interest in free agency. The Dodgers were the most recent entrant into his market alongside the Yankees, Mets, and Blue Jays. It appears the Phillies have also entered the fray for Loaisiga’s services, as reported by Jon Heyman of the New York Post. That makes at least five known suitors for the 32-year-old reliever, whom MLBTR projects to earn a two-year, $13MM deal.
Loaisiga is coming off his best season in five years. Despite having a length injury history, including IL stints in every season from 2019-25, he finally stayed off the IL in 2026 and delivered 67 innings out of the Diamondbacks’ bullpen. That was a nice surprise considering Loaisiga entered Spring Training as a non-roster invitee. Even better, he delivered a stellar 2.42 earned run average, 56.4% ground ball rate, and 5.0% walk rate, making him one of Arizona’s best relievers and a key upside play for bullpen-needy clubs in free agency.
That Loaisiga managed a 67-inning total is impressive considering he combined for just 51 1/3 innings from 2023-25 with the Yankees. Since breaking out with a 2.17 ERA in 70 2/3 innings back in 2021, Loaisiga had missed time for shoulder inflammation, elbow inflammation, a torn UCL (which required season-ending surgery in April 2024), and mid-back tightness. From 2022-25, he had a 3.81 ERA in just under 100 innings with a subpar 9.4% K-BB rate but an excellent 55.5% ground ball rate.
There were a lot of positives in Loaisiga’s performance this year beyond the improved health. After working with slightly diminished velocity on his trademark sinker in 2025, he bumped it back up to 97.9 mph with the D-backs, which was right in line with his career norms. According to Statcast, Loaisiga ranked in the 92nd percentile or better by hard-hit rate (31.7%), chase rate (35.9%), barrel rate (3.5%), and average exit velocity (85.2 mph). Every one of his pitches had a positive run value, led by the four-seamer at +10 RV.
The Phillies ranked eighth in the Majors with 5.6 fWAR out of their relievers this year. Most of that came from flamethrowing closer Jhoan Duran, who earned 2.9 fWAR with a career-best 1.82 ERA and 31.4% K-BB rate in 61 appearances. Tim Mayza and Jonathan Bowlan were solid, but José Alvarado, Orion Kerkering, and Chase Shugart posted subpar run prevention and ground ball rates.
Loaisiga would solve both problems on a relatively inexpensive deal. As MLBTR’s Leo Morgenstern noted in our Offseason Outlook series, the Phillies could probably use three relievers but may focus more on bolstering the lineup, especially by adding starting-caliber outfielders. Our Payroll Tracker has the team at a $265MM cash payroll for 2027, leaving around $26.8MM to spend before they match last year’s total.
Photo courtesy of David Frerker, Imagn Images

62,2026-11-15,"Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia CEO on Thursday, following an extramarital affair scandal and a board investigation into concerns that he may have been the subject of a ""foreign intelligence effort.""
It ends his reign at the chip company he cofounded in 1993. Over 33 years, Huang oversaw Nvidia's meteoric rise from a scrappy chipmaker to a multitrillion-dollar AI powerhouse.
Founders being kicked out of their own companies is more common than you might think.
Huang is in good company. Steve Jobs, Elon Musk, and Sam Altman of OpenAI are among the CEOs who have been ousted from the firms they founded.
Some cases stem from minor disagreements and plunging stock prices, but some CEOs felt betrayed, launched lawsuits, or were arrested.
Here are 11 tech founders who were cast out from the companies they built:
In 1985, nine years after cofounding Apple, Steve Jobs exited the company.
Slumping sales and Jobs' demanding management style led to a clash with CEO John Sculley. The board stripped Jobs, who was nominally Apple's chairman, of his duties, and he resigned.
Jobs then founded another computer company, NeXT, which Apple acquired in 1996 for $427 million. He became Apple CEO the following year.
Sam Altman cofounded OpenAI as a nonprofit in 2015 and was its cochair alongside Elon Musk. He became CEO four years later and was the face of the AI boom when ChatGPT launched in 2022.
In late 2023, the board ousted Altman, saying it had lost confidence in his ability to lead the company and that he was not ""consistently candid in his communications.""
After employees and investors revolted, Altman was reinstated just four days later.
Jerry Yang cofounded Yahoo while he was a Stanford student and later became CEO in 2007.
Then, Microsoft offered to buy the company for over $44 billion, a substantial premium on its market value. However, Yang did not want to sell. After negotiations collapsed, Yahoo's share price dropped as much as 20%.
The board faced criticism for its handling of the failed deal, with Yang being pressured to resign as CEO. He stayed at Yahoo in a different role until 2012.
Jack Dorsey has the rare accolade of being pushed out of the same company twice.
In 2008, his Twitter cofounder, Evan Williams, fired him as CEO.
Dorsey went on to start Square, the payments processor, before he was brought back as interim CEO in 2015. Not long afterward, Twitter dropped the ""interim"" from his job title.
In 2020, the activist investor firm Elliott Management criticized Dorsey's divided attention between Twitter and Square, and pushed for his removal. Dorsey stepped down as CEO the following year.
Sam Bankman-Fried became a poster boy for crypto, and his net worth peaked at $26 billion after founding the crypto exchange FTX.
In 2022, CoinDesk reported that his trading firm, Alameda Research, had a balance sheet largely made up of FTX's own crypto token.
Those close ties panicked investors, who rushed to withdraw their money from the crypto exchange — which didn't have enough to cover the withdrawals, partly due to lavish spending by FTX executives.
FTX filed for bankruptcy, and Bankman-Fried was replaced by John J. Ray III, an attorney who specializes in recovering funds from failed corporations, including Enron.
Bankman-Fried was sentenced to 25 years in prison in 2024.
Elon Musk founded X.com as a payments processor in 1999. A year later, it merged with Peter Thiel's Confinity, which created PayPal.
The merged company initially kept the name X.com, with Musk as CEO.
Leadership clashed with Musk over the name, his management style, and his desire to rewrite the platform's main infrastructure.
While Musk was away on his honeymoon, Thiel and his cofounder Max Levchin organized a letter of no confidence. The board voted to replace Musk and install Thiel as CEO.
Martin Eberhard cofounded Tesla in 2003, and Elon Musk joined the company the following year.
Although Eberhard had served as CEO since the company's inception, he was ousted in 2007. He later told Business Insider that Musk phoned him and said the board had met without him and decided he should step down.
Eberhard sued Tesla in 2009, accusing Musk of defamation and pushing him out of the company. The suit was settled.
Travis Kalanick cofounded Uber in 2009 and became CEO the following year.
His leadership saw a string of scandals, and the company was criticized for a culture of bullying and misogyny. Some executives expensed visits to strip clubs, The New York Times reporter Mike Isaac reported in his book ""Super Pumped: The Battle for Uber.""
In 2017, Uber lost hundreds of thousands of customers from a viral #DeleteUber campaign following accusations that the company intended to profit from President Donald Trump's travel ban.
After a former engineer, Susan Fowler, alleged sexual harassment and HR failures, Uber launched an independent investigation into its workplace culture, which led to Kalanick's resignation.
As depicted by Andrew Garfield in ""The Social Network,"" Eduardo Saverin cofounded Facebook with Mark Zuckerberg while they were at Harvard. He served as chief financial officer until he was squeezed out of the startup in 2005.
After Peter Thiel invested $500,000, the company was restructured, and the board issued millions of new shares. This led to Saverin's 30% stake being diluted to less than 10%.
Zuckerberg wrote in an email at the time and obtained by Business Insider in 2012: ""Is there a way to do this without making it painfully apparent to him that he's being diluted to 10%?""
Saverin sued. The lawsuit was settled out of court in 2009, restoring his title as cofounder and leaving him with a stake worth over $2 billion when Facebook went public.
Andrew Mason started Groupon in 2008 as a company that sells discounts on local businesses.
It had a lot of buzz early on, but after it went public in 2011, it started floundering. The share price fell some 90% from its peak.
CNBC's Herb Greenberg named Andrew Mason 2012's ""Worst CEO of the Year,"" criticizing his ""goofball antics, which can come off more like a big kid than company leader.""
In early 2013, Mason was fired by the board.
Palmer Luckey designed the prototypes for the Oculus Rift virtual-reality headset and raised $2.4 million through a Kickstarter campaign. His company was acquired by Facebook in 2014 for $2 billion, and is now known as Meta's Reality Labs division.
In 2016, Luckey donated to a pro-Trump group that spread anti-Hillary Clinton memes, sparking a backlash. He departed Facebook the following year.
Mark Zuckerberg denied that Luckey was fired for political views.
In a 2025 interview with ""60 Minutes,"" Luckey said, ""Everyone's got a different story, but it boils down to I gave $9,000 to a political group that was for Donald Trump and against Hillary Clinton.""

This version is subject to further edits and will be replaced automatically by the final Version of Record. All legal disclaimers apply.
                        
Countercurrent exchange describes the crossover of some property between two bodies flowing in opposite direction. Countercurrent circuits, which work more efficiently than concurrent circuits, are common in nature and mimicked in human engineering. An example is the beetle cryptonephridial complex (CNC)—a multi-organ water conservation system which makes key contributions to the water budget in a vast number of species. It is even used by some to harvest water from the atmosphere. The system evolved by a radical reorganisation of insect internal anatomy bringing the renal/Malpighian tubules and rectum into countercurrent configuration, and their isolation in a chamber by an unusual tissue of unknown origin—the perinephric membrane. The CNC is richly supplied with tracheae to meet the high metabolic demands of the system. How the CNC is assembled during development is unknown. Here we characterise CNC development in the model beetle Tribolium castaneum and discover the molecular signals orchestrating its assembly. We find that dynamic crosstalk between the developing precursor cells of the rectum, Malpighian tubules, perinephric membrane, and trachea rely on a single, differentially spliced fibroblast growth factor (FGF) receptor and two FGF pathway ligands. We also map the origin of the perinephric membrane to a previously uncharacterised population of mesodermal cells, providing clues to its evolutionary origin. This work illuminates the developmental processes underpinning the reorganisation of the insect internal body plan to assemble a natural countercurrent system, one which is fundamental to beetle physiology, ecology and evolutionary success.
We thank Matt Benton for the Tribolium stock, Anisha Kubasik-Thayil, Adrian Garcia-Burgos and Iain Porter (IMPACT imaging facility, University of Edinburgh) for assistance with imaging, Stephen Mitchell, Martin Singleton and Fernanda Cisneros Soberan (School of Biological Sciences Electron Microscopy unit, University of Edinburgh) for assistance with EM, Vivian Allison and Louise Dunn (CDBS histology facility, University of Edinburgh) for assistance with preparing blocks for EM, and Tracey Davey and Ross Laws (Electron Microscopy Research Services, Newcastle University) for making sections for EM. 	The holiday gifting season is upon us, and there’s a good chance tech products are dominating your loved ones’ wish lists. But there is a wide range of gifts — at a very wide range of price points — if you’re trying to set your giftee up with the latest and greatest gadgets.

	If you’re looking for budget-friendly tech gifts, the home tech category is a great option. Popular home tech products are typically less expensive than gaming consoles, nice headphones, and other staples, but they can be equally as useful for many giftees. One good example is the home security camera lineup offered by Blink. The Amazon brand offers some best-selling security upgrades that start well below $50 and make great gifts for all homemakers.

	Another option is office gear, such as a new printer from industry leaders like HP. This route is especially good for younger people, such as college students or recent grads looking to set up their dorm or first apartment.

	If you’re looking for a more substantial gift, audio and visual tech gifts are typically your best bet. On the audio side, one of our favorites this year is Sennheiser. The German brand has been putting out some great headphones that both audiophiles and average users can appreciate. On the visual side, we’re loving Sony’s cameras for any photographers on your list — especially if they’re relatively new to the art.



	For all tech gifts, Best Buy is a great first stop. The electronics retailer is running some great deals during the holiday season, especially if you sign up for the My Best Buy Membership. This membership offers exclusive pricing, rewards, and extended returns/exchanges, all for the very reasonable price of $29.99 a year.



	Here are four great tech gifts to pick up at Best Buy, starting at $28:



	$27.99



		These little security cameras from Blink punch way above their price point, offering crystal-clear 2K resolution, wide coverage, and an intuitive app experience. The cameras also have two-way audio with noise cancellation, letting you communicate with people in your home while you’re away. Best of all, the two-pack of Blink Mini security cameras is currently available at Best Buy for just $27.99.	



	$89.99



		A great tech gift under $100 is this HP DeskJet 2955 all-in-one printer. It’s compact and easy to fit in most workspaces, but can still produce high-quality prints, scans, and copies. You also get HP’s AI technology that helps correct layouts, avoid mistakes, and alter content.	



	$399.99



		Sennheiser is putting out some of the best headphones on the market right now, including these Momentum 5s. The brand’s flagship consumer noise-canceling cans are built around 42-millimeter dynamic drivers that help deliver warm, detailed sound, no matter what genre you’re enjoying. The battery will last up to 57 hours, and, unlike most headphones, it’s also user-replaceable for real longevity.	



	$1,199.99



		If you’re shopping for an aspiring photographer, Sony’s Alpha 6400 is a great way to set them up for success. It’s smaller and more approachable than a traditional DSLR but still delivers exceptional image quality thanks to a powerful 24.2-megapixel Exmor CMOS sensor. Plus, it shoots 4K video and has a tilting touchscreen for getting different angles and easy menu navigation.

Jordan Firstman’s “Club Kid” was the belle of Cannes. Critics raved about Firstman’s feature directorial debut, as well as his sensitive performance as a party promoter turned unlikely dad, and A24 swept in to buy the film for $17 million after a bidding war. But plenty of movies have been embraced at film festivals only to face rejection when they hit the wider world. Would “Club Kid” be another “Dope” or “Begin Again”?




Well, based on a very limited sample size, “Club Kid” seems to be performing just fine, thank you very much. Those raves and A24’s imprimatur of cool are paying off as the film enjoyed the best limited expansion of the year this weekend, grossing $756,060 across 27 screens. That translates into an impressive $28,002 per-screen average. Since opening in six screens on Nov. 6, “Club Kid” has earned nearly $1.5 million. But this very New York story has mostly played in major cities and liberal bastions like the Big Apple, Chicago, Austin, San Francisco, and Los Angeles. Will audiences across the country embrace the story of a drug-taking, sex-positive gay man and his adorable 10-year-old accidental son? We’ll find out on Thanksgiving when it goes wide. 



Related Stories Box Office: ‘Ebenezer’ Opens to Modest $19 Million, Showing Johnny Depp Is Still a Draw for the Right Price ‘Fatherland’ Wins Golden Frog at ToruńCAMERIMAGE 2026 — Full Winners List 
In order for “Club Kid” to be a financial success, it will need to more than double the $17 million that A24 shelled out for the rights, because theaters keep half the ticket sales. That could happen if “Club Kid” generates awards buzz and positive word-of-mouth spreads, something that seems plausible given how funny, touching, and life-affirming Firstman’s film is (seriously, audiences have been applauding as the credits roll). Plus, A24 is on a heater this year, releasing hits like “You Can See Everything” and “Backrooms” that have it rivaling the market shares of much larger studios like Warner Bros. and Paramount. 




There are certainly a lot of other Oscar hopefuls out there. Searchlight’s “Wild Horse Nine,” which is seen as a near lock for a Best Picture nod, earned $4.6 million domestically in its second weekend. The film about two CIA agents up to no good in Chile has earned $28 million globally. Then there’s “La Bola Negra,” a multigenerational gay epic from Netflix. It’s earned $3.5 million domestically over five weeks in theaters. And “The Only Living Pickpocket in New York,” which is generating Oscar buzz for John Turturro, picked up $322,830 to bring its domestic haul to $4.7 million after four weeks in theaters. 
“Wicker,” with its story of a fisherwoman who becomes romantically entangled with a wicker man, may be too weird to generate a much love from Academy voters, but it has done well for its distributor, Black Bear, earning $1.5 million in North America last weekend. Its global total stands at $36.7 million after four weeks of release. 
As for “Club Kid,” A24 thinks its trajectory mirrors that of other recent arthouse hits, with ticket sales pacing alongside those of “Moonlight” ($67 million gross) and “Anora” ($59.3 million gross) at this point in its expansion.  Exit scores are among the best in the studio’s history and the film is connecting with young audiences as 80% of ticket buyers were under the age of 35. Next weekend will be another test for “Club Kid” as it expands to 200 screens across the top 50 markets. If it keeps drawing crowds, Firstman and A24 will have a lot to celebrate.

Former “Saturday Night Live” repertory player and writer Bowen Yang returned to help open the show this weekend with his portrayal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang.
In the cold open, the nation’s most prominent tech-industry CEOs, including Elon Musk and Mark Zuckerberg, are at a CIA gathering devised to teach them how to identify when a woman may be duping them.
“America needs you to learn the difference between a woman who would actually want to have sex with you and a woman who is clearly a spy,” said a CIA guide, portrayed by comedian John Mulaney, the night’s host.
Mulaney, a former “SNL” writer, used the story of Huang, who resigned from Nvidia on Thursday following reports that he had extramarital relationships, including one with a suspected spy for China.
“What do you think the lesson is here?” Mulaney asked.
Zuckerberg, portrayed by Andrew Dismukes, answered: “Never date a human woman.”
Mulaney dismissed the response and those of a few of the other tech titans in the room, prompting PayPal co-founder Peter Thiel, portrayed by James Austin Johnson, to defend the group.
“Show us some respect,” he said. “We do a lot for this country. Name one time technology has let us down.”
Mulaney answered by mentioning Friday’s nationwide flight ground stop and Thursday’s global Microsoft glitch.
He tried to empathize, however, noting that it’s a rare time in human history when men “as pale and as strange as you” even survive, let alone possess “all the world’s wealth and power.”
Mulaney asked the group to remember the people they dated before they were rich and famous. “Now all of a sudden someone very beautiful is interested in you. Why do you think that might be?” he said. 
Zuckerberg chimed in. “Because she’s comforted by my Meta glasses recording her at all times.”
“No,” Mulaney said.
He later introduces someone “who learned this lesson the hard way.”
Enter Huang.
“It’s me, Jensen ‘Hide Your Wife’ Huang,” Yang said.
He explained that he’s into BDSM — “breakin’ da stock market” — and urged the men not to be like him.
“Women don’t like guys like us,” he said. “That’s how we became guys like us.”
Yang appeared in a subsequent sketch, “Little Park of Horrors,” a spoof of Broadway musicals, that centered on the city outlawing horse-drawn carriages and the two men (Marcello Hernández and Kam Patterson) who want to ride in one.
Eventually realizing the dangers and limitations, the pair opt for a vehicle ride in the Lincoln Tunnel only to be confronted by Yang’s Mary Todd Lincoln, who sings about the pitfalls of such a subterranean journey.
“No way it’s taking less than two hours,” Lincoln sings. “And it won’t be for free. I’m sayin,’ I’m sayin’ that you and you and you — you’re going to pay me.”
A fan favorite, Yang started as a writer in 2018 and joined the cast the next year. He left “SNL” in December 2025, and made his Broadway debut in September as Lincoln in playwright Cole Escola’s “Oh, Mary!”
The night’s musical guest was Phoebe Bridgers.
“SNL” airs on NBC, a division of NBCUniversal, which is also the parent company of NBC News.
Dennis Romero is a breaking news reporter for NBC News Digital.

President Donald Trump’s unusual silence over the nationwide air travel meltdown drew mounting criticism Saturday as thousands of stranded passengers faced another day of disruptions while the president continued posting about golf courses and other subjects.
Trump has not publicly addressed Friday’s nationwide ground stop, which brought air traffic across the United States to a halt following a massive communications breakdown.
CNN’s Kaitlan Collins called the president’s silence “uncharacteristic” in a video, noting that Trump had found time to post about California Gov. Gavin Newsom, Hollywood and renovations to a Washington golf course.
CNN anchor Jake Tapper also questioned the president’s priorities Saturday
“Trump has posted on social media a bunch of times since the FAA/nationwide airline crisis yesterday afternoon,” he wrote on X. “Not once about the FAA/nationwide airline crisis. Reminiscent of his midterms ‘closing argument’ about the reflecting pool.”
The White House told CNN that Trump has been receiving constant updates on the crisis.
Trump continued posting about unrelated subjects over the weekend, including a message claiming the stock market lost $3 trillion following the midterm elections that swept Democrats back into power on Capitol Hill.
Former Biden White House official Jesse Lee tore into the Trump administration.
“Trump’s Cabinet is in utter meltdown — and Trump himself is focused on golf courses…” Lee wrote.
Podcaster Jeff Storobinsky called Trump’s silence “very strange,” adding that it was “still bizarre especially when the president is posting about golf courses.”
Foreign policy reporter Lauren Rosen also weighed in, calling Trump’s absence “odd,” while Amy Siskind, an activist and author, delivered her own take.
“Recall, this is a man who never is afraid to grab a microphone or call into a show in progress,” she posted. “Simply strange and out of character.
”There has never been a camera Donald Trump couldn't find,"" MeidasTouch added on X. ""Then a national emergency hits and suddenly he's nowhere. Where is the President?""

70,2026-11-15,"This is a Mint Premium article gifted to you.Subscribe to enjoy similar stories.
Let’s not despair too much over the fall of Jenson Huang. American business has several candidates lined up to take his place as the next masked heroes of the business world. Elon Musk and Sam Altman are already in the fray, and there’s also Dario Amodei, who will be a serious contender post Anthropic’s trillion-dollar IPO.
American fascination with superheroes has long spilt into its business sphere. A pop culture history littered with the likes of Superman, Batman, Spider-Man, Wonder Woman, Captain America, Iron Man and Wolverine was bound to embrace the world of business and technology, since they control how we live, work, communicate and fight. In such a world, technology founders have been turned into superheroes, a trend that started with Steve Jobs and continues with Huang and Musk.
The problem is that real-life superheroes have weaknesses, blind spots and egos. The markets, boards of their companies, and employees may refuse to accept that, which is why questioning the superheroes is akin to sacrilege. Yet, when the mask slips, and it invariably does, these men, and occasional women, are revealed to have feet of clay.
Also read: Jensen Huang scandal: Nvidia India staff lose $600 million on paper
A paper titled Social Psychology and the Comic-Book Superhero: A Darwinian Approach by Carney J., Dunbar R., Machin A., David-Barrett T., Silva Junior M., offers an unusual explanation for our fascination with superheroes. It suggests that they help us make sense of a world that is now far too complex for most people. The failure of institutions to manage events leads us to such fictional characters who can save us from doom.
Into this world enter business superheroes with grand promises that go well beyond the mundane task of selling their products for exponential gain. Musk is saving humanity by taking us to Mars, while Altman’s mission is to give everyone on Earth a personal Artificial General Intelligence (AGI). Dario’s best-case scenario includes alleviating disease, poverty, and inequality, and making liberal democracy more effective. Zuckerberg's 2017 manifesto was titled Building a Global Community That Works for Everyone. This is the everything-for-everyone promise, and Huang’s leather jacket, Steve Jobs’s turtleneck, and Mark Zuckerberg’s hoodie all serve as the capes needed for such unselfish acts. The wonder is we all buy into that myth.
Look at how, ever since AI caught the imagination of the markets, global investors have showered their love on Nvidia or more precisely Huang, the man who made it all possible. Institutional and retail investors have fallen over each other to buy Nvidia shares, leading to the most dramatic expansion in any company's market value in history.
Nvidia’s board, in turn, that collection of wise men and women expected to provide the necessary oversight, did all it could to further the Huang as superhero myth. For decades since its birth, Nvidia didn’t have an independent Board chairman because who needed one when you had Huang. Not surprisingly, the board remained steadfast in its support for their hero even after The New York Times first reported his alleged relationship with Chinese citizen Alice Huang. Only when another affair, this time with a company employee, surfaced, did the board act, announcing his exit and hastily appointing its first-ever chairman.
But even in this final act, it refused to call out the man. Its statement announcing Huang’s departure stinks of pusillanimity, thanking him for his ‘profound’ contributions and ‘immeasurable achievements’. It’s the board’s way of telling Huang, it's not you, it's us.
This isn't a board looking to do the right thing, but one seeking its own redemption after ignoring the issue for months, even when there was concrete evidence warranting an investigation. According to a Wall Street Journal report, six months ago, Culper Research published a report raising questions about Nvidia’s business in China and casting doubts over an ‘uncomfortably close’ relationship between Huang and Alice Huang. The 40-page report said it plainly enough: “We are short Nvidia for one reason: the company has a significant China problem.” No one, not the US administration, not the markets and not the company’s board, paid any attention to that research report.
Also read: Why China will likely lose the most from the former Nvidia CEO’s fall from grace
That shouldn’t surprise us. We've seen it before. A lone voice rising against a powerful business, being ignored or drowned in a welter of accusations ranging from “they are just short sellers out to make a killing” or “they are enemies of progress”, etc. In the cacophony of protective outrage, the original problem is conveniently lost. Until, as has happened with Huang, it comes back to bite. Following the great reveal, Nvidia shed nearly $500 billion in market value. If you had ploughed your meagre savings into buying Nvidia shares, who would you hold accountable?
Blaming Huang is a cop-out because it is we who turned a brilliant founder into a tech evangelist, global statesman, and saviour of the world, all rolled into one.
Also read: Behind Nvidia affair has a vast Chinese spying machine 
Sundeep Khanna is a regular Mint columnist and author. His new book ""Made in India: The Story of Desh Bandhu Gupta, Lupin and Indian Pharma"", co-authored with Manish Sabharwal, is slated for release in February 2026.

72,2026-11-15,"The scale of the economic disruption caused by AI has led even conservative politicians to talk about universal basic income. But economists aren't convinced, and campaigners think we need to ask deeper questions.
At the German government's 2026 Digital Summit in early November, Chancellor Friedrich Merz was asked by a journalist to square a difficult economic circle: If artificial intelligence could make hundreds of thousands of people redundant in the next few years, might it become necessary for the government to give people a basic income to ease the ensuing economic disaster?
Surprisingly, Merz didn't respond with a flat dismissal — as might be expected from a conservative politician known for his neoliberal, market-friendly views.
""I've always been a bit cautious about this issue in recent years,"" he said, as reported by the news outlet RND. ""However, I'm also well aware that the issue of AI will change our working world so fundamentally that we'll have to give serious thought to many of these issues in the coming years — including the question: Is our productivity not good enough?""
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
What makes this ambiguity notable is that much of Merz's policies since becoming chancellor last year have involved making Germans work more: His government's planned pension reforms would see Germany's retirement age being raised. In July, he announced a crackdown on sick leave. And in spring 2025, having barely taken office, he caused much consternation by criticizing the concept of a ""work-life balance"" and declaring, ""We in this country must work more and more efficiently.""
But those policies seem to contradict the impending realities of the labor market in an AI world.
This week, the head of Germany's Federal Employment Agency, Andrea Nahles, warned that the introduction AI would cost Germany as many 800,000 jobs in the next 15 years. Though she also said AI could eventually create a similar number of jobs, clearly the socioeconomic upheaval would be considerable.
Nahles is not the only leading figure concerned about job losses caused by AI. Karsten Wildberger, Germany's minister for digital transformation and government modernization, takes any opportunity he can to warn that Germany must prepare for the economic disruptions of AI.
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
And even for Wildberger, a former CEO of a massive retail company, part of the future economy might come down to giving people free money. He was convinced, he told the Neue Osnabrücker Zeitung in March, ""that an unconditional basic income can be part of the solution to cushion the upheavals in the labor market.""
Campaigners have cautiously welcomed this new openness from unexpected quarters. ""It shows that in the face of technological transformation we also need to look differently at the question of how we need to secure people's economic stability,"" said Kirsten Herrmann, board member at the nonprofit Mein Grundeinkommen (My Basic Income), which runs various projects examining the social and economic impact of universal basic income (UBI).
""But I also think that individual statements by politicians don't amount to a political change, of course,"" she told DW. ""It just means that we need to enter a serious and constructive debate about what kind of a role a basic income can play.""
The idea of a universal basic income is very old: English Renaissance philosopher Thomas More mentions it in his 1516 book Utopia, for instance. But since the 20th century, it has largely been seen as an idealistic notion adopted by leftists — that is, until some of the world's most high-profile tech bosses latched onto the idea as a way to offset the utopian advances they said AI would bring.
In 2017, Elon Musk, who had co-founded the corporation OpenAI two years earlier, predicted that a universal basic income would at some stage become inevitable. Since then, leading figures in the AI sector, including Dario Amodei, CEO of Anthropic, and Sam Altman, the other founder of OpenAI, have all gone on record in support of UBI (though Altman's interest has cooled somewhat since he launched a massive UBI experiment in the US in 2019).
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
But Herrmann said it's a ""double-edged sword"" when such figures mention the idea. She isn't convinced that organizations like hers and people Elon Musk want the same thing.
""On the one hand, it's always nice when the debate about a basic income gets attention from prominent voices,"" she said. ""On the other hand, the basic income that people from the tech business talk about is a kind of neoliberal basic income: They talk about what to do when there are no more profitable jobs for people, because they still actually do need people to do certain things, whereas we talk about a basic income that is meant to give people a secure economic base — before the crisis in the job market happens.""
What makes the comments of Merz and Wildberger all the more eyebrow-raising is that mainstream German economists have long since rejected the basic income idea.
Dominik Enste, director of the democracy, society, and economic ethics department at the German Economic Institute in Cologne, dismissed the idea out of hand — AI or no AI. UBI was, he said, just too expensive, especially in a country that already has plenty of social welfare costs.
""Many economists have calculated that implementing this in many countries is financially unfeasible — in Germany, for one, simply because of the need to fund commitments that have already been made (including pension payments),"" he told DW in an email.
However, he also added: ""We will have to change our social security systems if work is replaced by AI and, if necessary, tax AI-generated income. But it will be a conditional basic income that still provides incentives to earn a living."" 
Other economists are more positive about the idea. ""How this plays out depends on many choices we make, and on who makes these choices, in whose interest,"" said Maximilian Kasy, professor of economics at the University of Oxford and author of a book on the impact of AI on society. ""But a lot of the current thrust is to replace workers to increase profits and increase corporate control, though this is not the only path possible.""
Kasy thinks a basic income could be a good solution, ""especially if it's financed in a progressive way (so that only those at the lower end of the income distribution are net recipients),"" he wrote. ""The 'small print' matters very much, though!""
To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video
If the warnings of Wildberger, Musk, Altman et al. are to be believed, AI is already causing a bigger upheaval than any other technological advance in history, partly because of the speed of the innovations, but also because of the scale of the potential impact. 
AI innovation is affecting the jobs of the traditional urban middle classes across the entire world. In 2024, the International Monetary Fund predicted that some 40% of employment in the world would be shaped by AI innovations, and as much as 60% in rich, industrialized nations.
For her part, Herrmann thinks we are not yet asking the truly fundamental questions about the socioeconomic implications of AI: Not just how do we replace the lost jobs and keep the capitalist system going, but what do we actually want to use AI for? As she put it: ""Who actually profits when we become more productive through AI? And how do we redistribute that profit to everyone?""
This, she argued, is why there needs to be more debate about how exactly AI is used in the world of work. ""Which jobs are going to be replaced by AI is not some law of nature,"" said Herrmann . ""It's something that we can shape politically and socially — and then the bigger question is: Who gets a say?"""

This does not mean that the defendants are guilty, though.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Judge Noël Wise, who presides over the proposed class-action lawsuit against Micron, Samsung, and SK hynix, has denied the companies’ motion to have the case dismissed. According to the decision, the defendants’ argument that the legal action is just “a rerun” of the same antitrust case filed against the industry in 2018 was rejected as the plaintiffs have established that there’s some new evidence suggesting that there was collusion between the big three memory chip manufacturers.
 




Micron, Samsung, and SK hynix argued that they watch each other like hawks because of how tight competition is. Judge Wise disagreed with this characterization, though, saying that what they are accused of goes beyond that. “Their contentions are more akin to hawks flying in formation — an activity that hawks ordinarily do not do.”
The plaintiffs, which are made up of 17 individuals and small businesses, allege that the companies made four parallel moves that restricted DRAM supply and led to the current shortage, which saw prices jump by as much as 500% in one year. One of the events they cited included the “simultaneous DRAM production cuts” from October 2022 to April 2023, which was quickly followed by a “coordinated pivot to HBM.” They also said that the three companies announced the winding down of DDR4 production in a span of three months and have failed to expand the production capacity “despite these extraordinary prices — the single most powerful supply signal the DRAM industry has ever produced.”
Other factors that the suing parties brought to the table include the introduction of stringent customer vetting procedures in response to the shortage, the refusal to sign long-term contracts for DRAM customers, Micron’s decision to shut down Crucial (its consumer memory brand), multiple statements projecting the shortage, as well as their involvement in previous price-fixing scandals.
The big three memory chip makers argued that all of these are logical business moves so that they can maximize their revenue during a period of great demand and that they are “consistent with [their own] independent self-interest.” They also said that they appear to have similar behaviors because they’re in a highly concentrated market, and that they’re all “reacting to the same market pressures,” thus it would make sense for them to have a similar response.
Despite their rebuttals, the judge decided that the case is plausible enough to move forward. This isn’t a judgment, though — both sides would still have to show their evidence and speak their arguments before the court before getting a decision. Because of this, the three companies are given until December 7 to respond to the original complaint with the case management conference, which will determine the administrative matters on the case, set for January 2027.

We look at the industry and market response to news that NVIDIA founder and CEO Jensen Huang was forced to step down after reports of affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and two of his employees.
AYESHA RASCOE, HOST:  You might have heard that Jensen Huang, founder of Nvidia and its now-former CEO, was found to have affairs with two employees as well as one with a Chinese businesswoman. The New York Times, citing U.S. intelligence, reported that the businesswoman, who was a client of Nvidia's, was recruited by China's top spy agency. China denies having anything to do with this. But the national security implications are huge, given that Nvidia designs advanced microchips and is involved in AI policy. So what now?Ricky Mulvey is a reporter with NPR's daily economics show, The Indicator from Planet Money. Welcome to the program.RICKY MULVEY, BYLINE: Glad to be here.RASCOE: Ricky, The Indicator explains a slice of the economy in 10 minutes or less. Today, you definitely have less, OK?MULVEY: (Laughter).RASCOE: So let's start here. Was this a failure on Nvidia's part or U.S. intelligence?MULVEY: Yeah. So let's take the personal indiscretions out of this for a moment. Jensen Huang has a personal security team, and hopefully they know who he is closely interacting with. Jensen Huang denied knowing that she was a spy. But Alice Huang, the alleged spy, was a television presenter for Chinese state television before pivoting to tech. And when she was with Jensen, she was an executive at a computing firm called Megaspeed. And Megaspeed was allegedly bypassing U.S. export restrictions to ship advanced chips to China.Investigators at the Commerce Department raised some flags about this company a couple of years ago. They thought it was unusual that Megaspeed had Nvidia chips just packed in boxes at its data center in Malaysia - sort of like leaving Amazon packages in your entranceway, except with highly coveted AI chips.In a statement provided to NPR, Megaspeed says it conducts lawful and legitimate business and that Alice Huang left the company last year. Now, Culper Research - a short seller, or someone who bets against companies - noted their uncomfortably close relationship back in May. So in other words, there was some smoke long before this fire.RASCOE: So Jensen Huang was forced to step down, but do some people want him back?MULVEY: Yeah. There's a petition for Jensen to return. It has over 120,000 signatures, including support from Elon Musk and, according to the petition's website, about 6,000 current Nvidia employees. Don't forget, Jensen Huang made a lot of people a lot of money over the long term.RASCOE: What other implications does his case have for the company and for the AI economy?MULVEY: For the company, this chaos wiped out hundreds of billions of dollars in Nvidia stock value. It's down about 13% since the news broke. And that translates to about $573 billion, or the entire value of Mastercard stock. The S&P 500, though, has held up in the meantime.Now, more granularly, this removal could actually benefit some American AI laboratories and companies. Jensen Huang championed selling advanced chips to China. And that strategy may have been good for Nvidia, but it also gave some Chinese AI companies the same high-powered tools that U.S. businesses have. Also this removal could be the loss of one of AI's chief dealmakers, and that may reduce confidence in the AI story moving forward.RASCOE: OK. So what are you watching for next in this saga?MULVEY: Yeah. Nvidia reports earnings on Tuesday, so you can expect that call to focus on the business. Colette Kress, the chief financial officer, is in as interim CEO. And, Ayesha, that's a tough gig. You know, my boss hooked up with an alleged spy. Now here are the revenue projections.I don't think that Nvidia's business changes that much in the near term. This scandal doesn't change the product, but maybe it's meaningful for the circular deals that Jensen helped lead. What happens to the confidence in those deals now that he's gone? But, I mean, who really knows what happens next?I'll paraphrase a post from Nathan Calvin. He's an AI executive. If you find three ants in your kitchen, the best estimate of the total number of ants in your kitchen is not three.RASCOE: That's reporter Ricky Mulvey with The Indicator. Thanks so much for speaking with us today.MULVEY: Thanks, Ayesha.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

So what are you watching for next in this saga?MULVEY: Yeah. Nvidia reports earnings on Tuesday, so you can expect that call to focus on the business. Colette Kress, the chief financial officer, is in as interim CEO. And, Ayesha, that's a tough gig. You know, my boss hooked up with an alleged spy. Now here are the revenue projections.I don't think that Nvidia's business changes that much in the near term. This scandal doesn't change the product, but maybe it's meaningful for the circular deals that Jensen helped lead. What happens to the confidence in those deals now that he's gone? But, I mean, who really knows what happens next?I'll paraphrase a post from Nathan Calvin. He's an AI executive. If you find three ants in your kitchen, the best estimate of the total number of ants in your kitchen is not three.RASCOE: That's reporter Ricky Mulvey with The Indicator. Thanks so much for speaking with us today.MULVEY: Thanks, Ayesha.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

 You might have heard that Jensen Huang, founder of Nvidia and its now-former CEO, was found to have affairs with two employees as well as one with a Chinese businesswoman. The New York Times, citing U.S. intelligence, reported that the businesswoman, who was a client of Nvidia's, was recruited by China's top spy agency. China denies having anything to do with this. But the national security implications are huge, given that Nvidia designs advanced microchips and is involved in AI policy. So what now?Ricky Mulvey is a reporter with NPR's daily economics show, The Indicator from Planet Money. Welcome to the program.RICKY MULVEY, BYLINE: Glad to be here.RASCOE: Ricky, The Indicator explains a slice of the economy in 10 minutes or less. Today, you definitely have less, OK?MULVEY: (Laughter).RASCOE: So let's start here. Was this a failure on Nvidia's part or U.S. intelligence?MULVEY: Yeah. So let's take the personal indiscretions out of this for a moment. Jensen Huang has a personal security team, and hopefully they know who he is closely interacting with. Jensen Huang denied knowing that she was a spy. But Alice Huang, the alleged spy, was a television presenter for Chinese state television before pivoting to tech. And when she was with Jensen, she was an executive at a computing firm called Megaspeed. And Megaspeed was allegedly bypassing U.S. export restrictions to ship advanced chips to China.Investigators at the Commerce Department raised some flags about this company a couple of years ago. They thought it was unusual that Megaspeed had Nvidia chips just packed in boxes at its data center in Malaysia - sort of like leaving Amazon packages in your entranceway, except with highly coveted AI chips.In a statement provided to NPR, Megaspeed says it conducts lawful and legitimate business and that Alice Huang left the company last year. Now, Culper Research - a short seller, or someone who bets against companies - noted their uncomfortably close relationship back in May. So in other words, there was some smoke long before this fire.RASCOE: So Jensen Huang was forced to step down, but do some people want him back?MULVEY: Yeah. There's a petition for Jensen to return. It has over 120,000 signatures, including support from Elon Musk and, according to the petition's website, about 6,000 current Nvidia employees. Don't forget, Jensen Huang made a lot of people a lot of money over the long term.RASCOE: What other implications does his case have for the company and for the AI economy?MULVEY: For the company, this chaos wiped out hundreds of billions of dollars in Nvidia stock value. It's down about 13% since the news broke. And that translates to about $573 billion, or the entire value of Mastercard stock. The S&P 500, though, has held up in the meantime.Now, more granularly, this removal could actually benefit some American AI laboratories and companies. Jensen Huang championed selling advanced chips to China. And that strategy may have been good for Nvidia, but it also gave some Chinese AI companies the same high-powered tools that U.S. businesses have. Also this removal could be the loss of one of AI's chief dealmakers, and that may reduce confidence in the AI story moving forward.RASCOE: OK. So what are you watching for next in this saga?MULVEY: Yeah. Nvidia reports earnings on Tuesday, so you can expect that call to focus on the business. Colette Kress, the chief financial officer, is in as interim CEO. And, Ayesha, that's a tough gig. You know, my boss hooked up with an alleged spy. Now here are the revenue projections.I don't think that Nvidia's business changes that much in the near term. This scandal doesn't change the product, but maybe it's meaningful for the circular deals that Jensen helped lead. What happens to the confidence in those deals now that he's gone? But, I mean, who really knows what happens next?I'll paraphrase a post from Nathan Calvin. He's an AI executive. If you find three ants in your kitchen, the best estimate of the total number of ants in your kitchen is not three.RASCOE: That's reporter Ricky Mulvey with The Indicator. Thanks so much for speaking with us today.MULVEY: Thanks, Ayesha.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

Don't forget, Jensen Huang made a lot of people a lot of money over the long term.RASCOE: What other implications does his case have for the company and for the AI economy?MULVEY: For the company, this chaos wiped out hundreds of billions of dollars in Nvidia stock value. It's down about 13% since the news broke. And that translates to about $573 billion, or the entire value of Mastercard stock. The S&P 500, though, has held up in the meantime.Now, more granularly, this removal could actually benefit some American AI laboratories and companies. Jensen Huang championed selling advanced chips to China. And that strategy may have been good for Nvidia, but it also gave some Chinese AI companies the same high-powered tools that U.S. businesses have. Also this removal could be the loss of one of AI's chief dealmakers, and that may reduce confidence in the AI story moving forward.RASCOE: OK. So what are you watching for next in this saga?MULVEY: Yeah. Nvidia reports earnings on Tuesday, so you can expect that call to focus on the business. Colette Kress, the chief financial officer, is in as interim CEO. And, Ayesha, that's a tough gig. You know, my boss hooked up with an alleged spy. Now here are the revenue projections.I don't think that Nvidia's business changes that much in the near term. This scandal doesn't change the product, but maybe it's meaningful for the circular deals that Jensen helped lead. What happens to the confidence in those deals now that he's gone? But, I mean, who really knows what happens next?I'll paraphrase a post from Nathan Calvin. He's an AI executive. If you find three ants in your kitchen, the best estimate of the total number of ants in your kitchen is not three.RASCOE: That's reporter Ricky Mulvey with The Indicator. Thanks so much for speaking with us today.MULVEY: Thanks, Ayesha.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

So what are you watching for next in this saga?MULVEY: Yeah. Nvidia reports earnings on Tuesday, so you can expect that call to focus on the business. Colette Kress, the chief financial officer, is in as interim CEO. And, Ayesha, that's a tough gig. You know, my boss hooked up with an alleged spy. Now here are the revenue projections.I don't think that Nvidia's business changes that much in the near term. This scandal doesn't change the product, but maybe it's meaningful for the circular deals that Jensen helped lead. What happens to the confidence in those deals now that he's gone? But, I mean, who really knows what happens next?I'll paraphrase a post from Nathan Calvin. He's an AI executive. If you find three ants in your kitchen, the best estimate of the total number of ants in your kitchen is not three.RASCOE: That's reporter Ricky Mulvey with The Indicator. Thanks so much for speaking with us today.MULVEY: Thanks, Ayesha.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

76,2026-11-15,"Dismissals follow probe that uncovered illegal oil refineries and smuggling in a region run by Kataib Hezbollah, a pro-Iranian armed group.
Save
Share
Iraq’s Prime Minister Ali al-Zaidi has dismissed nine senior security officials over an alleged oil smuggling operation in the Jurf al-Nasr area, a stronghold of the Iran-backed Kataib Hezbollah group.
Al-Zaidi chaired an emergency security meeting to review the findings of a committee investigating smuggling in Jurf al-Nasr, Babil province, military spokesperson Sabah al-Numan told the Iraqi News Agency (INA).
The move follows an investigation that uncovered illegal oil refineries and evidence of manipulation linked to the smuggling activities.
“In light of the investigation’s findings, [the prime minister] ordered that all individuals accused of involvement in oil smuggling operations be referred to the judiciary,” al-Numan said.
The prime minister “also ordered the dismissal” of nine officials, including security and intelligence officers in Babil province, an Interior Ministry commander, and the director of the Oil Pipelines Company at the Ministry of Oil, he said.
The police chief of Babil province was among those dismissed.
 

 
Kataib Hezbollah, an Iran-aligned paramilitary group and one of Iraq’s most heavily armed factions, has recently issued several sharp statements against the prime minister, threatening his government and accusing him of corruption.
Senior Iraqi politicians previously told the AFP news agency that al-Zaidi had repeatedly said he was ready to confront groups refusing to disarm.
Al Jazeera’s Peter Smith, reporting from Baghdad, said previous Iraqi governments had been careful not to address the issue of an alleged oil smuggling network worth up to $30m monthly.
“This area had effectively been run as a state within a state until now,” Smith said. “But that seems to have changed, with Prime Minister Ali al-Zaidi saying that no inch of Iraq will be allowed to have oil smuggling operations like this.”
Two strategic oil pipelines running through the south of the country, just south of Baghdad, “had effectively been tapped into”, Smith said, citing a leaked intelligence report. “Oil was being siphoned off, stolen, and then sold on the black market in an operation worth tens of millions of dollars every month.”
 

 
On Wednesday, the INA reported that “intelligence documents” containing satellite images “showed crude oil and oil-derivatives smuggling operations carried out by an armed faction” in Jurf al-Nasr, accusing an unnamed group of tapping into a pipeline there.
Al-Numan said the investigating committee “confirmed the accuracy” of the information.
Akeel Abbas, a senior non-resident fellow at the Iraq Initiative within the Atlantic Council, told Al Jazeera that oil smuggling by various political factions and armed groups “is common in Iraq” due to “commercial rather than political or strategic” gains.
Abbas said the latest crackdown has more to do with “militia disarmament”.
“The prime minister cleverly decided to frame it as anticorruption. That is why a few days ago, there was a report leaked by the government about this operation,” he said.
The prime minister wants to “cut off the financial lifeline to this particular group that has been so resistant” to the government’s “militia disarmament” initiatives, he added.









Follow Al Jazeera English:"

The global versions may come with smaller batteries, while leaked European prices suggest around €1,399 for the Pro and €1,784 for the Pro Max.
