78,2026-11-15,"
TOKYO -
 Artificial intelligence stocks could regain momentum in the second half of 2027 as excessive market expectations ease and investors assess the impact of tighter U.S. data center regulations, according to Daiwa Securities strategist Yugo Tsuboi, who argues that the recent selloff represents a market adjustment rather than the beginning of a prolonged downturn in AI investment.


Tsuboi said the sharp decline in U.S. equities on November 5 was primarily driven by trading positions and risk management strategies rather than fundamental problems in the economy or financial system. The selloff reflected a combination of selling by value-at-risk-driven investment strategies, rebalancing by leveraged exchange-traded funds and concerns about market liquidity.
Technology-focused long-short funds had already been experiencing weaker returns since the summer as semiconductor stocks underwent corrections. When market volatility increased, some investors accelerated profit-taking, triggering additional selling as funds adjusted their exposure to comply with risk limits.
Leveraged ETFs may have amplified the market swings through automatic portfolio adjustments, Tsuboi said, noting that similar mechanisms have contributed to volatility in South Korean equities.
However, he emphasized that the downturn did not exhibit the characteristics of a broader financial crisis. During a genuine liquidity shock, investors often sell even traditionally safe assets to raise cash, causing simultaneous disruptions across multiple markets.
In the November selloff, interest rates remained relatively stable and there was little evidence of widespread selling of safe-haven assets, suggesting that the turbulence was concentrated in equity positioning rather than financial system instability.
Tsuboi pointed to the VIX volatility index and its futures term structure as important indicators of market stabilization. When short-term volatility expectations exceed longer-term expectations, a condition known as backwardation, markets are typically experiencing elevated stress. A return to a more normal futures structure would indicate that selling pressure is subsiding.
The correction has also reduced the unusually high expectations embedded in AI-related stock valuations.
In Japan, the Nikkei 225 had substantially outperformed the broader TOPIX index since around April, reflecting investor enthusiasm for semiconductor manufacturers, AI infrastructure providers and data center-related companies.
The gap between the two indexes has since narrowed significantly, suggesting that investors have begun reassessing the long-term earnings potential of businesses associated with AI.
Tsuboi said stock prices had previously incorporated expectations for developments several years into the future rather than simply reflecting earnings prospects for 2026 and 2027. The recent adjustment has brought valuations closer to levels consistent with more sustainable growth assumptions.
Nevertheless, a prolonged slowdown in AI investment could have consequences extending well beyond financial markets.
The International Monetary Fund examined several possible outcomes for AI development in its October 2026 World Economic Outlook, including scenarios in which investment accelerates alongside productivity improvements, expansion encounters supply constraints, or investor expectations collapse and trigger a market correction.
Under the pessimistic scenario discussed by Tsuboi, global economic output in 2027 could be approximately 1% below its baseline projection. With global growth projected at around 3.4%, such a deterioration could reduce the growth rate to roughly 2.4%, leaving the world economy expanding but at a noticeably slower pace.
Tsuboi considers such an extreme outcome unlikely, arguing that AI-related infrastructure investment is more likely to experience a moderate slowdown than a complete reversal. His outlook falls between the IMF scenarios involving supply constraints and a sharper collapse in expectations, although closer to the more moderate case.
One of the biggest uncertainties facing the industry is the growing political resistance to the rapid expansion of data centers across the United States.
Following the November 2026 midterm elections, several newly elected Democratic governors have advocated restrictions on new facilities, citing concerns over electricity consumption, infrastructure costs, environmental pressures and the distribution of economic benefits.
Tsuboi highlighted differences between proposals in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, arguing that the political response is far from uniform.
In Texas, the proposed approach would suspend approvals for new data centers until the state legislature establishes appropriate protections. Such a policy would be particularly restrictive for new developments, although facilities that have already received approval would not necessarily be affected.
Ohio has taken a more conditional approach, under which new data centers could proceed if developers meet requirements involving electricity costs, labor protections and public financial support.
These conditions include requiring operators to bear their full electricity-related costs, establish appropriate labor arrangements and repay subsidies or tax incentives if they fail to satisfy agreed commitments.
Georgia has also seen proposals for temporary restrictions on new data center construction, although Tsuboi noted that the incoming governor had previously supported data center development while serving as mayor of Atlanta, suggesting that a comprehensive prohibition may not be the intended outcome.
The composition of state legislatures could further limit the implementation of restrictive policies.
Republicans retain control of at least one legislative chamber in each of the three states discussed, according to Tsuboi's analysis, creating potential obstacles to prolonged or statewide prohibitions even where governors favor tighter controls.
Consequently, he expects regulatory pressure to produce a cooling-off period rather than bring U.S. data center investment to a complete halt.
Singapore provides a possible model for how such restrictions could evolve.
The city-state introduced a moratorium on new data center development in 2019 because of limitations involving land, electricity and other resources. However, authorities subsequently developed more selective approval procedures designed to balance environmental considerations with the country's need to remain competitive in the digital economy.
Data center development has since resumed under tighter conditions, demonstrating that restrictions can lead to more sustainable investment practices rather than permanently suppressing the industry.
Tsuboi expects the United States may follow a comparable path, establishing clearer rules governing infrastructure development while allowing the AI industry to continue expanding.
Geopolitical competition provides another reason why American AI investment is unlikely to slow dramatically.
The race between the United States and China to develop advanced AI technologies has intensified, with patent activity illustrating China's growing capabilities.
Although the United States remains a leader in important areas of AI development, Tsuboi warned that substantially reducing investment could allow China to close the technological gap or overtake American competitors.
AI infrastructure has therefore become an economic security priority as well as a commercial investment opportunity, making it difficult for the United States to abandon large-scale development.
Another factor supporting continued investor interest is the changing distribution of economic gains between labor and capital.
Tsuboi pointed to the long-term decline in labor's share of national income in the United States and other Group of Seven economies, arguing that improvements in productivity associated with cloud computing, information technology and automation have increasingly benefited companies rather than workers.
As AI adoption accelerates, the economic benefits of higher productivity may continue to flow disproportionately toward businesses and their shareholders.
For investors, this creates a dilemma. Selling all AI-related holdings may reduce exposure to short-term market volatility, but it could also mean missing the longer-term financial gains from technological advances.
Tsuboi therefore argued that maintaining some exposure to AI-related investments remains important even as valuations undergo further adjustments.
Historical market performance following U.S. midterm elections also provides grounds for cautious optimism.
Tsuboi examined previous periods in which a Republican president faced a Democratic-controlled Congress, a political configuration that he said had occurred eight times in the 21 postwar midterm election cycles included in his analysis.
In those historical cases, stock prices had risen by an average of approximately 20% over the following 160 trading days.
He suggested that divided government does not necessarily produce unfavorable stock market conditions, since political constraints can encourage more moderate policies and reduce the risk of abrupt changes.
Nevertheless, the immediate outlook for AI-related equities remains uncertain as investors await clearer indications of how U.S. data center regulations will develop.
Tsuboi expects the market to advance only gradually through the remainder of 2026 and into early 2027 while political and regulatory uncertainties continue to weigh on sentiment.
A more substantial recovery could begin during the second half of 2027 as expectations for AI investment improve and the Federal Reserve potentially adopts a more accommodative monetary policy.
The overall outlook, he said, is for a period of slower, sustainable gains followed by a stronger acceleration later in the year, with AI stocks undergoing a temporary cooling-off phase rather than entering a lasting decline.

A historic guest hall at Kodaiji Temple in Kyoto, closely associated with Nene, the wife of warlord Toyotomi Hideyoshi, has been reconstructed for the first time in approximately 240 years, restoring a lavish space once used by Hideyoshi to entertain feudal lords. 
Keisei Electric Railway has suspended online seat reservations for its Skyliner express service connecting central Tokyo with Narita Airport after a cyberattack potentially exposed up to 3.37 million personal information records, with no indication of when the booking system will resume operations. 
A crowd of 2,300 people dressed as Santa Claus marched through Tokyo's Shibuya district in the rain on November 15 as part of a charity event aimed at supporting children battling serious illnesses in Japan and those unable to receive adequate medical care overseas. 
Japan's tourism industry is undergoing a major shift one year after China urged its citizens to avoid traveling to Japan, with Chinese visitor numbers falling sharply and businesses in Osaka struggling to recover lost revenue, while Kyoto has successfully attracted more Western tourists through a strategy aimed at reducing its dependence on the Chinese market. 
The number of people taken to hospital after being injured by deer in and around Nara Park has reached a record high in 2026, with foreign tourists accounting for more than 70% of cases, prompting warnings about the dangers of approaching the wild animals during their autumn mating season.

Japanese companies are facing an increasingly serious wave of cyberattacks, with personal information leaks and fraudulent financial transactions causing widespread damage, while the rapid development of artificial intelligence is making attacks faster and more sophisticated and driving demand for cybersecurity specialists known as white hackers. 
A quick mobile gaming session can drain more battery than expected. The game gets the blame, but several parts of your phone work at once: the screen stays lit, the processor handles animation, and the connection exchanges data.

PixAI is expanding its role in artificial intelligence-assisted content creation with the launch of PixAI Studio, an integrated production environment that brings image generation, animation, editing, and asset management into a single workspace, offering independent creators new opportunities to develop animated stories without the extensive resources traditionally associated with film and television production. 
The growth of mobile gaming throughout both central and eastern Asia has been extremely fast due to increasing smartphone usage and improvements in cellular networks.  
The mobile application offers fans a convenient way to look at the fixtures, game details, and settings of their profile.  
New Zealanders have a reputation for low tolerance of fuss, and that attitude shows up in how they spend their screen time.

79,2026-11-15,"If not listed, please contact your TV provider.
The Fantasy football trade deadline is approaching fast in most leagues, and now is your chance to strike on some of the best buy-low and buy-high trade candidates. Week 10 brought us plenty of action in the early slate of games and now comes the fallout. Let's take a look at the most important takeaways from the early-slate action and how you should approach these developments from a Fantasy football standpoint.
LaPorta has a pass attempts problem. Jared Goff has thrown 32 or fewer passes in five straight games and LaPorta does not have more than five targets in any of those games. The Lions' defense has played much better, but that unit has faced weak competition and just lost Aidan Hutchinson (possibly for the season). It's hard to trust LaPorta right now, but he's too talented to be this bad. It feels a little like Nico Collins where you know it's not the ideal situation but you also know a big game is around the corner. I wouldn't necessarily buy low on LaPorta but I do think he's bound to bounce back to some degree.
What happens against Minnesota stays against Minnesota
GOAL LINE WORK: Aaron Jones was treated like the goal line back and even scored from one yard out. In order to feel good about Jones or Jordan Mason, we need a consistent goal line back. We're not there yet but keep an eye on it going forward.
I had my doubts about Pollard this week facing this supremely tough run defense, and he rushed for only 33 yards. But Pollard had 8 catches for 66 yards and a TD. Unofficially, he ran about 65% of the routes, which is by far a season high. I think the Titans will do whatever they can to get the ball in his hands and he could have a nice Fantasy finish.
It's tough to know what to make of McGown's 12 carries and his goal-line TD. Jonathan Taylor had 16 carries and 2 catches, so you can certainly live with 18 touches for Taylor, but we'd certainly prefer 25 touches! He hardly ever has any real competition in the backfield. McGowan is playing well and might have a role going forward. I'll wait to hear what the team says about him before rushing to judgment.
Keenan Allen and Josh Downs were traded and Tyler Warren only got 3 targets? Granted, Daniel Jones threw 22 passes, but this is still a very disappointing performance from Warren. In fact, the entire TE position was garbage today (heading into the late window of games) unless you started Gavin Bartholomew!
Eleven catches for 80 yards on 13 targets! This is very exciting. We have a must-start WR in 3-WR leagues and a flex in the shallower formats. And, looking at Tyson's schedule going forward, the Saints will continue to throw a lot.
I can't confidently start Bryce Young anymore. Meanwhile, I'm not ready to drop Jalen Coker yet, but I'm definitely not starting him. As far as Tyler Shough goes, it wouldn't surprise me if he had some good games simply because he throws so much. With Tyson emerging alongside Olave, I want to be optimistic. These QBs are both droppable but also are justifiable starts if you're in a pinch.
When you look at Nico Collins and DJ Moore coming through today, just remember that it's hard to hold talented players down for too long. The same could be applied to Parker Washington a couple of weeks ago when Fantasy managers were frustrated with him. It was great to see this game from Collins. Continue to start him, but don't be surprised if he gets 6 targets next week.
I know he only had three catches today, but I love the eight targets and the rushing TD he got. I don't consider Concepcion a must-start, but I think he's very valuable as a WR3 or a flex. The Browns should continue to feature him.
An incredible game for Johnson as he put up 171 total yards and two TDs. What does this mean for him and Kenneth Walker? I assume Walker will get at least 80% of his normal workload going forward and be used at the goal line, but I would expect a slightly bigger role for Johnson. He's too talented to keep off the field.
Downs had four catches for 53 yards and a TD on five targets. Unofficially, he only ran eight routes (these route rates can change overnight)! It's a potentially big development and could cut into targets for Travis Kelce (played through an injury and missed a lot of snaps) and Rashee Rice
Wilson received 14 targets and turned them into 106 yards. Keep in mind, Omar Cooper Jr. left almost immediately with an injury and Geno Smith got hurt as well. I can't say I'd be super confident in Wilson if Cade Klubnik were under center, but it was good to see Wilson dominate targets.

80,2026-11-15,"What's the best visual analogy for a teetering AI-led bull market? 
For Stop Funding Billionaires, a group that's been protesting high-profile IPOs this year, the answer is  a six foot tall game of Jenga. The group deployed the protest tool at a recent #BlocktheIPO demonstration in San Francisco, aimed at Anthropic's debut set for next week. 
Organizer Jonathan Westin told Business Insider that the market feels increasingly precarious, and that the organization is concerned about Americans' exposure to the risks of the AI bubble popping. 
""It continues to be this very speculative shell game,"" he said of the tech-fueled market boom. ""It feels like a game of Jenga where if one thing falls, the whole system will fall.""
Stop Funding Billionaires staged two protests recently, first in front of Anthropic's office in New York City on November 9, and one a day later near the AI lab's office in San Francisco, where they unveiled the Jenga game, made by a Bay Area artist.
The reason they say Anthropic shouldn't go public is rooted in the same argument made by many of the market's AI bears: the future of AI is uncertain, and if the bull market collapses, it could compromise the retirement savings of working Americans and retirees. Though the excitement is high on Wall Street, a cohort of investing pros share the more bearish sentiment, and see Anthropic being a speculative investment when it hits public markets. 
""It comes down to investment risks that are going to be borne by working people, particularly working people whose retirement savings are exposed to it, which will be virtually anyone who is in an index fund,"" said fellow organizer, Elizabeth Parisian.
The group raised similar concerns when SpaceX began trading in June, protesting the historic IPO and sounding the alarm that changing the requirements to fast-track the newly minted stock into major indexes poses outsize risks to Americans' investment portfolios. 
Ahead of the SpaceX offering, market participants from normal retirees to professional investors dismay at the fact that rules were being bent in a way that could mean people would be exposed to Elon Musk's rocket and AI company whether they wanted to or not. 

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
""There's just so much at stake for regular people, and Anthropic is just plowing ahead,"" Westin noted. ""Regardless of all of these concerns, I think in many ways, it's trying to become too big to fail, similar to the banks in the 2008 crash, and this feels eerily familiar to us.""
Westin's views aligns with prominent tech and AI bears, including ""The Big Short"" investor Michael Burry, who recently said that it would be beneficial for humanity if a stock market crash were to prevent Anthropic and OpenAI from going public.
Westin was among five protesters arrested at the demonstration in New York, though he said he has no regrets and believes that more direct action is needed to help the public understand the risks related to giant IPOs from Anthropic and others. 
""Unfortunately, the financial system is now set up in a way where it's very opaque to the regular person what's at stake here,"" he said. ""If we can do more to shed light on how these IPOs are going to affect our everyday people, we think the more the better.""
Anthropic did not respond to a request for comment.

83,2026-11-15,"Alongside Quentin Tarantino, Guy Ritchie is one of the only modern directors who's actually built a body of work directly on the back of his own voice. Yes, there have been deviations, like his live-action adaptation of Disney's Aladdin, but for the most part, when Guy Ritchie makes something, you know that Guy Ritchie made it.
At the same time, though, after arriving like a hurricane with Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels in 1998, Ritchie has gone almost out of his way to prove that genre shouldn't get in the way of him telling stories. Ritchie's best movies have included glossy spy romps, gritty World War II movies, hugely popular adaptations of Sherlock Holmes, and, of course, that billion-dollar success with Aladdin.
Not everything has worked out massively well. Consensus suggests (a little cruelly) that his King Arthur movie, for instance, deserves to be forgotten. But no matter how far he wanders into franchise territory or away from the core gangster movie that he made at the start of his career, you can still spot a Ritchie joint very quickly. The editing is always fast-paced and energetic; the camera moves like it has somewhere to be ; and the dialog is always witty and rapid-fire. The cadence is always roughly the same.
Though he's made some excellent movies in other genres, it's when Ritchie returns to the criminal underworld that his signature style seems to work so well, work the best. His romanticized world of small-town London crooks and international arms dealers has proved incredibly entertaining over the past 30 years, and he has refined, subverted, and revived his own blueprint, obviously with huge success, with Netflix's The Gentlemen series.
2026 saw Ritchie return to his crime roots, but for a very different movie from Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels. In the Grey is probably his slickest looking crime movie, and unfortunately, it's also his most emotionally distant. It saw him reunite with Henry Cavill and Jake Gyllenhaal for a crime caper centered on a high-concept extraction team who are charged with stealing back a billion-dollar fortune from a ruthless despot.
On paper, In the Grey should have all the hallmarks of a classic Ritchie heist movie. It has fast-talking fixes, a sharp sartorial style, and entertaining set pieces, but the muted critical reception on release comes as no surprise when you actually watch it. It doesn't have the same London swagger because Ritchie here aims for a more generic international caper style, and there's just not as much of the right Guy Ritchie personality as you'd want there to be. A lot of Ritchie's work embraces grime, and In the Grey is just a little too clinical.
One strange quirk of Guy Ritchie's movie resumé is that he seems to make movies so quickly that some land without any sort of cultural footprint at all. That's very much the case for Wrath of Man. Ritchie's 2021 reunion with Jason Statham was very different from their first two collaborations, which relied quite heavily on Statham's unexpected comedic chops that have largely disappeared as he's morphed into this era's answer to Bruce Willis. Here he's a lot more po-faced.
Wrath of Man sees him play the enigmatically named ""H"", a mysterious newcomer to an LA armored truck company with a personal vendetta that's gradually revealed through a creatively unconventional timeline. Though it appears to be quite a generic action movie in comparison to Richie’s more stylish releases, critics were quite positive on it, especially the action sequences and the balance of multiple story threads. And Ritchie deserves credit for the way that he tells the fragmented story. It gives a conventional revenge tale more muscle, and so too does the excellent cast, which also includes Holt McCanally, Scott Eastwood, Joss Hartnett, and Eddie Marsan.
When Revolver arrived in 2005, critics were not kind and tore it to shreds, baffled by Ritchie's shift from cheeky East End gang wars to heavy-handed esoteric philosophy. But the same things that it was criticized for more than 20 years ago are exactly the reasons why it's aged better than might have been expected.
Revolver once again teams Ritchie with Jason Statham, who plays Jake Green, a gambler out for revenge against a petty casino owner. The story actively embraces an existential anxiety that also stands apart from the rest of what Guy Ritchie's done since. As a result, Revolver is easily Ritchie's most misunderstood movie and deserves far more appreciation for its audacity, especially following the success of Snatch.
Instead of making something that could have just been a direct follow-up to that, he swung for something genuinely strange, and he used the familiarity of a mobster revenge story to probe the very nature of human hubris. It looks great. Statham offers a strong performance, and Ray Liotta is on top-level form as a breakdown-prone mob boss. It does at times struggle under the weight of its own ambitions, but it's a bold subversion of the genre, and it's a fascinating companion piece to the rest of Ritchie's gangster movies.
After back-to-back box office flops with Swept Away and Revolver, Ritchie headed back to the comfort of the London underworld and started to show a little bit more of the blueprint that would later become The Gentlemen movie and TV series. RocknRolla was widely proclaimed a step back in the right direction and did make more money than its budget, and rightly so. The plot revolves around a massive real estate scam in London, which includes low-end hustlers, Russian oligarchs, bent politicians, and a heroin-addict rock star, played exceptionally well by Toby Kebbell.
This may have been Ritchie playing in his comfort zone, but he brought together a strong cast, including Gerard Butler, Idris Elba, Tom Hardy, and Kebble, to flesh out an excellent group of characters and the joy is in spending time with them. Kebble's Johnny Quid, in particular, is one of the best characters ever introduced in a Ritchie movie, and he steals basically every scene he appears in.
RocknRolla isn't exactly transformative. It was a much-needed return to the genre and his usual tropes for Guy Ritchie, but it was a welcome reminder of why everything he did was so good for crime movies.
Richie has a lot of fun with clashing seemingly very different worlds together. Behind the camera, he often follows that trend with his casting choices, notably bringing in massive Hollywood stars like Matthew McConaughey in The Gentlemen and Brad Pitt in Snatch to operate in grubby, small-time crime underworlds that don't seem to fit them.
In The Gentlemen, not only does he have McConaughey, he also clashes the disparate worlds of high society and London's criminal underbelly together to create a compelling, contradictory story. McConaughey plays Mickey Pearson, an American expat at the top of a lucrative marijuana empire, operating inside the estate of impoverished British aristocrats, who finds himself at the center of a massive gang war when he tries to sell his business off.
The Gentlemen's cast is excellent, and everybody seems to be having a lot of fun, especially Hugh Grant in a career-best comedic performance as sleazy tabloid photographer Fletcher, Charlie Hunnam as Pearson's dry-witted consigliere, and Colin Farrell as the equally dry and even funnier, tracksuit-wearing Coach. They're all top-tier Guy Ritchie characters, and the story is as delightfully twisty, self-consciously funny, and, of course, violent as Ritchie gets.
The movie obviously spawned an even more successful Netflix series, which is a standalone spinoff and not a continuation, but everything that you love about the series was born in this movie. Moreso, in fact.
Like the gentleman after it and Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels before it, Snatch inspired a TV spinoff that was never quite as good, but the original movie was further explosive proof that Guy Ritchie was really not a one-hit wonder. Released in 2000, Snatch is basically Guy Ritchie's Pulp Fiction. He was given a bigger budget, could attract international star power, and he delivered another intricate, lightning-fast narrative centered around a stolen 84-carat diamond in the chaotic world of unlicensed bare-knuckle boxing.
Lock, Stock... has been his announcement movie, but Snatch cemented Ritchie as a major voice in world cinema. He was helped along the way by an incredible performance by a barely understandable Brad Pitt as Irish Traveler boxer One Punch Mickey, and supporting roles from the likes of Benicio Del Toro, Vinnie Jones, Derek Farina, and, once again, Jason Statham. Even the characters who only get a moment or two on screen get an unforgettable line or a bizarre quirk, and the whole thing feels like an early greatest hits of Ritchie's signature style flourishes, including an excellent soundtrack.
It's bigger, louder, and incredibly confident when you put it next to Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels, and a lot of people would have it as his best crime movie, but this landmark of British pop culture could never be the best.
If Snatch was Guy Ritchie's Pulp Fiction, Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels was his Reservoir Dogs: a lower-budget, high-energy burst of irresistible confidence and swagger that announced a major new voice to the movie world. Its various quirks and tropes have been so copied or mocked over the years that it's easy to forget how great they were when they arrived in 1998, but they grabbed an audience immediately and simply wouldn't let go.
For all its twists, the plot is actually deceptively simple: four London chancers, played by Jason Statham, Dexter Fletcher, Nick Moran, and Jason Flemyng, lose a rigged car game and find themselves in hock for £500,000 to a local mob boss. It quickly escalates into a stylish, incredibly well-soundtracked comedy of errors that never loses its comic charm even as the bodies pile up.
Everyone was instantly floored by the movie's raw personality, which relied heavily on incredibly sharp dialogue, excellent editing, and lightning-in-a-bottle casting choices, which also included former footballer Vinnie Jones as the immediately iconic Big Chris.
The climax that sees multiple criminal factions unwittingly cross paths is an absolute masterclass in screenwriting, and the whole thing is both hilarious and endlessly quotable. It simply doesn't get old.

Interactive reads from the businessline desk
Diversification may have its virtues but so does concentrated investments. Sector funds are examples of concentrated bets. In this article, we draw a distinction between concentrated bets in the equity and the bond markets and show why the former is meaningful and the latter, somewhat risky.
In theory, equity has unlimited upside potential. That means stock prices can rise to any level. In the real world, stock prices have high, not unlimited, upside potential given our finite investment horizon. Yet, concentrated bets can be meaningful. If you expect the banking sector to do well in the coming year, buying units of a banking sector ETF or a sector fund could be optimal. But the downside can be high if your bet turns wrong.
Unlike equity, bonds are obligations. Bond prices are the present value of all future interest cash flows (obligations) expected over the life of a bond. Bond prices go up when interest rate comes down and decline when interest rate goes up. But the level that interest rate can decline is much less than the level it can go up, as interest rates are primarily driven by the inflation levels in the economy. So, the downside risk in bonds is greater than its upside potential.
Importantly, bonds do not carry a high upside potential like equity. Even if a company does well in any year, you will only receive the obligatory interest payment. Given this, why invest in sector bond funds? What if one company in that sector fails to make its interest payment on time? That company’s bond price will decline. But the failure by this company to pay interest on time can spook the bond market. The upshot is that bonds issued by other companies in the same sector could also see fall in prices. This could happen if the market becomes nervous about the sector and demands a higher credit risk premium from all companies in that sector.
Concentrated bets in the equity market could generate high returns, if your bet turns right. But that cannot be said of the bond market. So, diversification pays off for bond investments. Investing in different sectors may help to manage credit risk. Likewise, diversification across bond maturities could help when interest rate goes up. Note that this argument about diversification applies for direct investments in bonds and for bond funds. Your investment in bank deposits is another argument left for a later discussion.
(The author offers training programmes for individuals to manage their personal investments)

88,2026-11-15,"Türkiye’s fund liquidation process is proceeding as planned and is moving even faster than initially expected, Treasury and Finance Minister Mehmet Şimşek said.
Speaking in a live broadcast on private broadcaster Kanal 7 on Nov. 15, Şimşek said the process was being managed on the principles of transparency, efficiency and fairness, stressing that no public funds had been used for payments to fund investors.
Şimşek recalled that 131 funds managed by seven portfolio management companies were placed under liquidation on Sept. 17. The funds had approximately 456,000 individual investors.
“The payments are being made very quickly and transparently,” he said.
Şimşek said the Capital Markets Board (SPK) is also examining shares held by the funds that had been subject to abuse and manipulation in recent years.
He said 25 inspectors had been transferred from the Treasury and Finance Ministry to the SPK and that the examinations would be completed by the end of the month.
Following the reviews, criminal complaints would be filed against people found to have engaged in market-disrupting activities, while any illicit gains would be identified, he added.
Şimşek also said the fund issue had coincided with a decline in global stock markets.
“Yes, there has been a loss of value on the stock market. However, stock markets in emerging economies declined by an average of 8 percent during the same period, while Türkiye’s stock market fell by 9 percent,” he said.
People with savings in money market funds or other funds whose money remained invested may have experienced difficulties accessing liquidity, Şimşek said, adding that the liquidation process had resolved the issue.
“Banks hold only 1.3 billion Turkish Liras in these funds, corresponding to 0.002 percent of their total holdings. There is no possibility of this crisis spreading to the banking sector,” he said.
“In the past, an incident like this in Türkiye would have caused an increase in the exchange rate. There has been no significant rise in dollarization. Money withdrawn from the funds is being paid in Turkish liras, so there has been no dollarization,” Şimşek also said.
Şimşek said Türkiye had introduced taxes on all funds without distinguishing between domestic and foreign investors.
Some foreign funds had sold their shares and withdrawn their savings because of the latest developments, he said.
“We have long said that we do not want carry trade. We introduced taxes, while the Central Bank cut interest rates, making carry trade less attractive,” he said.
Şimşek said the authorities had investigated allegations of capital outflows but found no unusual movement.
He said money could leave the country through three channels: SWIFT transfers, crypto assets and electronic payment institutions. The authorities examined all three channels and found that outflows between Sept. 1 and Sept. 16 were entirely related to foreign trade, he explained.
The authorities also examined the first 16 days of each of the first nine months of the year before the fund issue emerged, he noted. Şimşek said $14.7 billion and 7 billion euros had been transferred through SWIFT, with all transactions linked to imports. “There was no difference between those figures and the payments made in September,” he added.
Şimşek said the government had also investigated claims that $8 billion had been transferred abroad through crypto assets.
“We examined the entire year. The total amount of crypto assets in Türkiye is $4 billion,” he said.
According to an examination by the Financial Crimes Investigation Board, or MASAK, the amount transferred through other channels was not even $200 million, he added.
Şimşek said he would travel to the G20 summit in mid-December before proceeding to New York for talks with MSCI’s senior management.
He said he would also meet global investors concerned about the issue and hold discussions with them.
“A permanent reform will have taken shape by mid-December,” Şimşek said.
MSCI suspended certain changes to its Türkiye indexes and warned that the country’s emerging-market status could be reviewed unless sufficient progress is made by June 2027.

Iran on Nov. 15 said that it will not remain in a state of “neither war nor peace,” referring to the current standoff between Iran and the United States, as shifting rhetoric from Washington following the U.S. elections leaves the region uncertain about the path ahead.
Türkiye’s fund liquidation process is proceeding as planned and is moving even faster than initially expected, Treasury and Finance Minister Mehmet Şimşek said.
Türkiye will carry a five-match losing streak into its UEFA Nations League clash against France on Nov. 15, following a 2-1 defeat to Belgium that marked head coach Sergen Yalçın’s debut."

90,2026-11-15,"As someone who’s breathing motorcycle and automotive news day in, day out, I’m often asked about my favorite motorcycles and cars. And since I'm particularly keen on adventure motorcycles, I often find myself repeating one motorcycle in my answers: the Yamaha Ténéré 700.
The reason is pretty simple. There is hardly any other motorcycle out there that’s as simple, as complete, and as genuinely capable of taking you just about anywhere. It doesn't need a dozen electronic riding aids or a complicated suspension setup to make its point. Give it a decent set of tires, a tank of fuel, and a willing rider, and it's ready to disappear down some forgotten mountain road.
But what if I told you that Yamaha thinks you could get much of that adventure without an adventure motorcycle at all? What if, instead of straddling a tall, intimidating dual-sport, you could do it on a scooter?
That might sound like sacrilege to the ADV faithful, but Yamaha appears to be entertaining exactly that idea with its new RW700 and RW350 scoots. These aren't your run-of-the-mill urban commuters with some adventure bodywork slapped on.
They're built around the rather interesting proposition that a scooter can be more than a city runabout, potentially offering a more approachable, practical and surprisingly capable way of getting off the beaten track. And while I'm not quite ready to trade my imaginary Ténéré 700 for a scooter, Yamaha has certainly got my attention.
Reason number one. At the heart of the RW700 is Yamaha's familiar 689cc CP2 parallel-twin, the same engine that powers the MT-07, YZF-R7, and Ténéré 700. It retains the characteristic 270-degree crank layout, but gets a slightly different state of tune for scooter duty.
Yamaha quotes 68.4 hp at 8,250 rpm, alongside 48 lb.ft of torque delivered between 6,500 and 6,750 rpm. A chain drive sends power to the rear wheel.
The equipment list is pretty interesting, too. Cruise control and a speed limiter are included as standard, while the Y-AMT transmission offers a choice between fully automatic operation and manual gear selection. The six-speed system does away with both the traditional clutch lever and foot-operated gear shifter. Instead, you can change gears through switches on the handlebars or simply let the electronics handle the job. Yamaha has also revised the clutch damping and shifting hardware, while tweaking the transmission software to better suit riding conditions on loose terrain.
Reason number two. An equally adept chassis. The RW700 uses a double-cradle steel frame, paired with a 41-mm inverted fork offering 6.2 in (157 mm) of travel. At the rear, a link-type setup with a single shock absorber provides another 6.1 in (156 mm) of travel. Both ends are designed to offer enough compliance for terrain that might be a little less forgiving than your usual city streets.
Wheels come in the form of a 17-inch front and 15-inch rear wire-spoke combination, wrapped in tubeless Pirelli Scorpion Rally STR tires. Yup, the same kind of rubber you’d typically find on hardcore adventure and dual-sport bikes. Braking duties are handled by twin 298-mm discs at the front, operated by hydraulic calipers, and a single 267-mm disc at the rear.
My only gripe is how heavy the thing really is for a scooter. Yamaha claims a wet weight of 487.2 lb (221 kg) for the standard RW700. It also gets a 31.9-in (810-mm) seat height, 6.7 in (170 mm) of ground clearance, and a 3.7-gallon (14-liter) fuel tank.
One of the more interesting touches is the choice between conventional scooter-style floorboards and proper, chunky footpegs. Other convenience features include self-cancelling turn signals and Yamaha's Smart Key system. The cockpit gets a 5-inch TFT instrument display, with Bluetooth connectivity for pairing your smartphone. It also supports Yamaha's Garmin StreetCross navigation app, which can be used free of charge.
Reason number three. Electronics. A six-axis IMU feeds data to a suite of rider aids, including cornering ABS, lean-sensitive traction control, slide control, brake control, and engine-braking management.
You get five selectable modes: Street, Sport, and Eco for tarmac, alongside Explore and Explore+ for venturing onto looser surfaces. The lattermost two also allow the rear ABS to be switched off, giving you more freedom to manage the back end on gravel.
Yamaha has even tailored the automated transmission's software for off-road use, allowing it to account for wheelspin and sliding when deciding how and when to shift gears. Step up to the RW700 Explore, and the equipment list gets even more generous. It adds adjustable front suspension, heated grips, a heated seat, illuminated handlebar switchgear, and a center stand as standard.
If the RW700 sounds like a little too much for your liking, Yamaha has a smaller alternative in the form of the RW350. It uses a less powerful 330cc single-cylinder engine, but doesn't appear to sacrifice much in the way of everyday equipment.
You still get nifty features like under-seat storage with enough room for full-face helmets, cruise control, a 5-inch TFT display, Yamaha's Smart Key system, and self-canceling turn signals. The choice between conventional floorboards and proper footpegs is available here, too, so you can decide how you want to position yourself depending on the kind of riding you’re doing.
The biggest mechanical distinction, naturally, is the transmission. Unlike the RW700, which uses Yamaha's six-speed Y-AMT setup, the RW350 sticks with a conventional V-belt automatic transmission. That should make it a more familiar proposition for anyone coming from a traditional scooter, without the added complexity of manually selecting gears.
And that's perhaps where the RW350 makes the most sense. Not everyone needs a 689cc twin-cylinder engine or a nearly 500-pound adventure scooter to get away from the city. A smaller single-cylinder package could offer a more manageable, less intimidating entry point into the whole adventure-scooter idea.
It might not have the same mechanical theatrics as its bigger sibling, but if Yamaha can get the balance between comfort, practicality, and genuine rough-road ability right, the RW350 could arguably be the more appealing everyday adventure companion.
Yamaha hasn't revealed pricing for either scooter just yet. For now, both models have only been announced for the European market, with no word on whether they'll eventually make their way to other regions.
What really excites me is how fine the lines of adventure riding have become now. The likes of Honda's X-ADV were some of the early offerings in the adventure-scooter category. But when a company like Yamaha that has years of adventure motorcycling experience steps into the segment, you know how big the potential market really could be.
That's largely because perhaps the next big adventure machine doesn't need a tall seat, a massive front wheel, or even a clutch lever. It might just need a CVT, a comfortable seat, and a willingness to take the long way home. Yamaha’s RW adventure scoots have all of that.
Source: Yamaha

No two inlays match, and the color shifts every time the knife moves.
Kizer built the handle around flowing lines and smooth transitions, so nothing on the frame catches a pocket edge or a palm. It looks dressy and wears plain, and the sandblasted, stonewashed finish helps there too, because its texture swallows the scratches and scuffs that daily carry prints on polished titanium.
Jonathan Styles designed the Topsail, and the grip stays calmer than the inlay suggests. It fills the palm, and Kizer says the contoured handle cuts fatigue in long sessions.
The blade is a 3.10-inch drop point in BÃ¶hler M390, a powder stainless that holds an edge through the kind of daily cutting that dulls lesser steels by the weekend. Itâll outlast the cardboard. Kizer runs it at 59 to 60 HRC with a flat grind and a blast finish, and the spine measures 0.12 inches thick. The edge runs clean from choil to point, with no recurve to fight a sharpening stone.
The drop point keeps its belly near the tip, so draw cuts and food-prep style slicing both stay on the menu, and geometry does the quiet work. A flat grind on 0.12-inch stock splits the difference between slicer and workhorse, thin enough to glide and thick enough to twist a little.
Wear resistance is the trait that built M390âs reputation in production folders, and itâs why the steel shows up on knives costing twice this sale price. Kizer says the steel keeps its edge under hard use, and the 59 to 60 HRC hardness is the number behind that claim. Hardness at that level also means the edge resists rolling when it meets staples, grit and pocket hazards that fold softer edges.
You get a front flipper and a thumb hole, so the blade opens the way your hand already wants to work. Pick your opener and go. The frame lock is cut from the titanium handle itself, which keeps the parts count low, the lockup free of flex, and the whole mechanism easy to rinse clean.
The front flipper tab sits at the bladeâs tang, ahead of the pivot, so a fingertip roll starts the blade moving before the hole ever comes into play. Itâs the opener your hand finds without looking, and the thumb hole takes over for slow, deliberate openings, for one-handed closes against a thigh, and for anyone wearing gloves.
The thumb hole is the triangular cutout in the blade, the one visual trick the design repeats in every finish. It also drops a little weight from the blade ahead of the pivot, and it gives a gloved thumb purchase.
Because the lock bar is part of the handle slab, thereâs no separate liner to loosen, and the titanium clip rides the same side of the knife. The lock face and the inlay sit on opposite sides of the same slab, which is why the show side stays clean.
Closed, the Topsail runs 4.07 inches of handle and disappears against a pocket seam at 3.43 ounces, with a titanium clip holding it tip-up on the right side of the pocket. You pay for the crystal. Hereâs the trade: the clip doesnât reverse, so left-side carry is out, and the inlay that makes this knife costs more than plain titanium. The M390 blade and the frame lock are why the money isnât only cosmetic.
At 7.15 inches open, the Topsail is a full-size EDC rather than a watch-pocket knife.

ire burns at Gwagwalada Main Market in Abuja as an eyewitness appeals for urgent intervention from emergency responders.

A fire outbreak has ravaged the Gwagwalada Main Market in the Federal Capital Territory, Abuja.
An eyewitness who recorded the incident in a video shared on Facebook claimed that the fire started at about 7pm on Saturday and was still burning when he posted the footage.
The video was shared by Sani Usman Zakariyya, who called on the relevant authorities and emergency responders to intervene urgently.
In the accompanying caption, he wrote, “FIRE OUTBREAK IN GWAGWALADA MAIN MARKET. The attention of fire service is urgently needed, and the security are afraid to open the market for security reasons. 
“The authorities should act fast before it spreads further. It’s not getting better at all. I’m currently at the scene in the market.”Make PUNCH part of your everyday experience.Add us to your Preferred Sources

97,2026-11-15,"The crypto market downturn sent Bitcoin below $64K and left Ether, XRP and Solana down 22% in a week, with the December Fed decision in focus.
Bitcoin dropped to $63,863 on November 15, 2026—a 4.97% fall in 24 hours, according to CoinGecko. The damage spread fast. Ether, XRP▼$0.9616 and solana each lost 22% over the week, while Bitcoin fell 17%, as bond yields climbed, investors locked in losses and margin calls from the AI selloff cascaded through portfolios. The decline pushed BTC▲$64,884.00 to a three-month low just above $62,000, raising the question of where relief might come. Analysts and strategists point to the December Fed decision as a potential turning point in this crypto market downturn.
Bitcoin and several of the largest alternative cryptocurrencies suffered substantial weekly declines. Ether, XRP and solana each lost 22%, putting their weekly falls five percentage points beyond Bitcoin’s 17% decline.
The token touched a fresh three-month low just above $62,000 as the selling continued. That brought BTC back into the broad price territory where analyst James Check said some investors had hesitated to buy during the summer.
BTC fell below $73,000 on November 10. The break below $72,000 on November 12 marked another deterioration, followed by the later three-month low.
Bitcoin and ether subsequently rebounded after another sharp drop. By November 15, however, BTC remained below $64,000 and its 24-hour change was still negative. The bounce occurred within a downturn that had already inflicted double-digit weekly losses across several major tokens.
Speaking with Anthony Pompliano on the Pomp Podcast, Wall Street veteran Jordi Visser argued that crypto investors had underestimated the bond market’s influence on Bitcoin. He cited a weekly increase of 35 basis points in two-year yields and connected that move to BTC’s decline.
Visser’s explanation centers on inflation fears and interest-rate expectations. He argued that those fears need to ease for the Fed to stop raising rates, and that a change in the liquidity backdrop would help Bitcoin recover. His outlook still included further tightening—at least two more rate increases, he said, with December’s size mattering enough to change Bitcoin’s direction.
Check described the decline as the fourth capitulation event since Bitcoin’s all-time high. His assessment focused on investors who passed on BTC in the mid-to-high $60,000s during the summer, bought in the $80,000s in September and were now selling at a loss.
Fundstrat’s Tom Lee offered another source of pressure: margin calls associated with the AI and Nvidia selloff, particularly among Asian retail investors. His explanation points to investors selling crypto while facing pressure elsewhere in their portfolios. Visser placed a different emphasis on that connection, saying the AI selloff had affected Bitcoin the previous week but considered the liquidity side of the market more relevant to its immediate direction.
Check’s account traces a shift from hesitation to chasing higher prices and then realizing losses. Investors who wanted cheaper Bitcoin during the summer eventually entered after BTC moved into the $80,000s, only to sell when it returned to prices they had previously considered attractive.
For that group, the November decline erased the benefit of waiting for stronger momentum before buying. Check’s explanation centers on the decisions of those late entrants and their willingness to exit after the market reversed.
Check characterized Bitcoin’s position as a deep-value zone, comparing it with earlier periods in February and late June of 2026, as well as late 2022. That valuation judgment does not mean the bottom is in. His view that investors gradually entering at such levels could eventually become profitable remains a forward-looking assessment, with further losses still possible before any recovery.
The weekly declines covered Bitcoin, ether, XRP and solana, spreading the damage across several of the market’s largest assets. Bitcoin’s 17% loss was severe, while the three other tokens each surrendered more than a fifth of their value over the week. The five-percentage-point gap shows how much harder those assets fell over the same interval.
The September purchases described by Check occurred in the $80,000s. Selling in the $60,000s locks in a loss for those positions, adding an investor-behavior dimension to the discussion of yields and monetary policy. His assessment also explains why a return to an earlier price range can feel very different to different holders. The immediate recovery challenge therefore has both a macroeconomic and a holder component. Visser looks for relief from interest-rate fears, while Check identifies investors already abandoning positions after the reversal.
Singapore-based, Nasdaq-listed miner BitFuFu held 1,454 BTC at October’s end, up from 1,388 BTC at September’s end. That 66-BTC increase represented 4.8% month-over-month growth. The company had increased its Bitcoin holdings for three consecutive months. Chairman and CEO Leo Lu reiterated its commitment to increasing those holdings, giving the operating update a longer-term treasury focus. These were October balances, preceding the later increase in November selling pressure.
BitFuFu mined 137 BTC in October, down from 167 BTC in September—a decline of about 18%. Its production consisted of 67 BTC from self-mining and 70 BTC from cloud mining. The production reduction was attributed mainly to an approximately 11.8% increase in mining difficulty during October. Managed hashrate edged up to 19.5 exahashes per second from 19.4 EH/s in September, leaving the company’s mining power relatively stable despite lower output. For the miner, the challenge was maintaining accumulation as greater mining difficulty weighed on production.
Visser’s bearish concern is that inflation fears keep interest-rate expectations elevated. He cited the rise in two-year yields as a force behind the decline and said the liquidity backdrop needed to improve for Bitcoin to turn around. His interpretation differs from Arthur Hayes’s view that higher interest rates could push wealthy investors toward Bitcoin and other assets.
Check’s description of investors realizing losses highlights a continuing source of potential selling. Buyers who entered after the September advance may remain under pressure as Bitcoin trades back in the $60,000s. Lee’s margin-call suggestion raises a second possible vulnerability: stress in AI-related investments spilling into crypto holdings. A durable recovery would have to contend with those pressures after the initial rebound. The strongest bullish argument in Visser’s account is relief from rate fears; the clearest threat is that inflation concerns keep the tightening outlook in place.
Visser said the next rally could find its catalyst in December if the Fed avoids a 50-basis-point increase. His scenario allows for another rate hike, with a smaller move providing enough relief to support Bitcoin. The policy backdrop included Fed Governor Christopher Waller’s statement that a 50-basis-point increase should be considered if October inflation came in hot. According to Visser’s interview, that inflation condition was met.
Visser pointed to Brent crude returning to its March price and inflation swaps reaching their lows for the year. Those observations formed part of his argument that inflation fears could ease enough to improve the policy outlook. His scenario links a potential Bitcoin recovery to that easing in fears and a smaller December increase. For holders searching why crypto is crashing, the central explanations are rising-rate concerns, investors realizing losses and possible selling linked to pressure in AI stocks. What happens next depends in part on whether those forces ease as the December decision approaches.
The break occurred on November 12, 2026. It followed the November 10 move below $73,000 and preceded the later three-month low just above $62,000.
CoinGecko recorded that price at 08:20:10 UTC on November 15, 2026. Bitcoin’s accompanying 24-hour change was a decline of 4.97%.
Visser points to the December rate decision. He expects further increases but believes a move smaller than 50 basis points could give Bitcoin room to rally.
It means a half-percentage-point increase in the Fed’s policy rate. Visser’s potential recovery scenario involves a smaller December increase while his broader outlook still includes additional tightening.
Check names February and late June of 2026, along with late 2022. He uses those periods as reference points for his assessment of Bitcoin after the decline.
The balance is dated October 31, 2026. It was 66 BTC higher than the company’s 1,388 BTC balance at September’s end.
The company produced 67 BTC through self-mining and 70 BTC through cloud mining. Together, those operations generated 137 BTC in October, compared with 167 BTC in September.
